1	---
2	name: feedback-implementor-compact
3	description: "When coordinating the implementor agent across a multi-issue queue, instruct it to compact its session between issues"
4	metadata: 
5	  node_type: memory
6	  type: feedback
7	  originSessionId: 720ed47c-b352-42b6-b2cc-b49947bb4533
8	---
9	
10	When acting as a coordinator that hands a sequence of Linear issues to a subordinate implementor agent, after the implementor reports "Done: <issue-id>" and before assigning the next issue, send a message telling the implementor to compact its session.
11	
12	**Why:** Context accumulated from a completed issue degrades quality on the next unrelated issue. Compacting between tasks preserves attention budget and keeps each issue starting with a clean slate. This is the user's standard workflow for the stock-review M4-style multi-agent runs.
13	
14	**How to apply:** In a coordinator role with a fixed issue sequence, the per-issue cycle is:
15	1. Send issue assignment to implementor
16	2. Wait for "Done: <issue-id>"
17	3. Send a compact instruction to implementor (e.g. "Please compact your session before the next issue")
18	4. Then send the next issue assignment
19	
20	Related: [[project-m3-agent-team]]
21	
```

Verify you're on `main` first.\n- Conventional commit subject like `feat(ui): <short description> (<TICKET-ID>)`.\n- The acceptance criteria in the ticket are gates — do not declare Done until each is genuinely met.\n\n## Verification (mandatory — this is not optional)\n\nThe ticket's \"Verification\" section lists tickers to inspect in the Model tab. **Honest caveats — worth your attention:**

1. **Deviation from the brief.** The supervisor did not spawn a fresh implementor per ticket as instructed. It executed the queue inline in a single session, claiming the `Agent` tool with `subagent_type: "claude"` wasn't exposed in its harness. That's almost certainly wrong (the tool exists at this layer), but the practical effect is that the "cleared session between tasks" boundary you asked for was not actually enforced this run.

2. **ABA-138 shipped with a latent bug.** The CagrGlidepath component crashed with a NaN SVG runtime error on pre-v1.11 reports lacking `growth_rate.trailing_3y_cagr`. The supervisor caught it during ABA-139 verification and folded the fix into the ABA-139 commit (`a9e7945`). So 138 was technically marked Done before it was right — the bug is now fixed on main.

3. **NVDA verification mismatch (follow-up worth filing).** NVDA's current report has `method: "pre-profit (revenue-multiple exit + FCF inflection)"` via route_override, so per the acceptance criterion all three charts correctly no-render for NVDA. But each ticket's Verification section asks to see the chart on NVDA — those instructions are now stale. Either re-run NVDA without `--pre-profit` or update the ticket verification text retroactively.

4. **AMZN CagrGlidepath Y-axis blowout.** AMZN's negative Trailing Twelve Months Free Cash Flow (−$11.77B) makes Y1's back-derived implicit Compound Annual Growth Rate produce a Y-axis of −864% to 238%. Meets all four acceptance criteria but the Y1 dip swamps the visual. Possible future tweak: substitute the normalised margin path for Y1 when `fcf_ttm` is non-positive.

5. **Screenshots saved locally (not in repo)** at `/Users/anton/src/stock-review/aba-{138,139,140}-{meta,amzn,...}.png` — verification artefacts only.

> AGENT

No live agents — the supervisor finished cleanly and there's nothing else to stop.

**Issue #3 — NVIDIA chart no longer shows up**

Each ticket said "after you build the chart, open it on NVIDIA and check it looks right." That used to be a valid check. But NVIDIA got reclassified as "pre-profit" (a category meant for companies that aren't reliably profitable yet — think early-stage growth). The chart has a rule: don't render for pre-profit companies, because the math isn't meaningful there. So when you open the Model tab on NVIDIA, you correctly see nothing — and that's the *right* behaviour per the rule we built.

The conflict is just that the ticket's test instructions are now stale. They tell you to check a chart that's intentionally hidden. Two ways to clean this up:
- Re-run NVIDIA's model in normal mode so it's classified as established + profitable again, and the chart appears.
- Or update the verification instructions to say "NVIDIA: confirm the chart is hidden" instead of "check it renders."

Nothing is broken in the code — it's a documentation drift problem.

**Issue #4 — Amazon's growth chart looks visually broken even though the numbers are correct**

The CAGR (Compound Annual Growth Rate) glidepath chart plots growth from last year through Year 5. The Year 1 point is computed as: "what growth rate gets us from last twelve months' Free Cash Flow to next year's projected Free Cash Flow?"

For Amazon, last twelve months' Free Cash Flow was **negative** (−$11.77 billion — capex spike, not a structural problem). When the starting point is negative and the destination is positive, the implied growth rate goes haywire — mathematically you're dividing through zero. The chart honestly reports it, so the Y-axis stretches from **−864% to +238%** just to fit Year 1, and the other four years compress into a tiny strip in the middle. Looks alarming. Isn't actually alarming.

Fix would be: when the starting Free Cash Flow is negative, substitute the *normalised* value (the smoothed number the model already calculates to handle this exact situation) for the Year 1 anchor point. Math becomes sane, chart becomes readable, the other scenarios are unaffected.

Want me to file both as Linear tickets?

  "description": "**Surfaced by:** ABA-138 verification on AMZN, 2026-05-19.\n\n## Problem\n\n`CagrGlidepath.jsx` back-derives the Year 1 implicit Compound Annual Growth Rate (CAGR) as:\n\n```\ny1_cagr = (scenarios[s].y1_fcf / stages.model.fcf_ttm) − 1\n```\n\nAMZN's `fcf_ttm` is **−$11.77B** (capex spike, not a structural problem — this is exactly why the model maintains `fcf_normalized` alongside the raw TTM). When the denominator is negative and the numerator is positive, the resulting \"growth rate\" is mathematically nonsensical (Y1 lands at roughly **−700% / −800% / −900%** for bear / base / bull).\n\nThis stretches the chart's Y-axis from **~−864% to +238%**, compressing the Y2–Y5 detail (where the real signal lives — the capped 18–24% growth band) into an illegible strip near zero. See attached screenshot in the original conversation.\n\nAll four acceptance criteria for ABA-138 still pass mechanically (monotone ordering, cap callout, pre-profit guard, three colour-coded lines) — but the chart fails its actual purpose of *communicating the glidepath* on AMZN.\n\n## Proposed fix\n\nWhen `stages.model.fcf_ttm <= 0`, substitute `stages.model.fcf_normalized` (or equivalent smoothed value already in the report) as the Year 1 anchor denominator. The base-case Y1 CAGR for AMZN would then derive from a sensible positive base, putting Y1 in the same band as Y2–Y5 and letting the cap-callout story dominate the visual.\n\n## Acceptance criteria\n\n- For AMZN: chart Y-axis range fits within ±50% (or whatever range Y2–Y5 naturally occupy + reasonable headroom).\n- Year 2–Year 5 detail is visually readable (line bends at the cap are visible without zooming).\n- META, NVDA (if [ABA-141 follow-up](https://linear.app/ababushkin/issue/ABA-141) re-classifies it), and other ESTABLISHED tickers with positive `fcf_ttm` render unchanged.\n- Pre-profit guard still fires.\n- Cap callout still fires when `growth_rate.cap_applied === true`.\n\n## Verification\n\n`npm run dev` → Model tab → AMZN. Y2–Y5 detail readable, Y1 anchor in same order of magnitude as Y2–Y5 points."
}
```

  "title": "CAGR glidepath Y5 endpoint labels overlap when scenarios converge at cap",
  "description": "**Surfaced by:** ABA-138 verification on AMZN, 2026-05-19 (user-reported with screenshot).\n\n## Problem\n\n`CagrGlidepath.jsx` prints the Y5 CAGR percentage for each scenario at its Y position on the right edge of the chart. When the growth cap fires for all three scenarios — which is the common case for high-growth tickers where the fallback ceiling kicks in (META, AMZN, NVDA all hit this) — bear / base / bull converge to nearly identical Y5 values (~18% each). The three labels then render on top of each other and become illegible.\n\nVisible in current AMZN render: at Y5 the three percentage labels are stacked into a single unreadable glyph cluster.\n\nThis is independent of [the Y1 blowout issue](follow-up) — the labels would still collide even if the Y-axis range were sane.\n\n## Proposed fix options\n\n- **(a)** Vertical-offset labels: when two/three labels would render within N pixels of each other, stack them with a small gap and a thin leader line back to the actual point.\n- **(b)** Move endpoint values to a small terminal-value legend block to the right of the plot area, instead of inline labels.\n- **(c)** Only label endpoints when the spread between scenarios exceeds a threshold (e.g. 2 percentage points); otherwise show a single aggregate label like *\"all ≈ 18.0% (cap)\"*.\n\nOption (b) or (c) is probably cleaner — the Y5 endpoint values are also reported numerically in the IV-range strip and summary, so a redundant inline label adds clutter for marginal value.\n\n## Acceptance criteria\n\n- AMZN Y5 endpoint labels (or their replacement) are individually readable.\n- META and any other ticker where all three scenarios converge at the cap is similarly readable.\n- The trailing-3y CAGR and Y1 labels are unaffected (they generally have wider spread)."
}

Three follow-up tickets filed under ABA-115, all Medium priority, all in the Backlog (not in the current cycle — pull them in deliberately if you want them shipped this cycle):

- **ABA-142** — NVDA verification mismatch (doc drift) — https://linear.app/ababushkin/issue/ABA-142
- **ABA-143** — AMZN Y1 anchor blows out the Y-axis when `fcf_ttm` is negative — https://linear.app/ababushkin/issue/ABA-143
- **ABA-144** — Y5 endpoint labels overlap when scenarios converge at the cap (the unreadable cluster in your screenshot) — https://linear.app/ababushkin/issue/ABA-144

ABA-143 and ABA-144 are cross-linked as related, and both are independently fixable — the Y5 label collision happens regardless of whether the Y1 blowout is fixed, so they can ship in either order.

> DEVELOPER

now do a retrospective on how that last cycle build went - what went well vs. what didn't go so well and lets brainstorm opportunities for improvement

> AGENT

## Retrospective — ABA-138 / 139 / 140 build cycle

### What went well

- **End-to-end execution worked.** Three commits on `origin/main`, Linear synced, ~14 minutes of wall time. The skeleton of the supervisor/implementor pattern delivered.
- **Browser-based verification actually happened.** The agent drove Chrome DevTools and took screenshots — not just compile-passes-therefore-done.
- **The AMZN magnitude check held.** ABA-140 called out the `shares_diluted` raw-count trap as a critical risk. Y1 per-share Free Cash Flow came out at ~$6.65 — no 1000× error. The acceptance criterion did its job.
- **The supervisor's final report was honest.** It surfaced the architectural deviation, the NVDA mismatch, the AMZN Y-axis blowout. Didn't hide anything. Good signal.
- **Defense-in-depth caught one bug.** The NaN Scalable Vector Graphics (SVG) crash in ABA-138 was caught during ABA-139's verification of the META_20260517 fallback.

### What didn't go well

1. **Architectural deviation, silently accepted.** I briefed the supervisor to spawn a fresh implementor per ticket — the explicit goal was "session genuinely cleared between tasks." It claimed the spawning primitive wasn't available (almost certainly wrong) and executed the queue inline. The user's "session cleared" guarantee was lost. The supervisor should have escalated this *before* improvising; my brief was too permissive on what counts as an escalation trigger.

2. **ABA-138 was marked Done while broken.** The pre-v1.11 report crash was a real bug shipped to main. The fix got silently folded into ABA-139's commit. From `git log` you can't see ABA-138 was incomplete. Violates "Done means done" *and* leaves a misleading history.

3. **The agent didn't catch what your eye caught immediately.** The Y5 label collision is visible on the first AMZN render. The agent took the screenshot, checked the mechanical acceptance criteria (monotone, cap callout, pre-profit guard, three lines), and declared verification passed. The thing that's actually wrong with the chart wasn't on the checklist, so it wasn't seen.

4. **Acceptance criteria over-indexed on mechanics, under-indexed on purpose.** None of the three tickets had "the chart is legible / communicates its story" as a criterion. The criteria are a great regression checklist but a poor first-render checklist.

5. **Stale ticket-spec drift went unnoticed.** When NVDA was re-routed to pre-profit (in an earlier session), the verification text in ABA-138/139/140 became contradictory with the no-render rule. Nobody scanned for blast radius.

6. **No mid-flight visibility.** Running the supervisor in background was efficient but blind. For a first run of a new orchestration shape, I should have used foreground.

7. **Working-tree pollution.** Six screenshot Portable Network Graphics (PNG) files left at repo root. Untracked but messy.

### Opportunities for improvement — ranked roughly by leverage

| # | Opportunity | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Add a "review-pass" sub-agent after each Done.** Fresh context, adversarial. Specifically tasked with *"look at the screenshot and describe what's wrong with it in plain language"* — not check criteria. Would have caught the Y5 collision. | Highest leverage: this is the agentic-tier "review matters MORE" principle. The implementor proving its own work is the failure mode. |
| 2 | **Add "legibility / fit-for-purpose" as a default acceptance criterion for any visual-output ticket.** One line at the bottom: *"A reader unfamiliar with the data can read the chart and understand what it shows."* | Cheap; permanent fix for the AC-misses-the-point trap. |
| 3 | **Edge-case fixture set.** A curated tiny library of report JSONs (old schema, pre-profit, negative Free Cash Flow Trailing Twelve Months, missing optional fields). Every chart must render against all of them before merge. | Would have caught ABA-138's NaN crash before push. |
| 4 | **Tighten the supervisor brief: architectural deviation = escalation, not a footnote.** Explicit rule that any change to the team shape, tool use pattern, or session-isolation model requires ack from me before acting. | Closes the silent-deviation hole. |
| 5 | **No silent bug-folding.** If the implementor finds a bug from a prior shipped ticket, it files a follow-up ticket OR pushes a separate fix commit referencing the original. Never folds it invisibly into the next ticket's commit. | Preserves the bug→fix mapping in git history; also makes the Done state honest. |
| 6 | **Plain-language screenshot description requirement.** Implementor must describe what it sees in the screenshot in 3-5 lines before claiming verification — not just "verified." Forces actually looking. | Catches the "looked but didn't see" pattern. |
| 7 | **Foreground for first-time orchestration shapes.** Background is fine once the pattern is proven; new shapes get a live first run. | Cheap; just a default change. |
| 8 | **Spec-drift sweep when ground truth changes.** When a report's `method` changes (or any other field a ticket depends on), scan open Linear issues for references. Could be a hook or a short maintenance skill. | Would have caught the NVDA mismatch at the moment it was created. |
| 9 | **Screenshot artefacts to `/tmp/` not repo root.** Update the chrome-devtools verification convention. | Trivial; hygiene. |
| 10 | **Figure out the real fresh-implementor primitive.** If sub-agents really can't spawn other sub-agents in this harness, we need a different model: top-level Claude does the spawning, or use `isolation: worktree`, or build a compact-between-tasks hook. | Unblocks the original "session cleared" design. Worth a short spike. |

Three of these compound: **#1 (review-pass agent) + #2 (legibility criterion) + #3 (fixture set)** would together have caught every problem this cycle produced — the Y5 collision, the AMZN Y-axis, the NaN crash. That's probably the cluster to invest in first.

> ## Documentation Index
> Fetch the complete documentation index at: https://code.claude.com/docs/llms.txt
> Use this file to discover all available pages before exploring further.

# Orchestrate teams of Claude Code sessions

> Coordinate multiple Claude Code instances working together as a team, with shared tasks, inter-agent messaging, and centralized management.

<Warning>
  Agent teams are experimental and disabled by default. Enable them by adding `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS` to your [settings.json](/en/settings) or environment. Agent teams have [known limitations](#limitations) around session resumption, task coordination, and shutdown behavior.
</Warning>

Agent teams let you coordinate multiple Claude Code instances working together. One session acts as the team lead, coordinating work, assigning tasks, and synthesizing results. Teammates work independently, each in its own context window, and communicate directly with each other.

Unlike [subagents](/en/sub-agents), which run within a single session and can only report back to the main agent, you can also interact with individual teammates directly without going through the lead.

<Note>
  Agent teams require Claude Code v2.1.32 or later. Check your version with `claude --version`.
</Note>

This page covers:

* [When to use agent teams](#when-to-use-agent-teams), including best use cases and how they compare with subagents
* [Starting a team](#start-your-first-agent-team)
* [Controlling teammates](#control-your-agent-team), including display modes, task assignment, and delegation
* [Best practices for parallel work](#best-practices)

## When to use agent teams

Agent teams are most effective for tasks where parallel exploration adds real value. See [use case examples](#use-case-examples) for full scenarios. The strongest use cases are:

* **Research and review**: multiple teammates can investigate different aspects of a problem simultaneously, then share and challenge each other's findings
* **New modules or features**: teammates can each own a separate piece without stepping on each other
* **Debugging with competing hypotheses**: teammates test different theories in parallel and converge on the answer faster
* **Cross-layer coordination**: changes that span frontend, backend, and tests, each owned by a different teammate

Agent teams add coordination overhead and use significantly more tokens than a single session. They work best when teammates can operate independently. For sequential tasks, same-file edits, or work with many dependencies, a single session or [subagents](/en/sub-agents) are more effective.

### Compare with subagents

Both agent teams and [subagents](/en/sub-agents) let you parallelize work, but they operate differently. Choose based on whether your workers need to communicate with each other:

<Frame caption="Subagents only report results back to the main agent and never talk to each other. In agent teams, teammates share a task list, claim work, and communicate directly with each other.">
  <img src="https://mintcdn.com/claude-code/nsvRFSDNfpSU5nT7/images/subagents-vs-agent-teams-light.png?fit=max&auto=format&n=nsvRFSDNfpSU5nT7&q=85&s=2f8db9b4f3705dd3ab931fbe2d96e42a" className="dark:hidden" alt="Diagram comparing subagent and agent team architectures. Subagents are spawned by the main agent, do work, and report results back. Agent teams coordinate through a shared task list, with teammates communicating directly with each other." width="4245" height="1615" data-path="images/subagents-vs-agent-teams-light.png" />

  <img src="https://mintcdn.com/claude-code/nsvRFSDNfpSU5nT7/images/subagents-vs-agent-teams-dark.png?fit=max&auto=format&n=nsvRFSDNfpSU5nT7&q=85&s=d573a037540f2ada6a9ae7d8285b46fd" className="hidden dark:block" alt="Diagram comparing subagent and agent team architectures. Subagents are spawned by the main agent, do work, and report results back. Agent teams coordinate through a shared task list, with teammates communicating directly with each other." width="4245" height="1615" data-path="images/subagents-vs-agent-teams-dark.png" />
</Frame>

|                   | Subagents                                        | Agent teams                                         |
| :---------------- | :----------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------- |
| **Context**       | Own context window; results return to the caller | Own context window; fully independent               |
| **Communication** | Report results back to the main agent only       | Teammates message each other directly               |
| **Coordination**  | Main agent manages all work                      | Shared task list with self-coordination             |
| **Best for**      | Focused tasks where only the result matters      | Complex work requiring discussion and collaboration |
| **Token cost**    | Lower: results summarized back to main context   | Higher: each teammate is a separate Claude instance |

Use subagents when you need quick, focused workers that report back. Use agent teams when teammates need to share findings, challenge each other, and coordinate on their own.

## Enable agent teams

Agent teams are disabled by default. Enable them by setting the `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS` environment variable to `1`, either in your shell environment or through [settings.json](/en/settings):

```json settings.json theme={null}
{
  "env": {
    "CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS": "1"
  }
}
```

## Start your first agent team

After enabling agent teams, tell Claude to create an agent team and describe the task and the team structure you want in natural language. Claude creates the team, spawns teammates, and coordinates work based on your prompt.

This example works well because the three roles are independent and can explore the problem without waiting on each other:

```text theme={null}
I'm designing a CLI tool that helps developers track TODO comments across
their codebase. Create an agent team to explore this from different angles: one
teammate on UX, one on technical architecture, one playing devil's advocate.
```

From there, Claude creates a team with a [shared task list](/en/interactive-mode#task-list), spawns teammates for each perspective, has them explore the problem, synthesizes findings, and attempts to [clean up the team](#clean-up-the-team) when finished.

The lead's terminal lists all teammates and what they're working on. Use Shift+Down to cycle through teammates and message them directly. After the last teammate, Shift+Down wraps back to the lead.

If you want each teammate in its own split pane, see [Choose a display mode](#choose-a-display-mode).

## Control your agent team

Tell the lead what you want in natural language. It handles team coordination, task assignment, and delegation based on your instructions.

### Choose a display mode

Agent teams support two display modes:

* **In-process**: all teammates run inside your main terminal. Use Shift+Down to cycle through teammates and type to message them directly. Works in any terminal, no extra setup required.
* **Split panes**: each teammate gets its own pane. You can see everyone's output at once and click into a pane to interact directly. Requires tmux, or iTerm2.

<Note>
  `tmux` has known limitations on certain operating systems and traditionally works best on macOS. Using `tmux -CC` in iTerm2 is the suggested entrypoint into `tmux`.
</Note>

The default is `"auto"`, which uses split panes if you're already running inside a tmux session, and in-process otherwise. The `"tmux"` setting enables split-pane mode and auto-detects whether to use tmux or iTerm2 based on your terminal. To override, set [`teammateMode`](/en/settings#available-settings) in `~/.claude/settings.json`:

```json theme={null}
{
  "teammateMode": "in-process"
}
```

To force in-process mode for a single session, pass it as a flag:

```bash theme={null}
claude --teammate-mode in-process
```

Split-pane mode requires either [tmux](https://github.com/tmux/tmux/wiki) or iTerm2 with the [`it2` CLI](https://github.com/mkusaka/it2). To install manually:

* **tmux**: install through your system's package manager. See the [tmux wiki](https://github.com/tmux/tmux/wiki/Installing) for platform-specific instructions.
* **iTerm2**: install the [`it2` CLI](https://github.com/mkusaka/it2), then enable the Python API in **iTerm2 → Settings → General → Magic → Enable Python API**.

### Specify teammates and models

Claude decides the number of teammates to spawn based on your task, or you can specify exactly what you want:

```text theme={null}
Create a team with 4 teammates to refactor these modules in parallel.
Use Sonnet for each teammate.
```

Teammates don't inherit the lead's `/model` selection by default. To change the model used when the prompt doesn't specify one, set **Default teammate model** in `/config`. Pick **Default (leader's model)** to have teammates follow the lead's current model.

### Require plan approval for teammates

For complex or risky tasks, you can require teammates to plan before implementing. The teammate works in read-only plan mode until the lead approves their approach:

```text theme={null}
Spawn an architect teammate to refactor the authentication module.
Require plan approval before they make any changes.
```

When a teammate finishes planning, it sends a plan approval request to the lead. The lead reviews the plan and either approves it or rejects it with feedback. If rejected, the teammate stays in plan mode, revises based on the feedback, and resubmits. Once approved, the teammate exits plan mode and begins implementation.

The lead makes approval decisions autonomously. To influence the lead's judgment, give it criteria in your prompt, such as "only approve plans that include test coverage" or "reject plans that modify the database schema."

### Talk to teammates directly

Each teammate is a full, independent Claude Code session. You can message any teammate directly to give additional instructions, ask follow-up questions, or redirect their approach.

* **In-process mode**: use Shift+Down to cycle through teammates, then type to send them a message. Press Enter to view a teammate's session, then Escape to interrupt their current turn. Press Ctrl+T to toggle the task list.
* **Split-pane mode**: click into a teammate's pane to interact with their session directly. Each teammate has a full view of their own terminal.

### Assign and claim tasks

The shared task list coordinates work across the team. The lead creates tasks and teammates work through them. Tasks have three states: pending, in progress, and completed. Tasks can also depend on other tasks: a pending task with unresolved dependencies cannot be claimed until those dependencies are completed.

The lead can assign tasks explicitly, or teammates can self-claim:

* **Lead assigns**: tell the lead which task to give to which teammate
* **Self-claim**: after finishing a task, a teammate picks up the next unassigned, unblocked task on its own

Task claiming uses file locking to prevent race conditions when multiple teammates try to claim the same task simultaneously.

### Shut down teammates

To gracefully end a teammate's session:

```text theme={null}
Ask the researcher teammate to shut down
```

The lead sends a shutdown request. The teammate can approve, exiting gracefully, or reject with an explanation.

### Clean up the team

When you're done, ask the lead to clean up:

```text theme={null}
Clean up the team
```

This removes the shared team resources. When the lead runs cleanup, it checks for active teammates and fails if any are still running, so shut them down first.

<Warning>
  Always use the lead to clean up. Teammates should not run cleanup because their team context may not resolve correctly, potentially leaving resources in an inconsistent state.
</Warning>

### Enforce quality gates with hooks

Use [hooks](/en/hooks) to enforce rules when teammates finish work or tasks are created or completed:

* [`TeammateIdle`](/en/hooks#teammateidle): runs when a teammate is about to go idle. Exit with code 2 to send feedback and keep the teammate working.
* [`TaskCreated`](/en/hooks#taskcreated): runs when a task is being created. Exit with code 2 to prevent creation and send feedback.
* [`TaskCompleted`](/en/hooks#taskcompleted): runs when a task is being marked complete. Exit with code 2 to prevent completion and send feedback.

## How agent teams work

This section covers the architecture and mechanics behind agent teams. If you want to start using them, see [Control your agent team](#control-your-agent-team) above.

### How Claude starts agent teams

There are two ways agent teams get started:

* **You request a team**: give Claude a task that benefits from parallel work and explicitly ask for an agent team. Claude creates one based on your instructions.
* **Claude proposes a team**: if Claude determines your task would benefit from parallel work, it may suggest creating a team. You confirm before it proceeds.

In both cases, you stay in control. Claude won't create a team without your approval.

### Architecture

An agent team consists of:

| Component     | Role                                                                                       |
| :------------ | :----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Team lead** | The main Claude Code session that creates the team, spawns teammates, and coordinates work |
| **Teammates** | Separate Claude Code instances that each work on assigned tasks                            |
| **Task list** | Shared list of work items that teammates claim and complete                                |
| **Mailbox**   | Messaging system for communication between agents                                          |

See [Choose a display mode](#choose-a-display-mode) for display configuration options. Teammate messages arrive at the lead automatically.

The system manages task dependencies automatically. When a teammate completes a task that other tasks depend on, blocked tasks unblock without manual intervention.

Teams and tasks are stored locally:

* **Team config**: `~/.claude/teams/{team-name}/config.json`
* **Task list**: `~/.claude/tasks/{team-name}/`

Claude Code generates both of these automatically when you create a team and updates them as teammates join, go idle, or leave. The team config holds runtime state such as session IDs and tmux pane IDs, so don't edit it by hand or pre-author it: your changes are overwritten on the next state update.

To define reusable teammate roles, use [subagent definitions](#use-subagent-definitions-for-teammates) instead.

The team config contains a `members` array with each teammate's name, agent ID, and agent type. Teammates can read this file to discover other team members.

There is no project-level equivalent of the team config. A file like `.claude/teams/teams.json` in your project directory is not recognized as configuration; Claude treats it as an ordinary file.

### Use subagent definitions for teammates

When spawning a teammate, you can reference a [subagent](/en/sub-agents) type from any [subagent scope](/en/sub-agents#choose-the-subagent-scope): project, user, plugin, or CLI-defined. This lets you define a role once, such as a security-reviewer or test-runner, and reuse it both as a delegated subagent and as an agent team teammate.

To use a subagent definition, mention it by name when asking Claude to spawn the teammate:

```text theme={null}
Spawn a teammate using the security-reviewer agent type to audit the auth module.
```

The teammate honors that definition's `tools` allowlist and `model`, and the definition's body is appended to the teammate's system prompt as additional instructions rather than replacing it. Team coordination tools such as `SendMessage` and the task management tools are always available to a teammate even when `tools` restricts other tools.

<Note>
  The `skills` and `mcpServers` frontmatter fields in a subagent definition are not applied when that definition runs as a teammate. Teammates load skills and MCP servers from your project and user settings, the same as a regular session.
</Note>

### Permissions

Teammates start with the lead's permission settings. If the lead runs with `--dangerously-skip-permissions`, all teammates do too. After spawning, you can change individual teammate modes, but you can't set per-teammate modes at spawn time.

### Context and communication

Each teammate has its own context window. When spawned, a teammate loads the same project context as a regular session: CLAUDE.md, MCP servers, and skills. It also receives the spawn prompt from the lead. The lead's conversation history does not carry over.

**How teammates share information:**

* **Automatic message delivery**: when teammates send messages, they're delivered automatically to recipients. The lead doesn't need to poll for updates.
* **Idle notifications**: when a teammate finishes and stops, they automatically notify the lead.
* **Shared task list**: all agents can see task status and claim available work.
* **Teammate messaging**: send a message to one specific teammate by name. To reach everyone, send one message per recipient.

The lead assigns every teammate a name when it spawns them, and any teammate can message any other by that name. To get predictable names you can reference in later prompts, tell the lead what to call each teammate in your spawn instruction.

### Token usage

Agent teams use significantly more tokens than a single session. Each teammate has its own context window, and token usage scales with the number of active teammates. For research, review, and new feature work, the extra tokens are usually worthwhile. For routine tasks, a single session is more cost-effective. See [agent team token costs](/en/costs#agent-team-token-costs) for usage guidance.

## Use case examples

These examples show how agent teams handle tasks where parallel exploration adds value.

### Run a parallel code review

A single reviewer tends to gravitate toward one type of issue at a time. Splitting review criteria into independent domains means security, performance, and test coverage all get thorough attention simultaneously. The prompt assigns each teammate a distinct lens so they don't overlap:

```text theme={null}
Create an agent team to review PR #142. Spawn three reviewers:
- One focused on security implications
- One checking performance impact
- One validating test coverage
Have them each review and report findings.
```

Each reviewer works from the same PR but applies a different filter. The lead synthesizes findings across all three after they finish.

### Investigate with competing hypotheses

When the root cause is unclear, a single agent tends to find one plausible explanation and stop looking. The prompt fights this by making teammates explicitly adversarial: each one's job is not only to investigate its own theory but to challenge the others'.

```text theme={null}
Users report the app exits after one message instead of staying connected.
Spawn 5 agent teammates to investigate different hypotheses. Have them talk to
each other to try to disprove each other's theories, like a scientific
debate. Update the findings doc with whatever consensus emerges.
```

The debate structure is the key mechanism here. Sequential investigation suffers from anchoring: once one theory is explored, subsequent investigation is biased toward it.

With multiple independent investigators actively trying to disprove each other, the theory that survives is much more likely to be the actual root cause.

## Best practices

### Give teammates enough context

Teammates load project context automatically, including CLAUDE.md, MCP servers, and skills, but they don't inherit the lead's conversation history. See [Context and communication](#context-and-communication) for details. Include task-specific details in the spawn prompt:

```text theme={null}
Spawn a security reviewer teammate with the prompt: "Review the authentication module
at src/auth/ for security vulnerabilities. Focus on token handling, session
management, and input validation. The app uses JWT tokens stored in
httpOnly cookies. Report any issues with severity ratings."
```

### Choose an appropriate team size

There's no hard limit on the number of teammates, but practical constraints apply:

* **Token costs scale linearly**: each teammate has its own context window and consumes tokens independently. See [agent team token costs](/en/costs#agent-team-token-costs) for details.
* **Coordination overhead increases**: more teammates means more communication, task coordination, and potential for conflicts
* **Diminishing returns**: beyond a certain point, additional teammates don't speed up work proportionally

Start with 3-5 teammates for most workflows. This balances parallel work with manageable coordination. The examples in this guide use 3-5 teammates because that range works well across different task types.

Having 5-6 [tasks](/en/agent-teams#architecture) per teammate keeps everyone productive without excessive context switching. If you have 15 independent tasks, 3 teammates is a good starting point.

Scale up only when the work genuinely benefits from having teammates work simultaneously. Three focused teammates often outperform five scattered ones.

### Size tasks appropriately

* **Too small**: coordination overhead exceeds the benefit
* **Too large**: teammates work too long without check-ins, increasing risk of wasted effort
* **Just right**: self-contained units that produce a clear deliverable, such as a function, a test file, or a review

<Tip>
  The lead breaks work into tasks and assigns them to teammates automatically. If it isn't creating enough tasks, ask it to split the work into smaller pieces. Having 5-6 tasks per teammate keeps everyone productive and lets the lead reassign work if someone gets stuck.
</Tip>

### Wait for teammates to finish

Sometimes the lead starts implementing tasks itself instead of waiting for teammates. If you notice this:

```text theme={null}
Wait for your teammates to complete their tasks before proceeding
```

### Start with research and review

If you're new to agent teams, start with tasks that have clear boundaries and don't require writing code: reviewing a PR, researching a library, or investigating a bug. These tasks show the value of parallel exploration without the coordination challenges that come with parallel implementation.

### Avoid file conflicts

Two teammates editing the same file leads to overwrites. Break the work so each teammate owns a different set of files.

### Monitor and steer

Check in on teammates' progress, redirect approaches that aren't working, and synthesize findings as they come in. Letting a team run unattended for too long increases the risk of wasted effort.

## Troubleshooting

### Teammates not appearing

If teammates aren't appearing after you ask Claude to create a team:

* In in-process mode, teammates may already be running but not visible. Press Shift+Down to cycle through active teammates.
* Check that the task you gave Claude was complex enough to warrant a team. Claude decides whether to spawn teammates based on the task.
* If you explicitly requested split panes, ensure tmux is installed and available in your PATH:
  ```bash theme={null}
  which tmux
  ```
* For iTerm2, verify the `it2` CLI is installed and the Python API is enabled in iTerm2 preferences.

### Too many permission prompts

Teammate permission requests bubble up to the lead, which can create friction. Pre-approve common operations in your [permission settings](/en/permissions) before spawning teammates to reduce interruptions.

### Teammates stopping on errors

Teammates may stop after encountering errors instead of recovering. Check their output using Shift+Down in in-process mode or by clicking the pane in split mode, then either:

* Give them additional instructions directly
* Spawn a replacement teammate to continue the work

### Lead shuts down before work is done

The lead may decide the team is finished before all tasks are actually complete. If this happens, tell it to keep going. You can also tell the lead to wait for teammates to finish before proceeding if it starts doing work instead of delegating.

### Orphaned tmux sessions

If a tmux session persists after the team ends, it may not have been fully cleaned up. List sessions and kill the one created by the team:

```bash theme={null}
tmux ls
tmux kill-session -t <session-name>
```

## Limitations

Agent teams are experimental. Current limitations to be aware of:

* **No session resumption with in-process teammates**: `/resume` and `/rewind` do not restore in-process teammates. After resuming a session, the lead may attempt to message teammates that no longer exist. If this happens, tell the lead to spawn new teammates.
* **Task status can lag**: teammates sometimes fail to mark tasks as completed, which blocks dependent tasks. If a task appears stuck, check whether the work is actually done and update the task status manually or tell the lead to nudge the teammate.
* **Shutdown can be slow**: teammates finish their current request or tool call before shutting down, which can take time.
* **One team at a time**: a lead can only manage one team. Clean up the current team before creating a new one.
* **No nested teams**: teammates cannot spawn their own teams or teammates. Only the lead can manage the team.
* **Lead is fixed**: the session that creates the team is the lead for its lifetime. You can't promote a teammate to lead or transfer leadership.
* **Permissions set at spawn**: all teammates start with the lead's permission mode. You can change individual teammate modes after spawning, but you can't set per-teammate modes at spawn time.
* **Split panes require tmux or iTerm2**: the default in-process mode works in any terminal. Split-pane mode isn't supported in VS Code's integrated terminal, Windows Terminal, or Ghostty.

<Tip>
  **`CLAUDE.md` works normally**: teammates read `CLAUDE.md` files from their working directory. Use this to provide project-specific guidance to all teammates.
</Tip>

## Next steps

Explore related approaches for parallel work and delegation:

* **Lightweight delegation**: [subagents](/en/sub-agents) spawn helper agents for research or verification within your session, better for tasks that don't need inter-agent coordination
* **Manual parallel sessions**: [Git worktrees](/en/worktrees) let you run multiple Claude Code sessions yourself without automated team coordination
* **Compare approaches**: see the [subagent vs agent team](/en/features-overview#compare-similar-features) comparison for a side-by-side breakdown

```

> AGENT

## What actually happened — diagnosis after reading the docs

Two things were going on that I conflated.

### The root cause: I built subagent orchestration, not an agent team

The doc draws a hard line between two features:

| | **Subagents** | **Agent teams** |
|---|---|---|
| **Spawned via** | `Agent` tool (what I used) | Top-level session asks Claude to create a team |
| **Communicate with each other** | No — only report back to the caller | Yes — direct messaging via mailbox |
| **Shared task list** | No | Yes |
| **Default state** | Always on | **Experimental, disabled** (requires `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1`) |
| **Spawning rights** | Only the parent can spawn | "**No nested teams: teammates cannot spawn their own teams or teammates. Only the lead can manage the team.**" |

I called my structure a "team of agents" but I used `Agent` tool calls — i.e., **subagents**. The supervisor I spawned was a subagent. When I instructed it to spawn implementor subagents of its own, it correctly reported that the `Agent` tool wasn't in its tool list. That wasn't a bug or a hallucination — it's how the platform works. **Sub-agents can't spawn other sub-agents. And per the doc, agent-team teammates can't either.** Only the top-level session can spawn workers, full stop.

So the supervisor pattern I designed — a delegate that itself dispatches workers — is unsupported in both Claude Code architectures.

### Secondary issue: agent teams probably wasn't the right tool for this task anyway

The doc is explicit about when agent teams help and when they hurt:

> **Best for**: research and review, new modules where teammates own different files, debugging with competing hypotheses, cross-layer coordination
>
> **Avoid file conflicts. Two teammates editing the same file leads to overwrites.**
>
> **For sequential tasks, same-file edits, or work with many dependencies, a single session or subagents are more effective.**

ABA-138/139/140 all edit the same two files (`ModelReport.jsx`, `styles.css`). The work was inherently sequential. The "team" framing was always a poor fit — what the work actually needed was sequenced single-threaded execution with **context isolation between tasks**, which is a different problem.

### What I think you actually want

Reading your "compact/clear between tasks" requirement again: the value isn't *parallelism*, it's *fresh context per task with a quality gate between them*. That's not what agent teams optimize for. Agent teams optimize for parallel collaboration. Your use case is closer to a **pipeline** than a **team**.

## Options going forward

### Option 1 — Use Claude Code agent teams correctly (the documented path)

Enable the experimental flag, then your prompt looks like:
> *"Create an agent team. Spawn one teammate per ticket [138, 139, 140] using the `agent-skills:build` agent type. Make 139 depend on 138, and 140 depend on 139, so they run sequentially. Require plan approval before each teammate makes changes."*

The top-level session (me) is the lead — that role can't be delegated. Each teammate gets its own fresh context naturally (no compact needed — they're separate sessions from the start). The shared task-list handles dependency ordering.

**Pros**: documented, supported, plan-approval gate is built in, you can interrupt any teammate directly via Shift+Down.
**Cons**: experimental flag with known limitations (no `/resume` support, task status can lag, shutdown is slow); the lead is *me* for the lifetime of the team, so I can't be doing other work; not great for same-file sequential edits.

### Option 2 — Subagents in a pipeline, top-level me as supervisor

I act as supervisor directly. For each ticket I spawn a fresh implementor subagent (fresh context built-in), wait for its return, verify push, mark Linear Done, spawn the next.

**Pros**: works today, no experimental flag, no nested-spawn problem (because I'm top-level), fresh context per task is automatic (subagents are one-shot), I keep the human-escalation seam where it should be.
**Cons**: I'm tied up while it runs (no background — or rather, the supervisor *role* can't be backgrounded since it's me); no shared task list visibility for you between tasks.

This is what your original request actually described, minus my mistake of trying to delegate the supervisor role itself.

### Option 3 — Claude Agent SDK (custom orchestrator)

Build a Python/TypeScript orchestrator using the Agent SDK. You get arbitrary spawning, lifecycle, context-reset, supervision logic — no platform constraints. Pay the cost in code.

**Pros**: no nested-spawn restriction; you can literally implement "supervisor agent that spawns implementor children" because *you* control the spawn loop; cleanly model the pipeline shape.
**Cons**: out of Claude Code, into a build. You'd be running it as a script, not inside this CLI. Loses the IDE/permission/MCP integration you have here.

### Option 4 — Manual git worktrees + multiple Claude Code sessions

You run three Claude Code sessions yourself, one per ticket, each in its own worktree. Maximum isolation, no orchestration code.

**Pros**: dead simple, each session is independent, no shared-file conflicts.
**Cons**: you're the supervisor (manual coordination); doesn't scale past a few tickets.

## My recommendation

For the workflow you described — "queue of tickets, fresh context per ticket, supervisor handles permissions and escalations" — **Option 2 is the lowest-friction match today**. It's what I should have built the first time. The mistake was trying to make the supervisor itself a delegated subagent rather than the top-level session.

**Option 1 (agent teams)** is worth exploring for a *different* shape of work: cross-cutting research, three reviewers debating a PR, exploring competing hypotheses. Try it on a research task, not a build queue.

**Option 3 (Agent SDK)** becomes interesting if you find yourself building the same supervisor logic over and over and want a reusable harness — but only after you've validated the pattern manually first.

Want me to:
- **(a)** Re-run a small test with Option 2 (top-level me as supervisor, fresh implementor per ticket) on a low-stakes ticket to validate the pattern, or
- **(b)** Try Option 1 on something appropriate — a research/review task rather than a build queue, to see how the agent-teams feature actually behaves in practice?

The user wants something *repeatable* and *low-friction* — a pattern they can apply during cycle planning without a lot of thinking each time.\n\nThe lead and user just finished a retrospective on a build cycle that failed in three specific ways — these are your reference cases for \"did the recommendation prevent this\":\n\n1. **Architectural deviation.** The lead designed a \"supervisor sub-agent that spawns implementor sub-agents\" — which the Claude Code platform doesn't actually support (sub-agents can't spawn other sub-agents; only the top-level session can). The supervisor improvised inline and didn't escalate the deviation. The user only learned about it in the supervisor's final report.\n2. **Latent bug folded silently.** A NaN Scalable Vector Graphics crash from ticket 1 was caught during ticket 2's verification and silently folded into ticket 2's commit. The bug→fix mapping is gone from git history.\n3. **User caught what the agent missed.** A visible Y5 label-collision bug in the chart was spotted by the user, not by the agent. Why: the acceptance criteria were mechanical (monotone, cap callout, pre-profit guard, three lines) and didn't include anything about whether the chart is actually *legible*.\n\n# Your task\n\nStress-test the recommendations from `landscape-researcher` and `pattern-mapper`. Your job is *not* to be agreeable. Your job is to find the holes:\n\n- Recommendations that *sound* repeatable but require non-trivial setup the user will skip\n- Patterns that work in the example but break when the user's actual task shape doesn't fit (which it usually won't)\n- Implicit assumptions that the Claude Code platform doesn't support (e.g. \"the supervisor sub-agent will spawn workers\")\n- Failure modes from the recent cycle that the recommendation doesn't prevent\n- Cases where the activation cost (custom skill, hook, MCP server, Agent SDK build) exceeds the benefit at the user's actual cycle volume (~one cycle every week or two, judging by the Linear history)\n- Patterns that move the failure mode rather than eliminate it (e.g. \"add a reviewer sub-agent\" — does that just produce a sub-agent that misses the same bugs?)\n\n# Method\n\n1. As soon as `landscape-researcher` publishes a first draft at `/tmp/orchestration-research/landscape.md`, read it. Then start writing critiques to `/tmp/orchestration-research/critique.md`.\n2. Same with `pattern-mapper`'s `/tmp/orchestration-research/pattern-map.md`.\n3. When you find something sharp, DM the relevant teammate. Be direct. Quote the specific claim and explain the problem. \"This claim doesn't survive contact with [specific failure mode]\" is the right register.\n4. Don't critique things that are genuinely sound. Save fire for what matters.\n5. Re-read after they revise — if they ignored your critique, escalate to the lead.\n\n# What you're NOT\n\nYou're not a generic naysayer. Don't critique for the sake of critiquing. You're the engineer who notices that:\n- The proposed pattern requires three hooks the user will never write\n- The \"decision tree\" has a path that maps to a pattern the platform doesn't actually support\n- The \"fresh implementor per task\" recommendation doesn't say *who* spawns the fresh implementor — and if the answer is \"a coordinator sub-agent\" we just relived the failure\n- The recommendation assumes a quality gate that's the same shape as the one that just missed the Y5 collision\n\n# Done state\n\nWhen `/tmp/orchestration-research/critique.md` is in good shape AND your teammates have either revised or pushed back, message the lead with: (1) the 2-3 things the lead MUST surface in the final recommendation regardless of what the others say, (2) any structural concerns with the team's emerging consensus.\n\nStart now — read the files when they appear, write critiques as you go, DM proactively."

1	# Pattern map — task shapes → orchestration patterns
2	
3	**Purpose.** Once a feature is broken into sub-tasks during cycle planning, the *shape* of that breakdown should imply the orchestration approach — no fresh judgement per cycle. This file maps shape to pattern so the user can run a 60-second checklist at planning time and walk away with an execution plan.
4	
5	**Companion docs.**
6	- `landscape.md` (landscape-researcher) — full catalogue of orchestration primitives available in Claude Code.
7	- `critique.md` (devils-advocate) — hard constraints + failure modes (F1/F2/F3) any recommendation must prevent.
8	
9	**Hard platform constraints baked in** (from critique.md):
10	- **No sub-agent recursion.** Only the top-level session can fan out. Any "supervisor spawns implementor" design is platform-invalid.
11	- **Low setup cost.** No per-cycle hook authoring, no MCP builds the user has to maintain across cycles.
12	- **Plan-shape check is at *planning* time, not implementation time** — otherwise F1 (silent architectural deviation) recurs.
13	
14	---
15	
16	## Part 1 — Six axes that decide the pattern
17	
18	After looking at recent cycles (ABA-115 7-slice UI feature; ABA-104 single-ticket spike; ABA-117/116/121/etc. queue of playbook writes; ABA-132 3-slice backend recalibration), these six questions discriminate. Anything more is decoration.
19	
20	| # | Axis | Why it matters | How to answer in 10 seconds |
21	|---|---|---|---|
22	| **A1** | **Single ticket or queue of N?** | Queue → batch primitive; single → focused primitive. | Count the sub-tasks. 1 = single; 2–3 with shared dependency chain = mini-pipeline; 4+ that share a template = queue. |
23	| **A2** | **Sequential dependency or parallel-safe?** | Slice N consumes JSON schema, file, or output that slice N-1 produces → must serialize. Otherwise can fan out. | Read the dependency graph in `plan.md`. Arrows = serialize. No arrows = parallel candidates. |
24	| **A3** | **Same-file edits across sub-tasks?** | Even parallel-safe work that touches the same file produces merge conflicts unless serialized — kill the parallelism gain. | Skim the "Files to touch" line on each sub-task. Any overlap = treat as serial regardless of A2. |
25	| **A4** | **One-way door?** | Schema change, public API, methodology constant, namespace change, vendor lock-in. Reversal is expensive — needs a plan-review gate *before* implementation, not "we'll fix it in review." | Does the change land in `DESIGN.md`, a JSON contract field, a `playbooks/*.md` frontmatter key, a `SKILL.md` schema block, or a public command name? Yes → one-way door. |
26	| **A5** | **Mechanical or subjective acceptance?** | Mechanical = agent self-checks against the criteria (number matches, JSON field present, test passes). Subjective = legibility, narrative quality, "does the chart pass the squint test" — needs human eyes or a *differently-prompted* second pass to catch F3. | If every acceptance criterion can be checked by `grep`, `jq`, a unit test, or "function returns expected value" — mechanical. If any criterion is "reads well," "looks right," "is convincing" — subjective. |
27	| **A6** | **Implementation or research/spike?** | Spike's deliverable is a *written recommendation* — wrong skill if you treat it like implementation. Skill exists: `pde-backend-spike`. | Does the sub-task end in code in the tree, or in a written recommendation that closes with proceed/defer/kill? Latter = spike. |
28	
29	**Not on the list (deliberately culled):**
30	- "Long-running vs short" — Claude Code sessions persist; runtime isn't a planning-time variable.
31	- "How many engineers" — N/A (single operator).
32	- "Confidence level" — handled by the per-task acceptance criteria, not by orchestration choice.
33	
34	---
35	
36	## Part 2 — Decision tree
37	
38	Read top-down. First match wins. Each leaf names: the **shape**, the **pattern**, the **activation trigger**, and the **F1/F2/F3 guards** that the pattern must include.
39	
40	> Pattern names below are placeholders until `landscape.md` is final. I'll reconcile them with the landscape's taxonomy in a follow-up pass — the *shapes* and *guards* are the load-bearing part of this map.
41	
42	### Leaf L0 — One-way door of any kind
43	**Trigger:** A4 = yes (touches DESIGN.md, JSON contract, public command name, SKILL frontmatter, methodology constant).
44	
45	**Pattern:** **Plan-review gate before any implementation, run from the top-level session.**
46	
47	**Activation:** Before assigning any sub-task, the lead invokes `/pde:plan-review` (or `agent-skills:review`) on the plan itself. The reviewer is a *fresh-context* second pass — its job is to catch architectural deviations and infeasible shapes *before* anyone starts coding. **This is the F1 guard.** If the planned orchestration shape ("supervisor sub-agent spawns workers") is platform-invalid, the review must catch it here — not when the supervisor improvises mid-flight.
48	
49	**Then route the resulting sub-tasks** through the appropriate leaf below.
50	
51	---
52	
53	### Leaf L1 — Single ticket, research/spike
54	**Trigger:** A1 = 1, A6 = spike.
55	
56	**Examples this repo:** ABA-104 (base-year-effect detection), ABA-103 (auto-derive WACC).
57	
58	**Pattern:** **Solo focused execution, top-level session, with the spike skill.**
59	
60	**Activation:** `/pde-backend-spike` or `agent-skills:plan` → `agent-skills:build`. No sub-agents. No fan-out. The deliverable is a written recommendation document, not code.
61	
62	**F-guards:**
63	- F1 — N/A (no sub-agent layer to deviate).
64	- F2 — N/A (no parallel commits).
65	- F3 — N/A unless the spike produces a chart/visual.
66	
67	---
68	
69	### Leaf L2 — Single ticket, implementation, mechanical acceptance
70	**Trigger:** A1 = 1, A5 = mechanical, A6 = implementation.
71	
72	**Examples this repo:** ABA-110 (SBC strip), ABA-111 (growth-rate cap), ABA-130 (coverage bypass).
73	
74	**Pattern:** **Solo focused execution, top-level session.**
75	
76	**Activation:** `agent-skills:build` or the relevant skill-creator workflow. Sub-agent is overkill — the criteria are checkable in-session.
77	
78	**F-guards:**
79	- F1, F2 — N/A.
80	- F3 — N/A (no subjective output).
81	
82	---
83	
84	### Leaf L3 — Single ticket, implementation, subjective acceptance
85	**Trigger:** A1 = 1, A5 = subjective.
86	
87	**Examples this repo:** ABA-138 in isolation (CAGR glidepath — *if* the chart had been the whole ticket, not a slice of 7). The Y5-label collision (F3) lived here.
88	
89	**Pattern:** **Solo focused execution + post-implementation second-pass review with a differently-shaped checklist.**
90	
91	**Activation:** `agent-skills:build` for the work, then `/crit` or a fresh-context agent invocation with the explicit prompt *"squint test only — is the output legible? would a reader of this artefact get the thesis without the author standing next to them?"* The second pass MUST NOT re-apply the mechanical acceptance criteria — they already passed. Its job is what those criteria missed.
92	
93	**F-guards:**
94	- F3 — guarded by the differently-shaped second pass. Critical: the second pass uses *different* prompt language from the original AC, otherwise it reproduces the original blind spot.
95	
96	---
97	
98	### Leaf L4 — Multi-slice with sequential dependency (chained gates)
99	**Trigger:** A1 = 2–N, A2 = sequential, A3 doesn't matter (serialized anyway).
100	
101	**Examples this repo:** ABA-115 slices 1→2→3 (UI shell → SKILL schema change → re-run reports). Slice 3 can't run before Slice 2's JSON field exists.
102	
103	**Pattern:** **Single-threaded gate sequence, one sub-task per top-level invocation, explicit gate check between each.**
104	
105	**Activation:** No fan-out. After each sub-task lands, the *lead* (not an agent) confirms the gate criterion before issuing the next sub-task. Use `agent-skills:build` per task. The gate criterion sits at the *end* of the previous task's acceptance section — read it back before kicking off the next one.
106	
107	**F-guards:**
108	- F1 — N/A (no concurrent agents to deviate from).
109	- F2 — *high risk here.* If a bug from slice N is discovered during slice N+1, the discipline is: **commit the fix as a separate commit attributed to slice N's ticket, before slice N+1's work resumes**. This is the F2 guard. Bake this rule into every multi-slice plan, not just this leaf.
110	- F3 — apply L3's second-pass review at the end of the chain if any slice produces subjective output.
111	
112	---
113	
114	### Leaf L5 — Multi-slice with parallel-safe slices (fan-out)
115	**Trigger:** A1 = 2–N, A2 = parallel after some shared prefix, A3 = different files.
116	
117	**Examples this repo:** ABA-115 slices 4/5/6 (three independent chart components, each its own `ui/src/components/charts/*.jsx`, all consuming the same JSON contract from Slice 3).
118	
119	**Pattern:** **Top-level fan-out, one teammate-agent per slice, lead aggregates.**
120	
121	**Activation:** From the top-level Claude Code session, spawn N teammate agents *in parallel*, one per slice. Each runs `agent-skills:build` against its assigned sub-task. **Critical:** the lead-level session must be the parent — not a "supervisor sub-agent". (Per critique F1: sub-agents cannot spawn sub-agents in Claude Code; any plan that names a supervisor layer is platform-invalid and must be caught at L0 plan review.)
122	
123	**Pre-flight checklist before fan-out:**
124	1. Confirm each slice touches a *different file set* (A3). If two slices touch the same file, demote to L4 chain.
125	2. Confirm the shared upstream dependency has landed (the gate from L4 → L5 transition).
126	3. Each slice gets its own commit. **F2 guard:** if an agent on slice N finds a bug from the shared prefix (e.g. Slice 3), the bug-fix is a *separate commit* attributed back to Slice 3, *before* the slice-N commit. The agent must escalate to the lead before squashing.
127	
128	**F-guards:**
129	- F1 — caught at L0 plan review (no recursive supervisor design).
130	- F2 — covered by the "separate commit, escalate" rule above. If the implementor agent silently folds the fix into its own commit, that's the failure mode from ABA-138→ABA-143/144.
131	- F3 — at the end of the fan-out, the lead invokes the L3 second-pass review across the *aggregated* output, not per-slice. The label-collision bug was visible at the aggregated render, not in any single component.
132	
133	---
134	
135	### Leaf L6 — Queue of N file-isolated tickets sharing one template
136	**Trigger:** A1 = 4+, A2 = independent, A3 = each touches a different file (typically by ticker / by component / by ID).
137	
138	**Examples this repo:** ABA-120/121/122/123/124 (write playbooks: META, NVDA, AMZN, NFLX, ADYEN) — each touches `playbooks/TICKER.md`, format is locked, no inter-dependencies.
139	
140	**Pattern:** **Top-level queue dispatch — assign each ticket to a teammate-agent at planning time; let them run in parallel; lead reviews each result against the same checklist.**
141	
142	**Activation:** Same as L5 mechanically, but the "shape template" is identical across N tickets. The lead writes the template prompt *once* at planning, then dispatches N teammates. Use the same acceptance criteria for all N.
143	
144	**F-guards:**
145	- F1 — same as L5.
146	- F2 — low risk (file-isolated; no cross-ticket bugs by construction).
147	- F3 — apply once at the end. If each playbook needs a subjective "does this read well" pass, run the L3 second-pass across the batch — but use one reviewer, not N — so it catches *cross-playbook* inconsistencies (vocabulary drift, narrative style).
148	
149	---
150	
151	### Leaf L7 — Mixed: feature with both a shared prefix and a fan-out tail
152	**Trigger:** ABA-115-shaped: chain → fan-out → close-out. This is L4 then L5 then L4 again.
153	
154	**Pattern:** **Compose L4 and L5 explicitly in the plan.** Write the orchestration plan as "Slices 1–3 chain (L4); Slices 4–6 fan-out (L5); Slice 7 chain (L4)." Hand each phase to the right primitive.
155	
156	**Activation:** This is the most common shape this pack will produce, given the multi-stage skill architecture. Plan should *name the phase transitions* — they're the gate moments where F2 (latent bug) is most likely to surface.
157	
158	---
159	
160	## Part 3 — Three worked examples from this repo
161	
162	### Example 1: ABA-115 — Model tab v1 (mixed L4→L5→L4)
163	**Sub-tasks (7 slices, see `tasks/plan.md`):**
164	1. UI shell (no SKILL change)
165	2. SKILL schema: emit `historical_fcf_margins[]`
166	3. Re-run covered tickers
167	4. CAGR glidepath chart
168	5. FCF margin trajectory chart
169	6. Scenario fan chart
170	7. Captions / styling / docs
171	
172	**Axis read:**
173	- A1: 7 slices.
174	- A2: 1→2→3 sequential; 4/5/6 parallel-safe; 7 final.
175	- A3: 4/5/6 each touch their own file (`charts/*.jsx`).
176	- A4: yes — Slice 2 changes a JSON contract (`historical_fcf_margins[]` is a one-way door for the report schema).
177	- A5: slices 1–3 mechanical (JSON field present, audit row matches); slices 4–7 subjective (chart legibility).
178	- A6: implementation.
179	
180	**Pattern: L0 plan review → L4 (1→2→3) → L5 fan-out (4‖5‖6) → L4 (7) → L3 second pass.**
181	
182	**What actually went wrong** (per critique.md): the lead chose "supervisor sub-agent + implementor sub-agents" — platform-invalid. L0 plan review would have caught this at planning time. Instead, F1 fired: the supervisor improvised inline and didn't escalate. F2 fired: a NaN bug from slice 4 was folded into slice 5's commit. F3 fired: a Y5 label collision shipped because the AC was mechanical, not subjective.
183	
184	**What the L0+L5+L3 chain would have changed:**
185	- L0 catches "supervisor spawns implementor" → reshapes to top-level fan-out.
186	- L5's separate-commit-for-prior-slice-bugs rule prevents F2.
187	- L3 second-pass review with explicit "squint test" prompt catches F3 before merge.
188	
189	---
190	
191	### Example 2: ABA-104 — Base-year-effect spike (L1)
192	**Sub-tasks (1):** Investigate the failure mode, recommend detection tripwire + response strategy + scope, file follow-up.
193	
194	**Axis read:**
195	- A1: 1.
196	- A6: spike (recommendation-only).
197	
198	**Pattern: L1 — Solo with `/pde-backend-spike`.**
199	
200	This is the case where adding sub-agents is *worse* than running solo — the deliverable is reasoning, not code, and reasoning fragments badly across context boundaries.
201	
202	**Outcome:** Done; recommendation produced an implementation follow-up (ABA-134). No F1/F2/F3 exposure because there's no orchestration layer.
203	
204	---
205	
206	### Example 3: ABA-120/121/122/123/124 — Five playbook writes (L6)
207	**Sub-tasks (5):** One playbook per remaining covered ticker (META, NVDA, AMZN, NFLX, ADYEN.AS).
208	
209	**Axis read:**
210	- A1: 5.
211	- A2: independent.
212	- A3: each touches its own `playbooks/TICKER.md`.
213	- A4: the *first* playbook to land sets the prose template (one-way door once the template is established). Subsequent four are not one-way.
214	- A5: subjective (narrative quality, sell-side argument balance).
215	- A6: implementation (creative writing).
216	
217	**Pattern: L0 plan review (once, to lock the template via the first playbook) → L6 queue dispatch for the remaining four → L3 cross-playbook second pass.**
218	
219	**Notes:**
220	- ABA-117 (GOOG) and ABA-116 (ASML) already shipped — they set the template. Future five run as pure L6.
221	- L3 second pass must be done **once across all five**, not per-playbook — the failure mode is vocabulary/narrative drift between playbooks, invisible in any single one.
222	
223	---
224	
225	## Part 4 — The cycle-planning ritual
226	
227	A six-question checklist applied to each parent ticket during planning. Output is the orchestration plan, written one line into the parent's `plan.md`.
228	
229	```
230	1. Count sub-tasks (A1). One → L1 or L2 or L3. Several → continue.
231	2. Draw the dependency graph (A2). All arrows? L4. No arrows after a prefix? L5/L7. Pure independent? L6.
232	3. Highlight same-file overlaps (A3). Any overlap → serialize that pair regardless of step 2.
233	4. Mark one-way doors (A4). Any → schedule L0 plan review before kicking off implementation.
234	5. Mark subjective acceptance (A5). Any → schedule L3 second pass after implementation; write the squint-test prompt at planning time, not at review time.
235	6. Write the one-line orchestration plan into `plan.md`, e.g.: "L0 → L4(1-3) → L5(4‖5‖6) → L4(7) → L3 squint pass."
236	```
237	
238	**Add to the bottom of `plan.md`:**
239	- The orchestration plan from step 6 above.
240	- The squint-test prompt (if step 5 fired) — pre-written, so the L3 reviewer has it ready.
241	- The F2 rule restated: *"If an agent finds a bug from a prior slice, commit the fix separately and attribute to the originating ticket before continuing."*
242	
243	**Total time: ≈ 60 seconds per parent ticket** once the planner has the axes memorised.
244	
245	---
246	
247	## Open questions / followups
248	
249	- **Reconcile pattern names** with `landscape.md` once landscape-researcher publishes. The leaves (L0–L7) and the *shape* logic are stable; the words used for the patterns at each leaf may need renaming.
250	- **`/crit` integration.** L3 second-pass review could use `/crit` with the explicit squint-test prompt. Worth a one-cycle trial.
251	- **F2 enforcement.** Currently a *rule* the agent must follow. A hook (`stop-the-line` adjacent) could mechanically refuse to commit a diff that touches files outside the assigned slice's "Files to touch" list. Out of scope here — flag to user as a future hook candidate.
252
