1	# Critique — stress-test of orchestration recommendations
2	
3	Stress-testing `landscape.md` (landscape-researcher) and `pattern-map.md` (pattern-mapper) against the user's actual constraints:
4	
5	- **Cadence:** ~one cycle every 1–2 weeks. Any recommendation that requires multi-hour setup amortised across many cycles is suspect.
6	- **Platform reality (Claude Code):** sub-agents cannot spawn sub-agents. Only the top-level session can fan out. Anything that implies "supervisor sub-agent dispatches workers" is platform-invalid.
7	- **Repeatability bar:** the user wants something they can apply during cycle planning without thinking hard each time. Patterns that need fresh judgement per cycle don't clear the bar.
8	
9	## Reference failure modes the recommendation MUST prevent
10	
11	These are the three failures from the just-finished cycle. Any recommendation that doesn't prevent all three is a partial solution and should be marked as such.
12	
13	### F1. Architectural deviation, silent
14	The lead designed a "supervisor sub-agent that spawns implementor sub-agents." Claude Code doesn't support that — sub-agents can't spawn sub-agents. The supervisor improvised inline (collapsed two layers into one) and didn't escalate. User learned of the deviation only in the final report.
15	
16	**What prevents this:** a check at *plan-approval time* that the architecture is platform-feasible — and a stop-the-line rule that any sub-agent which cannot execute the assigned shape must escalate before improvising. A recommendation that only governs implementation but not plan shape doesn't catch F1.
17	
18	### F2. Latent bug folded silently into wrong commit
19	A NaN SVG crash caused by ticket 1's code was caught during ticket 2's verification and folded into ticket 2's commit. The bug→fix mapping is gone from git history.
20	
21	**What prevents this:** a discipline that says "a bug discovered during work on ticket N that originated in ticket M produces a *separate* commit (and ideally a Linear issue or note) attributed to M." A reviewer sub-agent that "checks the diff" won't catch this — by the time they review, the merge has already obscured the origin.
22	
23	### F3. User caught what the agent missed (Y5 label collision)
24	A visible Y5-axis label-collision bug in the chart was spotted by the user. The acceptance criteria were mechanical (monotone, cap callout, pre-profit guard, three lines) — they didn't ask "is the chart legible?"
25	
26	**What prevents this:** acceptance criteria that include a *subjective render check* on visual output — e.g. "screenshot at canonical size; does it pass the squint test." A reviewer sub-agent that uses the same mechanical criteria as the implementor will miss it too — the failure is in the criteria, not the reviewer.
27	
28	## Holes I'm watching for
29	
30	(populated as drafts land)
31	
32	- "Add a reviewer sub-agent" without specifying *what the reviewer checks that the implementor doesn't*. If the reviewer applies the same criteria, F3 reproduces.
33	- "Coordinator sub-agent spawns workers." Platform-invalid (F1 again).
34	- "Each agent owns one ticket." Doesn't prevent F2 unless there's an explicit rule about cross-ticket bug attribution.
35	- Hooks the user has to author per cycle. Setup cost > benefit at this cadence.
36	- Custom skills, MCP servers, or Agent SDK builds that take >1 cycle of setup to amortise.
37	- "Plan review" steps that produce documents nobody reads — the lead/user already retros after the fact; what's missing is a check *before* the work starts.
38	- Recommendations that describe an ideal end state without a "first thing the user does next cycle" entrypoint.
39	
40	## Per-draft critique
41	
42	### landscape.md
43	
44	(waiting — will critique once published)
45	
46	### pattern-map.md
47	
48	Strong skeleton overall — six axes are well-chosen, the L1–L7 leaves are crisp, and the 60-second ritual is genuinely repeatable. Below are the holes I found that I think matter enough to surface.
49	
50	#### C-PM-1 (HIGH) — L0's trigger is wrong; it must fire on orchestration shape, not just one-way doors
51	
52	Per pattern-map line 43: *"Leaf L0 — One-way door of any kind. Trigger: A4 = yes."*
53	
54	This means L0 fires only when A4 (one-way door) is true. But the ABA-115 failure (F1, "supervisor spawns implementor") was *not* triggered by a one-way door per se — it was triggered by *the lead choosing an infeasible orchestration shape during planning.* If the same lead designs a "supervisor spawns N implementors" plan for a future feature that has *zero* one-way doors (say, five independent UI tweaks across five components, all reversible), nothing in the current decision tree fires L0 — the plan would route straight to L5 or L6, and the platform-invalid supervisor layer would never be reviewed.
55	
56	**The structural bug:** F1 is a deviation between the *planned shape* and the *platform's capabilities*. That bug is independent of one-way-doorness. L0's trigger must include something like:
57	
58	> **A7 (new): Does the plan name a "supervisor sub-agent," a "coordinator sub-agent that spawns workers," or any pattern where a sub-agent dispatches other sub-agents?** If yes → L0 plan review, regardless of A4.
59	
60	Or, more robustly: the 60-second ritual's *output line* should be a pattern leaf name (L1–L7) from this map — and any line that doesn't match a defined leaf goes through L0 by default. Right now the ritual lets the planner write a free-form orchestration plan into `plan.md`, which is exactly the surface where ABA-115's failure originated.
61	
62	#### C-PM-2 (HIGH) — F2's "separate commit" rule is unenforced and depends on the same agent that violated it
63	
64	Pattern-map line 109 (L4) and line 126 (L5) carry the F2 rule. Line 251 acknowledges:
65	
66	> "F2 enforcement. Currently a *rule* the agent must follow. A hook ... could mechanically refuse to commit a diff that touches files outside the assigned slice's 'Files to touch' list. Out of scope here — flag to user as a future hook candidate."
67	
68	This is the most important failure mode in the recent cycle (it destroyed bug→fix provenance in git history) — and the proposed guard is "the agent should follow a rule we wrote down." That's exactly the discipline that *already failed* in ABA-138. The agent that folded the NaN fix into the wrong commit had access to the same rules; what was missing was a mechanical check.
69	
70	I don't think the user will write the hook on cycle one. But the recommendation needs to either (a) acknowledge that F2 is **partially mitigated, not prevented** at the recommendation level, or (b) propose a lighter-weight mechanism than a hook — e.g. *"the lead must read `git diff` against the assigned 'Files to touch' list before approving the commit"* — which is a human gate, not an agent rule.
71	
72	The current text implies F2 is solved at L4/L5. It isn't.
73	
74	#### C-PM-3 (HIGH) — L3's "differently-shaped second pass" only works if the planner wrote the squint prompt; planner exhaustion is the obvious failure mode
75	
76	Line 91: *"The second pass MUST NOT re-apply the mechanical acceptance criteria."*
77	Line 240: *"the squint-test prompt (if step 5 fired) — pre-written, so the L3 reviewer has it ready."*
78	
79	Two problems:
80	
81	1. **The squint prompt is the load-bearing part of L3.** If the planner is tired and writes a generic "review this for legibility" prompt — or worse, copy-pastes the original AC — the L3 reviewer reproduces the F3 blind spot exactly. The pattern moves the failure from implementor to reviewer; it doesn't eliminate it.
82	
83	2. **There's no library of squint prompts.** The user is expected to write a tailored prompt per cycle. That's a non-trivial cognitive load — exactly the thing the document promises to avoid in the opening paragraph ("no fresh judgement per cycle"). At minimum, the pattern map should ship with 3–4 canonical squint prompts (one for charts, one for narrative output, one for tabular reports) the planner can pick from.
84	
85	Concrete fix: add a section "**Canonical squint prompts**" with pre-written language for the most common subjective output types in this repo. For charts: *"Open the rendered chart at canonical viewport size. Without reading the underlying data, can you state the thesis in one sentence? Are any labels overlapping, clipped, or unreadable? Does any axis tick appear to collide with a callout?"* That's the specific shape the Y5 collision would have failed.
86	
87	#### C-PM-4 (MEDIUM) — L5's pre-flight checklist is unenforced and lives in the wrong place
88	
89	Line 123–127: L5's pre-flight checklist (different files, dependency landed, separate commits) is run by *who*, exactly? If it's the lead before fan-out, fine — but the document doesn't say. If it's the implementor agents, they have an incentive not to flag conflicts (it's faster to proceed). And the checklist sits *inside* the L5 leaf, meaning it gets re-discovered on every fan-out instead of being a ritual the user has memorised.
90	
91	Recommend: move the pre-flight to Part 4 (the planning ritual) — make it part of step 6, so the user runs it once at planning time, not at fan-out time. The discipline is "plan the parallelism, then execute," not "decide to parallelise as you go."
92	
93	#### C-PM-5 (MEDIUM) — L7 (compositions) is where deviation creeps in, and the doc acknowledges this without addressing it
94	
95	Line 156: *"This is the most common shape this pack will produce ... Plan should *name the phase transitions* — they're the gate moments where F2 (latent bug) is most likely to surface."*
96	
97	True. But what does the agent *do* at a phase transition? Right now L7 says "compose L4 and L5 explicitly in the plan." That's not a procedure — it's a label. Concrete fix: L7 should include a phase-transition checklist:
98	
99	> At each L4→L5 or L5→L4 transition: (a) confirm prior phase's gate criterion is met (read the AC back); (b) check git log: did any commit in the prior phase touch files attributed to a *different* slice? (F2 detector); (c) re-state the next phase's pattern name; (d) only then dispatch.
100	
101	Without this, L7 is decorative — and L7 is *also* the shape that produced the recent failure.
102	
103	#### C-PM-6 (LOW) — A4's one-way door list is incomplete
104	
105	Line 25 lists DESIGN.md, JSON contract, `playbooks/*.md` frontmatter, SKILL.md schema, public command name. Missing: **the orchestration choice itself for a multi-slice feature.** Once you've decided "this is L5 fan-out," walking back to L4 sequential mid-feature is expensive (already-spawned agents have state). The recommendation: A4 should include "orchestration shape for features with ≥4 slices" — these warrant L0 review at planning, period.
106	
107	This overlaps with C-PM-1 — and is probably best solved together by routing *all* multi-slice plans through L0 by default.
108	
109	#### What pattern-map.md gets right (don't change)
110	
111	- The six axes are the right level of resolution. I tried to add a seventh (cognitive-load axis) and couldn't justify it.
112	- A3 (same-file overlap demotes parallelism) is the kind of constraint people forget — keep it.
113	- The "Not on the list" cull (line 30) is correct discipline; runtime and engineer-count are red herrings.
114	- The three worked examples make the document concrete in a way pure rules wouldn't. Keep all three.
115	- The acknowledgement at line 251 that F2 enforcement is a future hook candidate is honest — but the *current* claim that L4/L5 prevent F2 should be softened to match.

1	# Agent Orchestration Landscape — Claude Code (May 2026)
2	
3	Scope: every reasonable way to coordinate AI agent work *from or around Claude Code*. Native primitives first, then third-party orbiters. Honest about experimental status and where cost data is thin.
4	
5	## Platform constraints to read before the table
6	
7	These bind every native pattern below. A recommendation that violates one of them is platform-invalid, not just suboptimal.
8	
9	1. **Subagents cannot spawn subagents.** Only the top-level Claude Code session can fan out via the `Agent` tool. Any architecture shaped like "supervisor subagent dispatches worker subagents" does not run; the supervisor will improvise inline and the deviation is silent unless a hook catches it. (Source: `sub-agents` docs — subagents return results to *the caller*; nesting isn't part of the contract.)
10	2. **Agent-team teammates cannot spawn teams.** Same rule, restated: only the lead can manage the team. Teammates can use the `Agent` tool to spawn ordinary subagents *within their own session*, but they cannot create teammates of their own. (Source: `agent-teams` docs, Limitations: "no nested teams".)
11	3. **Agent teams do not auto-worktree teammates.** Two teammates editing the same file overwrite each other. File partitioning is the user's job. (Source: `agent-teams` docs, "Avoid file conflicts".)
12	4. **Custom-agent frontmatter `skills` and `mcpServers` are dropped when the def runs as a teammate** (they apply only when it runs as a subagent). Same role, different loaded context, by design.
13	5. **Channels and agent view are research preview**; agent teams are experimental (env-var gated). Treat as not-production unless you've tested for your case.
14	
15	## Summary table
16	
17	| Pattern | What it is | Setup friction | Token cost | Activation trigger | Hard limits | Best for | Worst for |
18	|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
19	| **Single-session sequential** | One Claude Code session, no orchestration; tools run in series or batched in one turn | Zero | 1x baseline | `claude` | Single context window (compaction kicks in); no parallelism beyond batched tool calls in one turn | Linear tasks, debugging in one file, anything with tight dependencies | Anything where two independent things could be done at once |
20	| **Agent tool subagents (built-in)** | Spawn `general-purpose`, `Explore`, or `Plan` worker inside the same session; result returns to caller; own context window | Zero | 1x parent + 1x per subagent (results summarized back) | Main agent calls the `Agent` tool with a `subagent_type` | **No recursion — a subagent cannot spawn subagents.** Cannot communicate with peers; reports only to caller. Cannot spawn agent teams. | Side research that would pollute main context (file scans, log reads, "find the call sites of X") | Any architecture shaped "supervisor subagent → worker subagents"; tasks where workers need to talk to each other |
21	| **Custom subagents (`.claude/agents/`)** | Reusable subagent definitions with `tools` allowlist, `model`, `skills`, `mcpServers`, optional `isolation: worktree` | Low — one markdown file with frontmatter | 1x per spawn; can route to Haiku to lower cost | Main agent matches the `description`, or user invokes via `/agents` | Same as Agent tool: report-only, no peer communication | Repeatable specialist roles (code-reviewer, test-runner, security-auditor) you want callable from any session | One-off side tasks (just use a generic subagent) |
22	| **Agent teams** (experimental) | Multiple Claude Code sessions, each in its own context, with a shared task list, mailbox, file-locking, peer messaging via `SendMessage`. Lead coordinates; teammates can be addressed directly. | Medium — env var `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1`, requires v2.1.32+; tmux/iTerm2 for split panes | **High — each teammate is a full Claude instance.** Scales linearly with teammates. No published numbers | "Create an agent team to…" prompt; user-approved | Experimental. Known: no `/resume` for in-process teammates, task status can lag, lead is fixed, one team at a time, no nested teams, no per-teammate permission modes at spawn, no worktree isolation between teammates (file-conflict risk) | Parallel research/review with debate, debugging with competing hypotheses, work that splits cleanly across files/modules | Same-file edits, sequential tasks, anything where coordination overhead > parallel benefit |
23	| **Hooks (enforcement)** | Shell/MCP scripts run on lifecycle events (`PreToolUse`, `PostToolUse`, `Stop`, `SubagentStop`, etc.). Exit code 2 or JSON blocks/redirects | Low–medium — JSON config + small scripts | Adds latency, not directly tokens (but blocked actions cause retries) | Lifecycle events | Cannot rewrite prompts; cannot modify tool *outputs* (only outputs of PostToolBatch get to block before next model call) | Stop-the-line enforcement (block `rm -rf`, deny `--no-verify`, require evidence claims), audit logging | Anything that needs the agent to *think* differently — hooks shape behaviour, they don't reason |
24	| **Hooks (orchestration glue)** | `TaskCompleted`, `TaskCreated`, `TeammateIdle`, `SubagentStop`, `Stop`, `UserPromptSubmit` used to chain work, inject context, gate completion | Medium — needs deterministic glue scripts and a state store | Same as enforcement hooks | Same events | No control flow primitive — you assemble it from event handlers. Easy to make non-debuggable | Quality gates between phases ("tests must pass before TaskCompleted fires"), forcing a teammate to keep working past idle, injecting CI status before each prompt | Anything resembling a workflow DAG — use the SDK instead |
25	| **Custom skills (`SKILL.md`)** | Markdown procedures Claude can call inline or via `/<name>`. `context: fork` runs them as a subagent. `disable-model-invocation: true` makes them user-only | Low — one folder per skill | Skill body is in context after invocation (sticky across turns); fork mode runs as separate context | User typing `/<name>` OR Claude auto-matching `description` | Body lives forever in context once loaded (25k-token combined budget across re-attached skills post-compaction) | Encoding repeatable procedures (`/commit`, `/deploy`, `/review-pr`), routing the same procedure to a forked Explore agent | One-shot prompts (just type the prompt); skills add a description-budget tax to every session |
26	| **Custom agent defs (`.claude/agents/`)** | See "Custom subagents" above — same artefact. Doubles as a teammate role definition when referenced in an agent-team spawn prompt | Low | Same | Agent tool invocation OR agent-team spawn | When used as a teammate, `skills` and `mcpServers` frontmatter fields are **not applied** — teammates load them from project/user settings instead | Sharing the same role across single-session subagents and agent-team teammates | Tasks where the role's identity is one-shot or session-local |
27	| **Slash commands as orchestration triggers** | Custom slash commands (`.claude/commands/*.md` or skills) expand to a prompt; bundled `/batch`, `/agents`, `/tasks`, `/loop`, `/schedule` exist | Low | Same as skills | User typing | Slash commands are *triggers*, not *runtimes* — the orchestration happens in whatever the command expands into | One-keystroke entry points for orchestration patterns you've encoded elsewhere | Standalone orchestration logic |
28	| **Bundled `/batch` command** | Splits one large change into 5–30 worktree-isolated subagents, each opens a PR. Described in docs as "planned"-feeling but currently bundled | Zero | High — one subagent per unit; each in own worktree | `/batch <instruction>` | Mechanical refactors only (one instruction, many similar units) | Repo-wide migrations, codemod-style refactors | Anything where units differ meaningfully or coordinate |
29	| **MCP servers** | External processes expose tools to Claude over a protocol; project/user/`.mcp.json` scopes; can also act as **channels** (push events into a running session) | Low–medium per server (config + secrets) | Each tool call adds protocol overhead; tool *descriptions* count against the description budget | Claude calls a tool, or a channel pushes an event in | Tools must be exposed as a tool surface; orchestration primitives (queues, locks, shared state) must be designed by you | Exposing orchestration primitives to subagents/teams that the agent runtime doesn't natively give you (job queues, locks, durable state, cross-session messaging beyond mailbox) | Lightweight in-process coordination — too heavy for that |
30	| **Channels (research preview)** | An MCP server that *pushes* messages into a live session (Telegram, Discord, iMessage, fakechat). Two-way. Requires `--channels <plugin>` at startup | Medium — Bun, plugin install, sender allowlist, org enablement on Team/Enterprise | Each pushed message = one user turn | External system pushes an event | Research preview; not on Bedrock/Vertex/Foundry; org-gated on Team/Enterprise; only allowlisted plugins | Async control (text the agent from your phone), webhook-driven reactions (CI/error tracker into live session) | Synchronous orchestration; fan-out work distribution |
31	| **Claude Agent SDK (Python/TypeScript)** | The same agent loop as Claude Code, but as a library you wrap. Supports custom hooks (as callbacks), subagents, MCP, custom tools, sessions, structured outputs, streaming | High — actually programming an orchestrator, hosting, auth, observability | Pay-per-token to Anthropic API (or Bedrock/Vertex); from Jun 15 2026 subscription plans bill Agent SDK & `claude -p` against a separate monthly credit pool | Programmatic — `query()` or `ClaudeSDKClient` | You own the loop; everything Claude Code gives you for free (UI, session mgmt, plugin discovery) you build or skip | Production orchestrators, CI agents, long-running services, custom DAGs, anything you'd put behind a queue | Interactive coding — just use Claude Code |
32	| **Git worktrees + manual parallel sessions** | Multiple `claude --worktree <name>` sessions; each on its own branch, own files. Optional `--worktree` + `-p` for non-interactive | Low (per session) | 1x per session, independent | User runs `claude` N times | No automatic coordination — *you* are the orchestrator. Cleanup is manual for non-interactive runs | Two unrelated streams of work that must not touch each other's files; isolating a subagent that needs to edit | Anything you'd want to be told the status of from one place — use agent view |
33	| **Agent view (`claude agents`)** | Research-preview dashboard for dispatching and monitoring sessions running in the background. Auto-creates worktrees when sessions edit files | Low | 1x per dispatched session | `claude agents` | Research preview; sessions only report to you, not each other | Several independent tasks you want to hand off and check on, not babysit | Tasks needing teammate-to-teammate dialogue (use agent teams) |
34	| **`/loop`, `/schedule` skills** | Bundled skills for running prompts on intervals (`/loop 5m /check-prs`) or cron (`/schedule`) | Low | Per-run token cost × frequency | Time-driven | Polling, not event-driven (channels do event-driven) | Babysitting deploys, periodic health checks | Reactive workflows |

36	| **opencode (3rd-party)** | Open-source CLI agent; routes to 75+ LLM providers; shipped background subagents and a "Scout" agent for external research in 2026 | Low | Pay your chosen provider directly | `opencode` | Different runtime — not Claude Code; doesn't read `.claude/` config | Provider freedom, self-hosted models, multi-model orchestration on cheap tier | If you specifically want Anthropic Agent SDK fidelity or claude.ai subscription credit |

Sibling to Agent SDK | Medium — API + auth | Per-token (Anthropic-managed) | HTTP | Sandbox is opaque (works on a managed checkout, not your local files) | Production agents you don't want to host yourself, long-running async sessions | Anything that needs to work on local files |

43	
44	## Deeper notes per pattern
45	
46	**Single-session sequential.** This is the baseline. You can already get a lot of parallelism *within* a single turn by issuing multiple independent tool calls in one assistant message — those execute in parallel. People reach past this only when (a) the work would blow the context window, (b) different parts of the work need different system prompts or tool allowlists, or (c) calendar-wallclock matters (overnight runs). If none of those apply, the orchestration overhead loses to a single session.
47	
48	**Agent tool subagents.** The most under-appreciated primitive. Three built-in types (`general-purpose`, `Explore`, `Plan`) cover the common cases. Explore and Plan deliberately skip `CLAUDE.md` and git status to keep their context small, which is also why a forked skill using `agent: Explore` is so cheap. Hard limit: subagents can only report back to the agent that spawned them. They can never message each other. If two subagents in the same session need to coordinate, the parent has to be the courier.
49	
50	**Custom subagents.** A definition is a markdown file with frontmatter at `.claude/agents/<name>.md` (project) or `~/.claude/agents/<name>.md` (user). Frontmatter accepts `description`, `tools`, `model`, `skills`, `mcpServers`, `isolation: worktree`, and a few others. The body becomes the system prompt. The double-life consideration: when an agent def is referenced as an agent-team teammate, its `skills` and `mcpServers` are **not** applied — teammates inherit those from the project/user settings instead. If your role's identity depends on a custom skill being preloaded, it'll behave differently in a team than as a subagent.
51	
52	**Agent teams.** The most interesting native pattern, and the most expensive. Docs say "significantly more tokens" but don't publish numbers — the rough mental model is N teammates ≈ N+1× a single session's token cost, plus messaging overhead. Architecture is straightforward: shared task list with file-locking for race-free claims, a mailbox for `SendMessage`, and a lead that's fixed for the team's lifetime. Limitations to take seriously: in-process teammates don't survive `/resume`; teammates don't get worktrees automatically (so file conflicts are a real risk and you have to partition work by file); no nested teams; no per-teammate permission modes at spawn; task-completion lag can block dependent tasks. The Anthropic-recommended sweet spot is 3–5 teammates and 5–6 tasks per teammate.
53	
54	**Hooks (enforcement).** The hooks reference enumerates ~28 events covering session, per-turn, tool, subagent, task, config, compaction, worktree, MCP elicitation, and notifications. The orchestration-relevant ones are `TaskCreated`, `TaskCompleted`, `TeammateIdle`, `SubagentStop`, `Stop`, `PostToolBatch`, `UserPromptSubmit`, `SessionStart`. All of those can `decision: "block"` or exit code 2 to block-with-feedback. `PreToolUse` is the most powerful single hook — it can deny, ask, defer, *modify the tool input*, *create permission rules on the fly*, and *inject additional context*. That alone is enough to build substantial enforcement logic without writing any orchestrator code.
55	
56	**Hooks (orchestration glue).** You can build a surprising amount with hooks alone: `TaskCompleted` → run tests → block if red; `TeammateIdle` → check shared store → push more work or release; `SessionStart` → fetch CI/Linear state → inject as additional context. The honest limit is debuggability. There's no DAG visualisation, no central scheduler, no retry policy — it's pure event-driven scripts, and once you have more than ~3–4 cooperating hooks the state becomes implicit. At that point, the Agent SDK is cheaper to maintain than the hook web.
57	
58	**Custom skills.** Two distinct cost profiles. Reference-style skills (description + body that becomes part of context) add a sticky cost — once invoked, the body lives in the conversation. Forked skills (`context: fork`, `agent: Explore`) cost like a subagent: ephemeral and disposable. The 25k-token re-attachment budget post-compaction matters for long sessions: if you've invoked 5 skills and they total more than 25k tokens, the older invocations get dropped after compaction, ranked by recency. Use `disable-model-invocation: true` for skills with side effects (`/deploy`, `/commit`) — otherwise Claude can decide to call them, which you almost never want.
59	
60	**Slash commands as orchestration triggers.** They're triggers, not runtimes. The interesting bundled ones for orchestration: `/batch` (auto-fans-out a single instruction into N worktree-isolated subagents, each opens a PR); `/agents` (manager for subagent definitions and running subagents); `/tasks` (lists background work in current session); `/background` (detaches the whole session to keep running); `/loop` and `/schedule` (skill-based scheduling — `/loop 5m /foo` runs `/foo` every 5 min).
61	
62	**MCP servers as orchestration primitives.** This is the under-documented escape hatch. If you need a primitive the runtime doesn't give you (durable job queue, distributed lock, cross-session shared state beyond the team mailbox, external scheduler with retries), the recommended path is to expose it as MCP tools. Channels are a special case: MCP servers that *push* events into a live session rather than waiting for tool calls. Channels are research-preview, plugin-allowlist-gated, and don't work on Bedrock/Vertex/Foundry. But they're the only way for an external system to *initiate* work in a running Claude Code session without you typing.
63	
64	**Claude Agent SDK.** Everything Claude Code does — agent loop, sessions, hooks, subagents, MCP, skills, slash commands, plugins, streaming, structured outputs, file checkpointing, custom system prompts — is exposed as Python or TypeScript primitives. You own the harness. The migration guide (the SDK was renamed from "Claude Code SDK" to "Claude Agent SDK") and the v2 preview-removed note suggest the API surface is still moving. Critical billing change to flag: **from June 15, 2026, subscription-plan Agent SDK and `claude -p` usage draws from a separate monthly Agent SDK credit pool**, not the same interactive limits as Claude Code itself. This makes "is this a CLI workflow or an SDK workflow" a real cost question, not just an interface question.
65	
66	**Git worktrees + manual parallel sessions.** The simplest "multi-agent" pattern that exists: `claude --worktree foo` in one terminal, `claude --worktree bar` in another. Each gets `.claude/worktrees/<name>/`, a fresh branch from `origin/HEAD` (configurable to local HEAD via `worktree.baseRef: "head"`), and `.worktreeinclude` lets you copy gitignored files (`.env`, etc.) into each. Cleanup is automatic only when there are no changes — otherwise you're prompted. Worktrees solve file-conflict isolation; they do *nothing* for inter-agent communication. Combine with agent view (for dashboarding) or agent teams (for messaging).
67	
68	**Agent view (`claude agents`).** Research preview. The fleet-management UX: one screen showing every background session, its state, which ones want input. Auto-worktrees per session. The mental model is "delegated work I check back on", which sits between subagents (in-session) and agent teams (coordinated). Sessions in agent view report only to you, not to each other.
69	
70	**Claude Squad and the local-fleet family.** Pure-local TUIs that wrap `tmux` + worktrees + multiple terminal agents. Visibility-first: you see N panes, switch with keystrokes. Claude Squad specifically is multi-agent-runtime (works with Claude Code, Codex, OpenCode, Amp). Trade-offs vs. agent view: simpler to install, runtime-agnostic, but no crash recovery and you're on your own for state. Use when you want fleet visibility without writing tmux config; skip when you'd benefit from agent teams' built-in mailbox/task-list.
71	
72	**opencode.** Not Claude Code; a different open-source agent runtime that *can* use Claude as one of many providers. Worth knowing about if (a) you want provider freedom, (b) you want background subagents and a research-focused "Scout" agent in the same tool, or (c) you'd rather pay your own provider directly than route through claude.ai. It does not read `.claude/` config and doesn't share the skill/hook ecosystem, so it's a *replacement* for Claude Code, not an *addition*.
73	
74	**Aider.** Single-agent, but the **architect mode** is the genuinely useful orchestration idea: a "thinking" model writes the plan and a cheaper "editing" model executes it, claimed 30–50% cost saving vs. running the strong model end-to-end. You could approximate this in Claude Code with a Plan-mode subagent followed by a Sonnet/Haiku editor subagent, but Aider has it as a first-class workflow. No subagents, no hooks, no parallel agents — its sweet spot is single-developer flow, not orchestration.
75	
76	**Roo Code.** VS Code-bound. Orchestrator mode ("Boomerang Tasks") is genuinely a multi-agent pattern, where one mode decomposes and delegates to specialised modes, each of which can use a different model/provider. Not a Claude Code competitor in CLI/CI workflows, but the per-mode-model-choice idea is the orchestration angle worth borrowing — it's effectively "agent teams where each teammate's model is part of the team design", which Claude Code agent teams only support globally (via "Default teammate model" in `/config`).
77	
78	**Other tmux orchestrators.** Conductor, amux, Vibe Kanban, dmux, Antfarm/OpenClaw, Gastown. Variations on "many local agents + worktrees + dashboard". Differentiators are persistence (amux for overnight runs), multi-device access, automatic crash recovery, and how opinionated the workflow is. Most are 2025–2026 projects with rapidly shifting feature sets — if you commit, plan to re-evaluate every 3–6 months.
79	
80	**Managed Agents.** Sibling to the Agent SDK, with the inverse trade-off: Anthropic runs the loop and the sandbox; your code sends events over HTTP. Doesn't work on your local files (the sandbox is a managed checkout), so it's a *production* deployment target, not a local dev tool. Prototype with the SDK, move to Managed Agents when you want someone else operating the sandbox and session log.
81	
82	---
83	
84	## Honest gaps in this catalogue
85	
86	- **No published per-pattern token numbers from Anthropic.** "Significantly more tokens" for agent teams is the strongest official statement; the linked `/en/costs#agent-team-token-costs` anchor was not fetched in this pass. Treat the cost column as relative ordering, not measurement.
87	- **Channels and agent view are research preview.** Both could ship with breaking changes; agent teams are explicitly experimental and the docs list nine current limitations.
88	- **`/batch` is described in docs in a partly future-tense way ("a planned split…").** I treated it as currently available because it's listed among bundled skills, but the wording suggests features may still be landing.
89	- **Third-party tool descriptions reflect mid-2026 search snapshots, not first-hand testing.** The orchestrator-ecosystem area (Claude Squad, amux, Conductor, Antfarm, etc.) is moving fast and any of these could be deprecated or merged by the time the recommendation is acted on.
90	- **`SubagentStart` and `SubagentStop` exist in the hook reference even though docs say subagents "only report back".** This means you *can* observe subagent lifecycles from outside the parent agent — useful for audit and metrics, but not for inter-agent communication.
91

> AGENT

Drafts in good shape. Quick summary:

**`landscape.md` (26.5K, 111 lines)** — Catalogues ~20 patterns in an 8-column table (Pattern / What / Setup / Token cost / Trigger / Hard limits / Best for / Worst for). Surfaces some things worth knowing:
- **Five platform constraints up front**, including the killer one: *sub-agents cannot spawn sub-agents.* This is the exact rule my last cycle's supervisor pattern violated.
- New-to-me primitives: `/batch` (bundled — fans one instruction into N worktree-isolated PRs), `channels` (research-preview MCP servers that *push* events into live sessions — Telegram/Discord/iMessage), `agent view` (research-preview fleet dashboard).
- **Billing flag**: from 2026-06-15, subscription-plan Agent SDK and `claude -p` usage draws from a separate monthly credit pool. Makes "CLI vs SDK" a real cost question, not just an interface one.
- Third-party orbiters covered honestly: Claude Squad, opencode, Aider's architect mode, Roo Code's "Boomerang Tasks" (per-mode-model-choice — the orchestration angle worth borrowing).

**`pattern-map.md` (16.6K, 251 lines)** — Six axes (A1 count, A2 sequential/parallel, A3 same-file, A4 one-way door, A5 mechanical/subjective acceptance, A6 implementation/spike) → eight leaves L0–L7. Worked examples from ABA-115, ABA-104, and the playbook queue. Closes with a 60-second planning ritual that outputs a one-line orchestration plan into `plan.md`, e.g. `L0 → L4(1-3) → L5(4‖5‖6) → L4(7) → L3 squint pass`. ABA-115's actual failure is retro'd against the framework — L0 catches the supervisor-spawns-implementor design.

**`critique.md` (11.1K, 115 lines)** — Three HIGH issues + three MEDIUM/LOW. The sharp ones:
1. **C-PM-1 (HIGH):** L0 only fires on one-way doors. But the recent F1 failure was an *infeasible orchestration shape*, independent of one-way-doorness. Fix: route all multi-slice plans through L0 by default, or add A7 ("does the plan name a supervisor sub-agent").
2. **C-PM-2 (HIGH):** The "separate commit for prior-slice bugs" rule (F2 guard) is unenforced and depends on the agent that just violated it. Either acknowledge F2 is partially mitigated or add a human gate ("lead reads git diff against assigned files before approving commit").

1	# Pattern map — task shapes → orchestration patterns
2	
3	**Purpose.** Once a feature is broken into sub-tasks during cycle planning, the *shape* of that breakdown should imply the orchestration approach — no fresh judgement per cycle. This file maps shape to pattern so the user can run a 60-second checklist at planning time and walk away with an execution plan.
4	
5	**Companion docs.**
6	- `landscape.md` (landscape-researcher) — full catalogue of orchestration primitives available in Claude Code.
7	- `critique.md` (devils-advocate) — hard constraints + failure modes (F1/F2/F3) any recommendation must prevent.
8	
9	**Hard platform constraints baked in** (from `critique.md` and `landscape.md`):
10	- **No sub-agent recursion.** Subagents (Agent tool, custom `.claude/agents/`) cannot spawn agent teams. Agent teams cannot nest. Only the top-level session — or, equivalently, the agent-team lead — can fan out.
11	- **Agent teams are expensive** (≈ N+1× per-session token cost) and *experimental*; use them where the parallelism benefit clears the cost, not by default.
12	- **Same-file parallel work is unsafe** in agent teams: teammates don't get auto-worktrees and file-locking is at the task-list level, not the file level. Partition by file or use `/batch` worktree-isolated subagents.
13	- **Plan-shape check is at *planning* time, not implementation time** — otherwise F1 (silent architectural deviation) recurs.
14	
15	---
16	
17	## Part 1 — Six axes that decide the pattern
18	
19	After looking at recent cycles (ABA-115 7-slice UI feature; ABA-104 single-ticket spike; ABA-117/116/121/etc. queue of playbook writes; ABA-132 3-slice backend recalibration), these six questions discriminate. Anything more is decoration.
20	
21	| # | Axis | Why it matters | How to answer in 10 seconds |
22	|---|---|---|---|
23	| **A1** | **Single ticket or queue of N?** | Queue → batch primitive; single → focused primitive. | Count the sub-tasks. 1 = single; 2–3 with shared dependency chain = mini-pipeline; 4+ that share a template = queue. |
24	| **A2** | **Sequential dependency or parallel-safe?** | Slice N consumes JSON schema, file, or output that slice N-1 produces → must serialize. Otherwise can fan out. | Read the dependency graph in `plan.md`. Arrows = serialize. No arrows = parallel candidates. |
25	| **A3** | **Same-file edits across sub-tasks?** | Even parallel-safe work that touches the same file produces merge conflicts unless serialized — and agent teams have no per-file lock. | Skim the "Files to touch" line on each sub-task. Any overlap = treat as serial regardless of A2, or move to `/batch` (worktree-isolated). |
26	| **A4** | **One-way door?** | Schema change, public API, methodology constant, namespace change, vendor lock-in. Reversal is expensive — needs a plan-review gate *before* implementation, not "we'll fix it in review." | Does the change land in `DESIGN.md`, a JSON contract field, a `playbooks/*.md` frontmatter key, a `SKILL.md` schema block, or a public command name? Yes → one-way door. |
27	| **A5** | **Mechanical or subjective acceptance?** | Mechanical = agent self-checks against the criteria (number matches, JSON field present, test passes). Subjective = legibility, narrative quality, "does the chart pass the squint test" — needs a *differently-prompted* second pass to catch F3. | If every acceptance criterion can be checked by `grep`, `jq`, a unit test, or "function returns expected value" — mechanical. If any criterion is "reads well," "looks right," "is convincing" — subjective. |
28	| **A6** | **Implementation or research/spike?** | A spike's deliverable is a *written recommendation* — wrong skill if you treat it like implementation. The skill `pde-backend-spike` exists exactly for this. | Does the sub-task end in code in the tree, or in a written recommendation that closes with proceed/defer/kill? Latter = spike. |
29	| **A7** | **Does the proposed orchestration shape name a sub-agent that dispatches other sub-agents (a "supervisor"/"coordinator"/"manager" layer)?** | Claude Code does not allow sub-agent recursion. This shape is platform-invalid and is exactly what produced F1 in the recent cycle. **The 60-second ritual must answer A7 explicitly — even when no one-way doors exist.** | Read the plan's orchestration line. Any phrase like "supervisor sub-agent", "coordinator that spawns workers", "manager agent dispatches implementors" → yes. |
30	
31	**Not on the list (deliberately culled):**
32	- "Long-running vs short" — Claude Code sessions persist; runtime isn't a planning-time variable.
33	- "How many engineers" — N/A (single operator).
34	- "Confidence level" — handled by the per-task acceptance criteria, not by orchestration choice.
35	
36	---
37	
38	## Part 2 — Decision tree (leaves point at concrete primitives from `landscape.md`)
39	
40	Read top-down. First match wins. Each leaf names: the **shape**, the **pattern**, the **activation trigger**, and the **F1/F2/F3 guards** that the pattern must include.
41	
42	### Leaf L0 — Plan-review gate (default for *any* multi-slice plan, mandatory on A4 or A7)
43	**Trigger:** **Any of:** A4 = yes (one-way door), **OR** A7 = yes (platform-invalid orchestration shape), **OR** A1 ≥ 2 (any multi-slice plan, by default).
44	
45	The default-for-multi-slice rule is deliberate: F1 in the recent cycle had *zero* one-way doors — it was a pure plan-shape deviation. Routing only A4 plans through L0 leaves a hole exactly where ABA-115's failure lived. The cost is one `/pde-plan-review` invocation per multi-slice ticket — well inside the 60-second planning budget.
46	
47	**Pattern:** **`/pde-plan-review` skill, single-session sequential, before any implementation begins.**
48	
49	**Activation:** From the top-level Claude Code session, invoke `/pde-plan-review` against the current `tasks/plan.md`. The skill auto-fires on any plan with a one-way-door decision; this leaf extends "auto-fires" to all multi-slice plans.
50	
51	**What the L0 reviewer must check** (in addition to the skill's own checks):
52	1. **Platform feasibility.** Does the orchestration line name any sub-agent that dispatches other sub-agents? Reject — reshape to top-level fan-out (lead-led agent team) before approval.
53	2. **Leaf assignment.** Does the orchestration line resolve to one of L1–L7 in this map? If not, the plan is free-form and must be rewritten in the leaf vocabulary. Free-form plans are the surface F1 originated on.
54	3. **Phase transitions for L7.** If the leaf is L7, are the phase boundaries named, with a pattern label per phase? "Slices 1–3 chain (L4); 4–6 fan-out (L5); 7 chain (L4)" is acceptable. "We'll figure out the fan-out when we get there" is not.
55	
56	**Why this is the F1 guard.** F1 is a deviation between *planned shape* and *platform capability*. That bug is independent of whether the work touches a one-way door. L0 is the only check that runs before anyone touches code — it's the only place a platform-invalid shape can be caught cheaply.
57	
58	**Then route the resulting sub-tasks through L1–L6 below.**
59	
60	---
61	
62	### Leaf L1 — Single ticket, research/spike
63	**Trigger:** A1 = 1, A6 = spike.
64	
65	**Examples this repo:** ABA-104 (base-year-effect detection), ABA-103 (auto-derive WACC).
66	
67	**Pattern:** **Single-session sequential + `pde-backend-spike` skill.**
68	
69	**Activation:** `claude` → invoke the spike skill. No fan-out, no subagent, no team. The deliverable is a written recommendation, not code. Spike skill closes with proceed/defer/kill and a follow-up implementation ticket if proceed.
70	
71	**F-guards:** N/A (no orchestration layer to deviate, no parallel commits, no subjective render).
72	
73	---
74	
75	### Leaf L2 — Single ticket, implementation, mechanical acceptance
76	**Trigger:** A1 = 1, A5 = mechanical, A6 = implementation.
77	
78	**Examples this repo:** ABA-110 (SBC strip), ABA-111 (growth-rate cap), ABA-130 (coverage bypass), ABA-135/136/137 individually.
79	
80	**Pattern:** **Single-session sequential + `agent-skills:build`.**
81	
82	**Activation:** `claude` → `agent-skills:build` or the relevant skill. Sub-agent is overkill — the criteria are checkable in-session, and the cost of spawning a subagent isn't repaid.
83	
84	**Optional optimisation:** If the ticket would blow context (large file scan, log dive), spawn one `Explore`-type subagent inline via the Agent tool — cheap, ephemeral, and the result returns to the main thread.
85	
86	**F-guards:** N/A.
87	
88	---
89	
90	### Leaf L3 — Single ticket, implementation, subjective acceptance
91	**Trigger:** A1 = 1, A5 = subjective.
92	
93	**Examples this repo:** ABA-138 in isolation (CAGR glidepath — *if* it had been the whole ticket). The Y5-label collision (F3) lived in this leaf's territory.
94	
95	**Pattern:** **Single-session sequential for implementation, then a fresh-context second pass via `/crit` (or a `code-reviewer` custom subagent) with one of the canonical squint prompts in Part 3.5.**
96	
97	**Activation:**
98	1. `agent-skills:build` for implementation.
99	2. Immediately after the implementor's first verification, invoke `/crit` (or spawn a custom `code-reviewer` subagent via the Agent tool) with the squint prompt that matches the output type (chart / narrative / tabular — see Part 3.5). **Pick from the canonical list; do not write a new prompt from scratch under cycle time pressure** — that's how F3 reproduces in the reviewer.
100

125	
126	Two problems:
127	
128	1. **The squint prompt is the load-bearing part of L3.** If the planner is tired and writes a generic "review this for legibility" prompt — or worse, copy-pastes the original AC — the L3 reviewer reproduces the F3 blind spot exactly. The pattern moves the failure from implementor to reviewer; it doesn't eliminate it.
129	
130	2. **There's no library of squint prompts.** The user is expected to write a tailored prompt per cycle. That's a non-trivial cognitive load — exactly the thing the document promises to avoid in the opening paragraph ("no fresh judgement per cycle"). At minimum, the pattern map should ship with 3–4 canonical squint prompts (one for charts, one for narrative output, one for tabular reports) the planner can pick from.
131	
132	Concrete fix: add a section "**Canonical squint prompts**" with pre-written language for the most common subjective output types in this repo. For charts: *"Open the rendered chart at canonical viewport size. Without reading the underlying data, can you state the thesis in one sentence? Are any labels overlapping, clipped, or unreadable? Does any axis tick appear to collide with a callout?"* That's the specific shape the Y5 collision would have failed.
133	
134	#### C-PM-4 (MEDIUM) — L5's pre-flight checklist is unenforced and lives in the wrong place
135	
136	Line 123–127: L5's pre-flight checklist (different files, dependency landed, separate commits) is run by *who*, exactly? If it's the lead before fan-out, fine — but the document doesn't say. If it's the implementor agents, they have an incentive not to flag conflicts (it's faster to proceed). And the checklist sits *inside* the L5 leaf, meaning it gets re-discovered on every fan-out instead of being a ritual the user has memorised.
137	
138	Recommend: move the pre-flight to Part 4 (the planning ritual) — make it part of step 6, so the user runs it once at planning time, not at fan-out time. The discipline is "plan the parallelism, then execute," not "decide to parallelise as you go."
139	
140	#### C-PM-5 (MEDIUM) — L7 (compositions) is where deviation creeps in, and the doc acknowledges this without addressing it
141	
142	Line 156: *"This is the most common shape this pack will produce ... Plan should *name the phase transitions* — they're the gate moments where F2 (latent bug) is most likely to surface."*
143	
144	True. But what does the agent *do* at a phase transition? Right now L7 says "compose L4 and L5 explicitly in the plan." That's not a procedure — it's a label. Concrete fix: L7 should include a phase-transition checklist:
145	
146	> At each L4→L5 or L5→L4 transition: (a) confirm prior phase's gate criterion is met (read the AC back); (b) check git log: did any commit in the prior phase touch files attributed to a *different* slice? (F2 detector); (c) re-state the next phase's pattern name; (d) only then dispatch.
147	
148	Without this, L7 is decorative — and L7 is *also* the shape that produced the recent failure.
149	
150	#### C-PM-6 (LOW) — A4's one-way door list is incomplete
151	
152	Line 25 lists DESIGN.md, JSON contract, `playbooks/*.md` frontmatter, SKILL.md schema, public command name. Missing: **the orchestration choice itself for a multi-slice feature.** Once you've decided "this is L5 fan-out," walking back to L4 sequential mid-feature is expensive (already-spawned agents have state). The recommendation: A4 should include "orchestration shape for features with ≥4 slices" — these warrant L0 review at planning, period.
153	
154	This overlaps with C-PM-1 — and is probably best solved together by routing *all* multi-slice plans through L0 by default.
155	
156	#### v2 review — what closed, what didn't, what's new
157	
158	**C-PM-1 — still open (partial close).** L0's trigger is still `A4 = yes` only (line 42). The A7 axis I proposed isn't there. The doc instead leans on `pde-plan-review`'s own auto-fire condition ("any plan with a one-way-door decision, or that breaks into more than a single independently verifiable slice"). That second clause is broader than the doc surfaces — and is the real F1 catcher. But this is *implicit*: the L0 leaf trigger as written would not fire for a 5-slice all-reversible feature. Recommend either (a) explicitly add to L0 trigger: "OR plan breaks into ≥2 independently verifiable slices" — which matches `pde-plan-review`'s actual behaviour — or (b) restate at the top of Part 4: "L0 always runs for multi-slice features, regardless of A4."
159	
160	**C-PM-2 — partial close, improved.** Line 135 now names a concrete `PreToolUse` hook (one script, exit 2 on out-of-scope file). This is meaningfully better than v1's hand-wave. But the hook is still framed as "stronger guard" and is in Part 5 (out-of-scope follow-ups) at line 265. The *primary* guard remains the written rule in the teammate brief — the same shape as the rule that already failed. Recommend the doc explicitly mark in the F-guard block: *"F2 prevention is rule-based until the optional PreToolUse hook is in place — track recurrence."*
161	
162	**C-PM-3 — closed.** Line 91 ships a concrete squint-prompt verbatim with the critical "you have not seen the acceptance criteria" instruction. Line 266 proposes the custom `squint-reviewer` subagent as a follow-up to remove per-cycle prompt-writing. Good fix.
163	
164	#### New concerns introduced in v2
165	
166	**C-PM-7 (MEDIUM) — L5 cost claim is uncalibrated and the leaf still recommends agent teams as default.** Line 138: "Agent teams are ≈ N+1× a single-session cost. Three chart teammates ≈ 4× a single session." Line 267 acknowledges this number isn't calibrated. For ABA-115 4/5/6, was wallclock actually binding? Three sequential single-session chart implementations would have been 3× a single session (no team overhead), versus 4× with the team — and at this cycle cadence (1 cycle / 1–2 weeks) the wallclock savings of doing them in parallel is probably a few hours, not days. The leaf needs a "when not to fan out" check: if the slices are small and sequential single-session would finish in the same operator-attention window, skip the team. Suggest a concrete heuristic in L5: *"Use agent teams when (a) each slice has ≥1 hour of implementation work AND (b) the operator wants the wallclock back. Otherwise, run them sequentially in L4 — same total token cost, simpler shape."*
167	
168	**C-PM-8 (MEDIUM) — Experimental flag is a fragile foundation for the load-bearing leaf.** Line 124 names `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1` as the activation mechanism. L5 is the central new primitive the recommendation introduces. If Anthropic changes or removes the flag mid-cycle, the user's planning ritual points at a non-existent primitive. Recommend the doc include a "fallback" line at L5: *"If agent teams are unavailable, demote L5 to N sequential L2/L3 single-sessions. The F-guards still apply; only the wallclock changes."* This is a one-line addition that makes the recommendation survive the experimental flag going away.
169	
170	**C-PM-9 (LOW) — `/batch` vs agent-teams branching in L6 is reasonable but the test is fuzzy.** Line 157: *"For the playbook queue specifically: Path A. Each playbook is a different ticker with different sell-side debate axes; the work is not mechanical."* Fine for the specific case. But the *general* test "is this mechanical?" is exactly the kind of subjective call the doc is otherwise good at avoiding. Recommend a sharper rule: *"If the per-unit input fits in <100 tokens (e.g. just a filename, a ticker, a regex), use `/batch`. If it needs ≥200 tokens of context (a playbook brief, a domain narrative), use agent teams."* A token-count test is mechanical; a "is this mechanical?" judgement is not.
171	
172	#### What pattern-map.md gets right (don't change)
173	
174	- The six axes are the right level of resolution. I tried to add a seventh (cognitive-load axis) and couldn't justify it.
175	- A3 (same-file overlap demotes parallelism) is the kind of constraint people forget — keep it.
176	- The "Not on the list" cull (line 30) is correct discipline; runtime and engineer-count are red herrings.
177	- The three worked examples make the document concrete in a way pure rules wouldn't. Keep all three.
178	- The acknowledgement at line 251 that F2 enforcement is a future hook candidate is honest — but the *current* claim that L4/L5 prevent F2 should be softened to match.
179

94	
95	Strong skeleton overall — six axes are well-chosen, the L1–L7 leaves are crisp, and the 60-second ritual is genuinely repeatable. Below are the holes I found that I think matter enough to surface.
96	
97	#### C-PM-1 (HIGH) — L0's trigger is wrong; it must fire on orchestration shape, not just one-way doors
98	
99	Per pattern-map line 43: *"Leaf L0 — One-way door of any kind. Trigger: A4 = yes."*
100	
101	This means L0 fires only when A4 (one-way door) is true. But the ABA-115 failure (F1, "supervisor spawns implementor") was *not* triggered by a one-way door per se — it was triggered by *the lead choosing an infeasible orchestration shape during planning.* If the same lead designs a "supervisor spawns N implementors" plan for a future feature that has *zero* one-way doors (say, five independent UI tweaks across five components, all reversible), nothing in the current decision tree fires L0 — the plan would route straight to L5 or L6, and the platform-invalid supervisor layer would never be reviewed.
102	
103	**The structural bug:** F1 is a deviation between the *planned shape* and the *platform's capabilities*. That bug is independent of one-way-doorness. L0's trigger must include something like:
104	
105	> **A7 (new): Does the plan name a "supervisor sub-agent," a "coordinator sub-agent that spawns workers," or any pattern where a sub-agent dispatches other sub-agents?** If yes → L0 plan review, regardless of A4.
106	
107	Or, more robustly: the 60-second ritual's *output line* should be a pattern leaf name (L1–L7) from this map — and any line that doesn't match a defined leaf goes through L0 by default. Right now the ritual lets the planner write a free-form orchestration plan into `plan.md`, which is exactly the surface where ABA-115's failure originated.

112

100	
101	**Why this is the F3 guard.** The reviewer must *not* see the original AC. The label-collision bug passed all five mechanical criteria. If the reviewer uses the same criteria, it passes again. The squint prompts in Part 3.5 are deliberately *different* checklists, designed to fail on the kinds of bugs mechanical AC misses.
102	
103	**F-guards:**
104	- F3 — guarded by the differently-shaped second pass.
105	- F1, F2 — N/A.
106	
107	---
108	
109	### Leaf L4 — Multi-slice with sequential dependency (chained gates)
110	**Trigger:** A1 = 2–N, A2 = sequential.
111	
112	**Examples this repo:** ABA-115 slices 1→2→3 (UI shell → SKILL schema change → re-run reports). ABA-132 slices 1→2→3 (calibration design → impl → verification).
113	
114	**Pattern:** **Single-session sequential, one sub-task per turn, explicit gate read at each boundary.**
115	
116	**Activation:** No fan-out, no team, no subagent layer between slices. After each sub-task lands, the *operator* reads the gate criterion at the end of that slice's plan.md section and confirms before kicking off the next slice. Use `agent-skills:build` per task.
117	
118	**Why agent-teams is wrong here.** The slices share state (schema, JSON output, file contents). Agent teams' file-locking is task-level not file-level, and the upstream slice's output is the downstream slice's input — there's no parallelism gain to amortise the +1× token cost.
119	
120	**F-guards:**
121	- F1 — N/A.
122	- F2 — **partially mitigated, not prevented.** *This is the F2-hot leaf.* The written rule (slice-N+1 finds a bug from slice N → separate commit attributed to slice N, before slice N+1 work resumes) is the same shape as the rule that already failed in ABA-138 → silently-folded-into-ABA-139. Add a **human commit gate**: before approving any commit in a multi-slice chain, the *operator* (not an agent) runs `git diff --stat` on the staged change, compares the file list against the current slice's "Files to touch" line in `plan.md`, and asks "is anything in this diff from a prior slice?" If yes — abort the commit, extract the prior-slice fix as a separate commit attributed to its originating ticket, then re-stage the current slice. This is 15 seconds of human-eyeball-on-the-diff per commit; it costs less than the next F2 incident. Full mechanical prevention requires the `PreToolUse` hook listed in Part 5 — deferred to the user.
123	- F3 — apply L3's second-pass review at the chain's end if any slice produced subjective output.
124	
125	---
126	
127	### Leaf L5 — Multi-slice, parallel-safe slices after a shared prefix
128	**Trigger:** A1 = 2–N, A2 = parallel after some shared prefix, A3 = different files.
129	
130	**Examples this repo:** ABA-115 slices 4/5/6 (three independent chart components, each `ui/src/components/charts/*.jsx`, all consuming the same JSON contract from Slice 3).
131	
132	**Pattern:** **Agent teams (the `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1` primitive) — top-level lead + N teammates, one slice per teammate.**
133	
134	**Activation:** From the top-level Claude Code session, spawn an agent team (3–5 teammates is the Anthropic-suggested sweet spot, matches the chart trio exactly). Lead writes the task list, each teammate claims one slice's task, all teammates report to the lead. **The lead must be a top-level session, not a subagent** — agent teams cannot nest.
135	
136	**Pre-flight checklist before fan-out** (lead runs this once before assigning tasks):
137	1. Confirm each slice touches a *different file set* (A3). If two slices touch the same file, demote that pair to L4 chain — agent teams' file-locking won't save you.
138	2. Confirm the shared upstream dependency has landed (the gate from L4 → L5 transition). The agent team should start *after* the chain ends, not in parallel with it.
139	3. Each slice gets its own commit. Restate the F2 rule in the lead's task assignment to each teammate.
140	
141	**F-guards:**
142	- F1 — caught at L0 plan review (now mandatory on A7 = yes and on any multi-slice plan).
143	- F2 — **partially mitigated, not prevented** — same caveat as L4. The "separate commit, escalate, do not silently fold" rule is written into each teammate's brief, but it's the same shape that already failed. Add the **lead commit gate**: when a teammate reports back with a commit ready to merge, the *lead* (the top-level operator, not a sub-agent) reads `git diff --stat` against that teammate's assigned "Files to touch" list before approving. Any file outside the list = abort the merge, extract the cross-slice fix as its own commit attributed to the originating ticket. Full mechanical prevention requires the `PreToolUse` hook in Part 5.
144	- F3 — at the end of the fan-out, the lead invokes L3's second-pass review (`/crit` with one of the canonical squint prompts in Part 3.5) across the *aggregated* output, not per-slice. The label-collision bug was visible at aggregated render, not in any single component.
145	
146	**Cost note.** Agent teams are ≈ N+1× a single-session cost. Three chart teammates ≈ 4× a single session. Justified when wallclock matters and the slices are genuinely independent; *not* justified for 2-slice fan-outs (L4 is cheaper).
147	
148	**When `/batch` is better than agent teams here.** If the parallel slices are truly *mechanical* (codemod-style — same instruction over many files), use the bundled `/batch` command instead. It auto-creates worktrees per unit and opens one PR per unit. The recent cycle's chart slices are *not* mechanical (each chart has bespoke data flow), so agent teams is the right fit.
149	
150	---
151	
152	### Leaf L6 — Queue of N file-isolated tickets sharing one template
153	**Trigger:** A1 = 4+, A2 = independent, A3 = each touches a different file.
154	
155	**Examples this repo:** ABA-120/121/122/123/124 (write playbooks: META, NVDA, AMZN, NFLX, ADYEN) — each touches `playbooks/TICKER.md`, format is locked, no inter-dependencies.
156	
157	**Pattern:** **Two viable paths:**
158	
159	**Path A — Agent teams** (recommended for creative work like playbooks, where each unit needs judgement).
160	- Same activation as L5: top-level lead + N teammates, one ticket per teammate. The template prompt is identical across teammates; vary only the ticker.
161	
162	**Path B — `/batch`** (for mechanical N-unit refactors that share one instruction).
163	- `/batch "rewrite all imports of X to Y"` style. Each unit gets its own worktree and PR. Best when the work is truly identical across units.
164	
165	**For the playbook queue specifically: Path A.** Each playbook is a different ticker with different sell-side debate axes; the work is not mechanical.
166	
167	**F-guards:**
168	- F1, F2 — same as L5.
169	- F3 — apply *once across the batch*, not per-playbook. Cross-playbook vocabulary drift and narrative inconsistency is invisible in any single playbook. One reviewer reads all N back-to-back.
170	
171	---
172	
173	### Leaf L7 — Mixed: feature with a shared prefix and a fan-out tail (composition of L4 + L5)
174	**Trigger:** ABA-115-shaped: chain → fan-out → close-out.
175	
176	**Pattern:** **Compose L4 and L5 explicitly in the plan. Write the orchestration plan as "Slices 1–3 chain (L4); Slices 4–6 fan-out (L5); Slice 7 chain (L4)." Each phase uses its own primitive.**
177	
178	**Activation:** Don't try to make a single orchestration primitive cover all of it. The phase transitions are where F1 and F2 surface most often — pause between phases, confirm gates, then choose the next primitive.
179	
180	**Phase-transition checklist** (run at *every* L4→L5, L5→L4, L4→L3, L5→L3 boundary — this is the procedure, not just a label):
181	1. **Gate read.** Open the prior phase's last slice in `plan.md`; read its acceptance criteria aloud. Each one met? If no — pause; do not transition.
182	2. **Cross-slice diff scan.** `git log <prior-phase-start>..HEAD --stat`. Did any commit touch files attributed to a slice in a *different* phase? If yes — that's an F2 hit; extract those changes into a separately-attributed commit before continuing.
183	3. **Next-phase pattern restated.** Say the next phase's leaf name (L4, L5, L6, or L3) out loud. If the answer is "I'm not sure" — go back to Part 4 ritual step 6 and re-derive from axes.
184	4. **Dispatch.** Only now spawn the next phase's primitive.
185	
186	This is ~90 seconds at each transition. It's the difference between L7 being a procedure and L7 being a sticker on the plan that means nothing in execution.
187	
188	**This is the most common shape this repo will produce** given the multi-stage skill architecture (UI ↔ SKILL ↔ reports). Plan must *name the phase transitions* so the operator knows when to switch primitives — and run the checklist above at each one.
189	
190	---
191	
192	## Part 3 — Three worked examples from this repo
193	
194	### Example 1: ABA-115 — Model tab v1 (L7 composition: L0 → L4 → L5 → L4 → L3)
195	**Sub-tasks (7 slices, see `tasks/plan.md`):**
196	1. UI shell (no SKILL change)
197	2. SKILL schema: emit `historical_fcf_margins[]`
198	3. Re-run covered tickers
199	4. CAGR glidepath chart
200	5. FCF margin trajectory chart
201	6. Scenario fan chart
202	7. Captions / styling / docs
203	
204	**Axis read:**
205	- A1: 7 slices.
206	- A2: 1→2→3 sequential; 4/5/6 parallel-safe; 7 final.
207	- A3: 4/5/6 each touch their own file (`charts/*.jsx`).
208	- A4: yes — Slice 2 changes a JSON contract (`historical_fcf_margins[]`).
209	- A5: slices 1–3 mechanical (JSON field present, audit row matches); slices 4–7 subjective (chart legibility).
210	- A6: implementation.
211	
212	**Pattern: L0 (`/pde-plan-review`) → L4 single-session chain (1→2→3) → L5 agent team of 3 teammates (4‖5‖6) → L4 single-session (7) → L3 `/crit` squint-test pass across the aggregated UI.**
213	
214	**What actually went wrong** (per `critique.md`): the lead picked "supervisor sub-agent + implementor sub-agents" — platform-invalid because subagents can't nest. L0 would have caught this at planning time and reshaped to top-level fan-out. Instead, F1 fired (the supervisor improvised inline, didn't escalate), F2 fired (a NaN bug from slice 4 was folded into slice 5's commit instead of attributed back as ABA-143/144), F3 fired (Y5 label collision shipped because AC was mechanical).
215	
216	**What L0→L5→L3 would have changed:**
217	- L0 catches "supervisor spawns implementor" at plan review → reshapes to the lead-led agent team.
218	- L5's separate-commit rule + the optional `PreToolUse` git hook prevents F2.
219	- L3's `/crit` with explicit squint-test prompt catches F3 before merge.
220	
221	---
222	
223	### Example 2: ABA-104 — Base-year-effect spike (L1)
224	**Sub-tasks (1):** Investigate the failure mode, recommend detection tripwire + response strategy + scope, file follow-up.
225	
226	**Axis read:**
227	- A1: 1.
228	- A6: spike (recommendation-only).
229	
230	**Pattern: L1 — single-session + `pde-backend-spike`.**
231	
232	This is the case where adding sub-agents or a team is *worse* than running solo. The deliverable is reasoning, not code, and reasoning fragments badly across context boundaries.
233	
234	**Outcome (already shipped):** Done; recommendation produced an implementation follow-up (ABA-134). No F1/F2/F3 exposure because no orchestration layer existed.
235	
236	---
237	
238	### Example 3: ABA-120/121/122/123/124 — Five playbook writes (L6, Path A)
239	**Sub-tasks (5):** One playbook per remaining covered ticker (META, NVDA, AMZN, NFLX, ADYEN.AS).
240	
241	**Axis read:**
242	- A1: 5.
243	- A2: independent.
244	- A3: each touches its own `playbooks/TICKER.md`.
245	- A4: the *first* playbook established the template (ABA-117/116 already shipped). Subsequent five are not one-way.
246	- A5: subjective (narrative quality, sell-side argument balance).
247	- A6: implementation (creative writing).
248	
249	**Pattern: L6 Path A — agent team, 5 teammates, one ticker each, lead aggregates.**
250	
251	Why not `/batch`: each playbook is *not* mechanical. Each ticker has a different debate (AI search disruption for GOOG, EUV monopoly for ASML, etc.). `/batch` would produce structurally identical playbooks; agent teams produces ticker-thesis-aware playbooks.
252	
253	**F-guard for this case:** L3 cross-playbook second pass. One reviewer reads all five back-to-back to catch vocabulary drift and narrative-style inconsistency. *Not* one reviewer per playbook — the failure mode is between playbooks, invisible inside one.
254	
255	---
256	
257	## Part 3.5 — Canonical squint prompts (use these verbatim; do not write fresh)
258	
259	L3's second-pass review is only as good as the prompt it runs. The recent cycle's Y5 label collision happened because the AC was mechanical; an L3 reviewer given a generic "review for legibility" prompt would have reproduced the same blind spot. These prompts are pre-written so the planner can pick one in five seconds at planning time, not draft one under time pressure.
260	
261	**Rules of use:**
262	- Pick the prompt that matches the output type. Don't mix.
263	- The reviewer must *not* see the original acceptance criteria. Send the squint prompt and the rendered artefact only.
264	- Run after the implementor reports green on mechanical AC, *before* merge.
265	
266	---
267	
268	**Squint prompt — CHARTS / visualisations** (the F3-original failure mode)
269	
270	> "You have not seen the acceptance criteria for this chart. Open the rendered chart at its canonical viewport size (the size a reader would actually see — assume desktop browser unless told otherwise).
271	>
272	> Answer these questions, in order. Do not skip any.
273	>
274	> 1. **One-sentence thesis test.** Without reading the underlying data or any caption, can you state in one sentence what this chart is arguing? If no — what's confusing?
275	> 2. **Label legibility.** Are any labels overlapping, clipped at chart edges, or unreadable at the rendered size? Look in particular at: axis tick labels, scenario/series endpoints (right edge, top edge), data-point callouts, and any annotation that points at a specific location on the chart.
276	> 3. **Axis sanity.** Does the y-axis range make sense — or is one extreme outlier blowing out the scale and flattening the rest? Does the x-axis tick density match the rendered width (too dense = ticks collide; too sparse = unclear time scale)?
277	> 4. **Colour and ordering.** If there's an implied ordering (bear < base < bull, low to high, etc.), does the visual ordering match? Do any two series share enough colour/style that a reader could swap them?
278	> 5. **Edge cases.** What happens at the chart's edges — left edge (start of series), right edge (most-recent or projected-Y5), top edge (chart maximum)? List anything that looks wrong, ugly, overlapping, or unclear at the edges specifically. Edges are where label collisions hide.
279	> 6. **What's missing.** Is there a current-state reference line, a callout for a fired condition (e.g. a growth cap), or a unit/scale indicator that a reader needs and isn't there?
280	>
281	> Name every issue, even small ones. Do not stop at the first issue — list them all. An issue that 'technically passes the spec' but produces a worse reader experience is in-scope for this review."
282	
283	This prompt would have caught the Y5 endpoint-label collision (question 5) and the AMZN negative-fcf y-axis blowout (question 3).
284	
285	---
286	
287	**Squint prompt — NARRATIVE OUTPUT** (playbooks, methodology notes, signal/model rationale prose)
288	
289	> "You have not seen the acceptance criteria for this document. Read it once at normal speed — do not study it.
290	>
291	> Answer these questions in order:
292	>
293	> 1. **One-paragraph thesis test.** In one paragraph, what is this document arguing? If you can't state the thesis after one read-through, the document is unclear.
294	> 2. **Vocabulary consistency.** Are the same concepts named with the same words throughout? List any term that drifts (e.g. 'segment' in one section, 'business line' in another, 'vertical' in a third — three names for the same thing is a smell).
295	> 3. **Confident-but-unsupported claims.** Are there assertions that a sceptical reader would push back on — and is the supporting evidence in the document? Quote one such claim verbatim if you find one.
296	> 4. **Audience leakage.** Is this written for the same reader throughout? If sections switch register (technical → marketing → internal-jargon), name the shifts.
297	> 5. **Structural mismatch.** Does the document's section ordering match what a reader looking for the answer would expect? Or do they have to hunt?
298	> 6. **Comparison to siblings (only if applicable — multi-document review).** If you are reviewing this alongside sibling documents (e.g. multiple playbooks), what's *different* between them in style, depth, structure, or vocabulary that isn't justified by the subject matter?
299	>
300	> Name every issue. The goal is to find the gap between 'technically correct' and 'a reader would actually use this'."
301	
302	This prompt is the right shape for the L6 cross-playbook second pass — question 6 is specifically the cross-doc vocabulary-drift check.
303	
304	---
305	
306	**Squint prompt — TABULAR / STRUCTURED REPORT OUTPUT** (Signal/Model/Screen JSON, the report viewer's tabs)
307	
308	> "You have not seen the acceptance criteria for this report or JSON output. Open the rendered view (UI tab if applicable, or the JSON itself).
309	>
310	> Answer in order:
311	>
312	> 1. **At-a-glance verdict.** What is this report *telling* a reader? State the verdict and primary number in one sentence. If you have to read three sections to know, the report is buried.
313	> 2. **Number sanity.** For every numeric field: does the magnitude look right (units in the right place — billions vs millions, percent vs decimal)? Does any number conflict with another (e.g. P/E and PEG implying different growth rates without explanation)?
314	> 3. **Missing fields, silent.** Are there fields you'd expect for this kind of report that are absent — and is the absence acknowledged (e.g. 'N/A — pre-profit') or just silently missing?
315	> 4. **Audit trail.** If overrides, caps, or substitutions were applied, are they named — or did some assumption silently swap in?
316	> 5. **Reader's next question.** What's the most likely next question a reader has after seeing this? Is the answer in the report?
317	>
318	> Name every issue you find."
319	
320	---
321	
322	**How to pick.** A5 = subjective with a chart artefact → use the charts prompt. A5 = subjective with prose artefact → narrative prompt. A5 = subjective with a structured report → tabular prompt. If the artefact is mixed (e.g. ABA-115 Model tab has charts + summary text), run the charts prompt for the chart components and the tabular prompt for the summary strip — they take ~2 minutes each.
323	
324	---
325	
326	## Part 4 — The cycle-planning ritual
327	
328	A six-question checklist applied to each parent ticket during planning. Output is the orchestration plan, written one line into the parent's `plan.md`.
329	
330	```
331	1. Count sub-tasks (A1). One → L1 / L2 / L3 by axes A5/A6. Several → continue.
332	2. Draw the dependency graph (A2). All arrows? L4. No arrows after a prefix? L5/L7. Pure independent → L6.
333	3. Highlight same-file overlaps (A3). Any overlap → serialize that pair regardless of step 2 (or move to `/batch` if truly mechanical).
334	4. Mark one-way doors (A4). Any → run `/pde-plan-review` (L0) before kicking off any sub-task implementation.
335	5. Mark subjective acceptance (A5). Any → schedule L3 second pass (`/crit` with squint-test prompt) after implementation. Write the squint-test prompt now, not at review time.
336	6. Write the one-line orchestration plan into `plan.md`, e.g.: "L0 → L4(1-3) → L5 team-of-3(4‖5‖6) → L4(7) → L3 squint pass."
337	```
338	
339	**Add to the bottom of `plan.md` for every multi-slice ticket:**
340	- The orchestration plan from step 6 above.
341	- The squint-test prompt (if step 5 fired) — pre-written, ready for the reviewer.
342	- The F2 rule restated verbatim: *"If an agent finds a bug from a prior slice, commit the fix as a separate commit attributed to the originating ticket before continuing. Do not silently fold."*
343	
344	**Total time: ≈ 60 seconds per parent ticket** once the axes are memorised.
345	
346	---
347	
348	## Part 5 — Open questions / follow-ups (out of scope for this map)
349	
350	- **F2 enforcement hook.** Currently F2 is a rule in the teammate brief. A `PreToolUse` hook on `git commit` that compares the staged diff against the teammate's "Files to touch" list and exits 2 on out-of-scope edits would mechanically prevent the failure. One small script, one config entry. Worth one cycle of setup if F2 recurs.
351	- **Custom `squint-reviewer` subagent.** L3's second pass currently uses `/crit`. A custom `.claude/agents/squint-reviewer.md` with the squint-test prompt baked into the system prompt would be a one-time cost that pays back across every L3 leaf — and removes the risk of the operator forgetting to pre-write the prompt at planning time.
352	- **Agent-teams cost calibration.** `landscape.md` flags that Anthropic hasn't published per-pattern token numbers. After the next L5 fan-out, log actual token consumption and compare against the "≈ N+1×" rough mental model. If costs are 2× the estimate, the L5 threshold for "is parallelism worth it?" needs revisiting.
353	- **Channels for async hand-off** between phase transitions in L7. Currently the operator is the courier between L4 and L5 phases. A channel that pushes "L4 done; gate criterion met" into the agent-team lead's session might tighten the L7 hand-off, but it adds dependency on a research-preview feature — defer unless the L7 transition becomes a recurring drag.
354	
```
