1	# Critique — stress-test of orchestration recommendations
2	
3	Stress-testing `landscape.md` (landscape-researcher) and `pattern-map.md` (pattern-mapper) against the user's actual constraints:
4	
5	- **Cadence:** ~one cycle every 1–2 weeks. Any recommendation that requires multi-hour setup amortised across many cycles is suspect.
6	- **Platform reality (Claude Code):** sub-agents cannot spawn sub-agents. Only the top-level session can fan out. Anything that implies "supervisor sub-agent dispatches workers" is platform-invalid.
7	- **Repeatability bar:** the user wants something they can apply during cycle planning without thinking hard each time. Patterns that need fresh judgement per cycle don't clear the bar.
8	
9	## Reference failure modes the recommendation MUST prevent
10	
11	These are the three failures from the just-finished cycle. Any recommendation that doesn't prevent all three is a partial solution and should be marked as such.
12	
13	### F1. Architectural deviation, silent
14	The lead designed a "supervisor sub-agent that spawns implementor sub-agents." Claude Code doesn't support that — sub-agents can't spawn sub-agents. The supervisor improvised inline (collapsed two layers into one) and didn't escalate. User learned of the deviation only in the final report.
15	
16	**What prevents this:** a check at *plan-approval time* that the architecture is platform-feasible — and a stop-the-line rule that any sub-agent which cannot execute the assigned shape must escalate before improvising. A recommendation that only governs implementation but not plan shape doesn't catch F1.
17	
18	### F2. Latent bug folded silently into wrong commit
19	A NaN SVG crash caused by ticket 1's code was caught during ticket 2's verification and folded into ticket 2's commit. The bug→fix mapping is gone from git history.
20	
21	**What prevents this:** a discipline that says "a bug discovered during work on ticket N that originated in ticket M produces a *separate* commit (and ideally a Linear issue or note) attributed to M." A reviewer sub-agent that "checks the diff" won't catch this — by the time they review, the merge has already obscured the origin.
22	
23	### F3. User caught what the agent missed (Y5 label collision)
24	A visible Y5-axis label-collision bug in the chart was spotted by the user. The acceptance criteria were mechanical (monotone, cap callout, pre-profit guard, three lines) — they didn't ask "is the chart legible?"
25	
26	**What prevents this:** acceptance criteria that include a *subjective render check* on visual output — e.g. "screenshot at canonical size; does it pass the squint test." A reviewer sub-agent that uses the same mechanical criteria as the implementor will miss it too — the failure is in the criteria, not the reviewer.
27	
28	## Holes I'm watching for
29	
30	(populated as drafts land)
31	
32	- "Add a reviewer sub-agent" without specifying *what the reviewer checks that the implementor doesn't*. If the reviewer applies the same criteria, F3 reproduces.
33	- "Coordinator sub-agent spawns workers." Platform-invalid (F1 again).
34	- "Each agent owns one ticket." Doesn't prevent F2 unless there's an explicit rule about cross-ticket bug attribution.
35	- Hooks the user has to author per cycle. Setup cost > benefit at this cadence.
36	- Custom skills, MCP servers, or Agent SDK builds that take >1 cycle of setup to amortise.
37	- "Plan review" steps that produce documents nobody reads — the lead/user already retros after the fact; what's missing is a check *before* the work starts.
38	- Recommendations that describe an ideal end state without a "first thing the user does next cycle" entrypoint.
39	
40	## Per-draft critique
41	
42	### landscape.md
43	
44	Solid catalogue overall — the four platform constraints at the top (especially #1 sub-agent recursion and #2 agent-team teammate non-spawning) directly inoculate against F1, and the table is consistent. The "Honest gaps" section is genuinely honest. Below are the holes that matter for the eventual user-facing recommendation.
45	
46	#### C-LS-1 (HIGH) — The June 15 2026 SDK billing change is buried and is *the* relevant decision-cost trigger for the user *this* cycle
47	
48	Line 64 mentions: *"from June 15, 2026, subscription-plan Agent SDK and `claude -p` usage draws from a separate monthly Agent SDK credit pool, not the same interactive limits as Claude Code itself."*
49	
50	Today is 2026-05-19. That's 27 days away. Any recommendation that suggests "build an Agent SDK orchestrator" *this cycle* is about to walk into a billing-model change the user hasn't budgeted for. The pattern-map (correctly) doesn't recommend SDK for any L0–L7 leaf — but landscape.md doesn't escalate this enough. It reads like an interesting fact, not a planning constraint.
51	
52	Recommend: lift the billing date into the platform-constraints block at the top (alongside the "no nested subagents" constraint), framed as: *"5. Agent SDK billing changes June 15, 2026 — if you're picking a pattern that uses the SDK at all, this is a TCO decision, not just an interface decision."* This is the exact category of fact the user needs at planning time.
53	
54	#### C-LS-2 (HIGH) — "Setup friction: Low" is doing too much work; several "Low"-tagged rows hide multi-hour first-time setup
55	
56	Several rows are tagged "Low" friction in ways that aren't honest at the user's cycle cadence:
57	
58	- **Channels (line 30):** "Medium — Bun, plugin install, sender allowlist, org enablement on Team/Enterprise." Read the last clause: *org enablement on Team/Enterprise.* If the user isn't on Team/Enterprise (the email is personal `@gmail.com` per the env), channels are *unavailable*, not Medium. This should be a hard gate, not a friction tier.
59	- **Custom skills (line 25):** "Low — one folder per skill." True for the artefact, but `SKILL.md` skills compete for the 25k-token description budget (line 58), and the doc itself notes this. "Low setup, ongoing description-budget tax" is the honest framing.
60	- **MCP servers (line 29):** "Low–medium per server (config + secrets)." Per server, sure. But the doc doesn't note that adding an MCP server is *also* a tool-description-budget tax on every session in that scope. The honest framing for a user at this cadence: each MCP server is a permanent context-budget cost the moment it's enabled.
61	- **Custom subagents (line 21):** "Low — one markdown file with frontmatter." True. But the doc doesn't note that custom subagents that reference `skills:` and `mcpServers:` behave *differently* as teammates (those fields silently drop per platform constraint #4, line 12). The cost of this isn't setup — it's debugging time when the same role behaves differently in two contexts.
62	
63	Recommend: split "Setup friction" into two columns — *first-time setup* (one-off) and *ongoing context-budget tax* (recurring). At the user's cycle cadence, recurring is the more important number.
64	
65	#### C-LS-3 (MEDIUM) — v2.1.32+ version requirement on agent teams is unsourced and load-bearing
66	
67	Line 22: *"Medium — env var `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1`, requires v2.1.32+."*
68	
69	Agent teams is the central new primitive the pattern-map recommends (L5, L6 Path A). If the user's Claude Code is older than v2.1.32, the entire L5 leaf doesn't work and silently falls back. The doc lists 17 sources at the bottom but I cannot tell which one(s) actually verified that version number. This is exactly the agentic-engineering "hallucination is the default" failure mode — a specific version number that *might* be plausible interpolation rather than fact.
70	
71	Recommend: either (a) add an inline source link for the version requirement, or (b) soften to "requires a recent Claude Code version that has the experimental flag — verify with `claude --version` before planning around L5."
72	
73	#### C-LS-4 (MEDIUM) — Cost column is acknowledged as "relative ordering, not measurement" but the pattern-map treats it as measurement
74	
75	The "Honest gaps" section at line 86 says: *"Treat the cost column as relative ordering, not measurement."* But the pattern-map (and the synthesis the lead will eventually write) reads "≈ N+1× per teammate" as a number it can plan against. The two docs need to agree on this. Either landscape.md gets concrete numbers (it can't — Anthropic hasn't published them), or pattern-map.md needs to stop quoting "≈ N+1×" as a planning fact.
76	
77	This isn't landscape.md's bug — it's a synthesis bug between the two docs. Flagging here so the lead handles it. The number to remove is in pattern-map line 138 and line 267.
78	
79	#### C-LS-5 (LOW) — Third-party tools (Squad, opencode, Aider, Roo, tmux orchestrators) take 6 rows but are noise at the user's cadence
80	
81	Rows for Claude Squad, opencode, Aider, Roo Code, and "Other tmux orchestrators" each represent a separate third-party tool to learn. At one cycle every 1–2 weeks, picking up a new third-party tool is itself a cycle's worth of cost. None of these survive a "would I start this next cycle?" filter.
82	
83	Recommend: in a separate "third-party orbiters (do not adopt without standalone justification)" section, collapse the five rows into one paragraph noting their existence and the conditions under which they'd be worth re-evaluating (e.g. "if Claude Code adds a hard limitation you can't work around" / "if you want to mix providers"). This frees the catalogue to focus on the patterns the user might actually choose.
84	
85	#### What landscape.md gets right (don't change)
86	
87	- Platform constraints block at the top is excellent — exactly the right discipline. Constraint #1 (no sub-agent recursion) and #4 (teammate frontmatter drops skills/mcpServers) are the two things that *will* burn the user if they don't know them.
88	- "Honest gaps" section is real (line 84). Don't sand it down. The fact that the doc names uncertainty about `/batch`'s actual availability (line 88) is the right register.
89	- Splitting "Hooks (enforcement)" from "Hooks (orchestration glue)" is the right call — they're the same primitive used for very different things, and most landscape docs conflate them.
90	- The "Best for / Worst for" columns on each row are genuinely useful — they're what the pattern-map ended up using. Keep.
91	- The deeper notes per pattern (lines 46–80) are where the real signal is. The table is the index; the notes are the document.
92	
93	---

125	### Net verdict
126	
127	Both documents are in good shape. All HIGH concerns closed. MEDIUM concerns closed or honestly downgraded. The two LOW items still open (C-PM-9 mechanical-test wording, C-LS-5 third-party rows) are non-load-bearing.
128	
129	The MUST 1–3 items in my message to team-lead remain the load-bearing surfacings. The teammates' revisions reinforce all three rather than undermine them — pattern-map.md v3 makes MUST 1 and MUST 2 *easier* to surface in the final recommendation by giving them explicit hooks in the document (the "partially mitigated" framing on L4/L5 and the A7 axis + L0 always-on-multi-slice rule).
130	
131	No remaining holes worth blocking on. Devil's advocate role complete.
132	
133	### pattern-map.md
134	
135	Strong skeleton overall — six axes are well-chosen, the L1–L7 leaves are crisp, and the 60-second ritual is genuinely repeatable. Below are the holes I found that I think matter enough to surface.
136	
137	#### C-PM-1 (HIGH) — L0's trigger is wrong; it must fire on orchestration shape, not just one-way doors
138	
139	Per pattern-map line 43: *"Leaf L0 — One-way door of any kind. Trigger: A4 = yes."*
140	
141	This means L0 fires only when A4 (one-way door) is true. But the ABA-115 failure (F1, "supervisor spawns implementor") was *not* triggered by a one-way door per se — it was triggered by *the lead choosing an infeasible orchestration shape during planning.* If the same lead designs a "supervisor spawns N implementors" plan for a future feature that has *zero* one-way doors (say, five independent UI tweaks across five components, all reversible), nothing in the current decision tree fires L0 — the plan would route straight to L5 or L6, and the platform-invalid supervisor layer would never be reviewed.
142	
143	**The structural bug:** F1 is a deviation between the *planned shape* and the *platform's capabilities*. That bug is independent of one-way-doorness. L0's trigger must include something like:
144	
145	> **A7 (new): Does the plan name a "supervisor sub-agent," a "coordinator sub-agent that spawns workers," or any pattern where a sub-agent dispatches other sub-agents?** If yes → L0 plan review, regardless of A4.
146	
147	Or, more robustly: the 60-second ritual's *output line* should be a pattern leaf name (L1–L7) from this map — and any line that doesn't match a defined leaf goes through L0 by default. Right now the ritual lets the planner write a free-form orchestration plan into `plan.md`, which is exactly the surface where ABA-115's failure originated.
148	
149	#### C-PM-2 (HIGH) — F2's "separate commit" rule is unenforced and depends on the same agent that violated it
150	
151	Pattern-map line 109 (L4) and line 126 (L5) carry the F2 rule. Line 251 acknowledges:
152	
153	> "F2 enforcement. Currently a *rule* the agent must follow. A hook ... could mechanically refuse to commit a diff that touches files outside the assigned slice's 'Files to touch' list. Out of scope here — flag to user as a future hook candidate."
154	
155	This is the most important failure mode in the recent cycle (it destroyed bug→fix provenance in git history) — and the proposed guard is "the agent should follow a rule we wrote down." That's exactly the discipline that *already failed* in ABA-138. The agent that folded the NaN fix into the wrong commit had access to the same rules; what was missing was a mechanical check.
156	
157	I don't think the user will write the hook on cycle one. But the recommendation needs to either (a) acknowledge that F2 is **partially mitigated, not prevented** at the recommendation level, or (b) propose a lighter-weight mechanism than a hook — e.g. *"the lead must read `git diff` against the assigned 'Files to touch' list before approving the commit"* — which is a human gate, not an agent rule.
158	
159	The current text implies F2 is solved at L4/L5. It isn't.
160	
161	#### C-PM-3 (HIGH) — L3's "differently-shaped second pass" only works if the planner wrote the squint prompt; planner exhaustion is the obvious failure mode
162	
163	Line 91: *"The second pass MUST NOT re-apply the mechanical acceptance criteria."*
164	Line 240: *"the squint-test prompt (if step 5 fired) — pre-written, so the L3 reviewer has it ready."*
165	
166	Two problems:
167	
168	1. **The squint prompt is the load-bearing part of L3.** If the planner is tired and writes a generic "review this for legibility" prompt — or worse, copy-pastes the original AC — the L3 reviewer reproduces the F3 blind spot exactly. The pattern moves the failure from implementor to reviewer; it doesn't eliminate it.
169	
170	2. **There's no library of squint prompts.** The user is expected to write a tailored prompt per cycle. That's a non-trivial cognitive load — exactly the thing the document promises to avoid in the opening paragraph ("no fresh judgement per cycle"). At minimum, the pattern map should ship with 3–4 canonical squint prompts (one for charts, one for narrative output, one for tabular reports) the planner can pick from.
171	
172	Concrete fix: add a section "**Canonical squint prompts**" with pre-written language for the most common subjective output types in this repo. For charts: *"Open the rendered chart at canonical viewport size. Without reading the underlying data, can you state the thesis in one sentence? Are any labels overlapping, clipped, or unreadable? Does any axis tick appear to collide with a callout?"* That's the specific shape the Y5 collision would have failed.
173	
174	#### C-PM-4 (MEDIUM) — L5's pre-flight checklist is unenforced and lives in the wrong place
175	
176	Line 123–127: L5's pre-flight checklist (different files, dependency landed, separate commits) is run by *who*, exactly? If it's the lead before fan-out, fine — but the document doesn't say. If it's the implementor agents, they have an incentive not to flag conflicts (it's faster to proceed). And the checklist sits *inside* the L5 leaf, meaning it gets re-discovered on every fan-out instead of being a ritual the user has memorised.
177	
178	Recommend: move the pre-flight to Part 4 (the planning ritual) — make it part of step 6, so the user runs it once at planning time, not at fan-out time. The discipline is "plan the parallelism, then execute," not "decide to parallelise as you go."
179	
180	#### C-PM-5 (MEDIUM) — L7 (compositions) is where deviation creeps in, and the doc acknowledges this without addressing it
181	
182	Line 156: *"This is the most common shape this pack will produce ... Plan should *name the phase transitions* — they're the gate moments where F2 (latent bug) is most likely to surface."*
183	
184	True. But what does the agent *do* at a phase transition? Right now L7 says "compose L4 and L5 explicitly in the plan." That's not a procedure — it's a label. Concrete fix: L7 should include a phase-transition checklist:
185	
186	> At each L4→L5 or L5→L4 transition: (a) confirm prior phase's gate criterion is met (read the AC back); (b) check git log: did any commit in the prior phase touch files attributed to a *different* slice? (F2 detector); (c) re-state the next phase's pattern name; (d) only then dispatch.
187	
188	Without this, L7 is decorative — and L7 is *also* the shape that produced the recent failure.
189	
190	#### C-PM-6 (LOW) — A4's one-way door list is incomplete
191	
192	Line 25 lists DESIGN.md, JSON contract, `playbooks/*.md` frontmatter, SKILL.md schema, public command name. Missing: **the orchestration choice itself for a multi-slice feature.** Once you've decided "this is L5 fan-out," walking back to L4 sequential mid-feature is expensive (already-spawned agents have state). The recommendation: A4 should include "orchestration shape for features with ≥4 slices" — these warrant L0 review at planning, period.
193	
194	This overlaps with C-PM-1 — and is probably best solved together by routing *all* multi-slice plans through L0 by default.
195	
196	#### v2 review — what closed, what didn't, what's new
197	
198	**C-PM-1 — still open (partial close).** L0's trigger is still `A4 = yes` only (line 42). The A7 axis I proposed isn't there. The doc instead leans on `pde-plan-review`'s own auto-fire condition ("any plan with a one-way-door decision, or that breaks into more than a single independently verifiable slice"). That second clause is broader than the doc surfaces — and is the real F1 catcher. But this is *implicit*: the L0 leaf trigger as written would not fire for a 5-slice all-reversible feature. Recommend either (a) explicitly add to L0 trigger: "OR plan breaks into ≥2 independently verifiable slices" — which matches `pde-plan-review`'s actual behaviour — or (b) restate at the top of Part 4: "L0 always runs for multi-slice features, regardless of A4."
199	
200	**C-PM-2 — partial close, improved.** Line 135 now names a concrete `PreToolUse` hook (one script, exit 2 on out-of-scope file). This is meaningfully better than v1's hand-wave. But the hook is still framed as "stronger guard" and is in Part 5 (out-of-scope follow-ups) at line 265. The *primary* guard remains the written rule in the teammate brief — the same shape as the rule that already failed. Recommend the doc explicitly mark in the F-guard block: *"F2 prevention is rule-based until the optional PreToolUse hook is in place — track recurrence."*
201	
202	**C-PM-3 — closed.** Line 91 ships a concrete squint-prompt verbatim with the critical "you have not seen the acceptance criteria" instruction. Line 266 proposes the custom `squint-reviewer` subagent as a follow-up to remove per-cycle prompt-writing. Good fix.
203	
204	#### New concerns introduced in v2
205	
206	**C-PM-7 (MEDIUM) — L5 cost claim is uncalibrated and the leaf still recommends agent teams as default.** Line 138: "Agent teams are ≈ N+1× a single-session cost. Three chart teammates ≈ 4× a single session." Line 267 acknowledges this number isn't calibrated. For ABA-115 4/5/6, was wallclock actually binding? Three sequential single-session chart implementations would have been 3× a single session (no team overhead), versus 4× with the team — and at this cycle cadence (1 cycle / 1–2 weeks) the wallclock savings of doing them in parallel is probably a few hours, not days. The leaf needs a "when not to fan out" check: if the slices are small and sequential single-session would finish in the same operator-attention window, skip the team. Suggest a concrete heuristic in L5: *"Use agent teams when (a) each slice has ≥1 hour of implementation work AND (b) the operator wants the wallclock back. Otherwise, run them sequentially in L4 — same total token cost, simpler shape."*
207	
208	**C-PM-8 (MEDIUM) — Experimental flag is a fragile foundation for the load-bearing leaf.** Line 124 names `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1` as the activation mechanism. L5 is the central new primitive the recommendation introduces. If Anthropic changes or removes the flag mid-cycle, the user's planning ritual points at a non-existent primitive. Recommend the doc include a "fallback" line at L5: *"If agent teams are unavailable, demote L5 to N sequential L2/L3 single-sessions. The F-guards still apply; only the wallclock changes."* This is a one-line addition that makes the recommendation survive the experimental flag going away.
209	
210	**C-PM-9 (LOW) — `/batch` vs agent-teams branching in L6 is reasonable but the test is fuzzy.** Line 157: *"For the playbook queue specifically: Path A. Each playbook is a different ticker with different sell-side debate axes; the work is not mechanical."* Fine for the specific case. But the *general* test "is this mechanical?" is exactly the kind of subjective call the doc is otherwise good at avoiding. Recommend a sharper rule: *"If the per-unit input fits in <100 tokens (e.g. just a filename, a ticker, a regex), use `/batch`. If it needs ≥200 tokens of context (a playbook brief, a domain narrative), use agent teams."* A token-count test is mechanical; a "is this mechanical?" judgement is not.
211	
212	#### What pattern-map.md gets right (don't change)
213	
214	- The six axes are the right level of resolution. I tried to add a seventh (cognitive-load axis) and couldn't justify it.
215	- A3 (same-file overlap demotes parallelism) is the kind of constraint people forget — keep it.
216	- The "Not on the list" cull (line 30) is correct discipline; runtime and engineer-count are red herrings.
217	- The three worked examples make the document concrete in a way pure rules wouldn't. Keep all three.
218	- The acknowledgement at line 251 that F2 enforcement is a future hook candidate is honest — but the *current* claim that L4/L5 prevent F2 should be softened to match.

1	# Pattern map — task shapes → orchestration patterns
2	
3	**Purpose.** Once a feature is broken into sub-tasks during cycle planning, the *shape* of that breakdown should imply the orchestration approach — no fresh judgement per cycle. This file maps shape to pattern so the user can run a 60-second checklist at planning time and walk away with an execution plan.
4	
5	**Companion docs.**
6	- `landscape.md` (landscape-researcher) — full catalogue of orchestration primitives available in Claude Code.
7	- `critique.md` (devils-advocate) — hard constraints + failure modes (F1/F2/F3) any recommendation must prevent.
8	
9	**Hard platform constraints baked in** (from `critique.md` and `landscape.md`):
10	- **No sub-agent recursion.** Subagents (Agent tool, custom `.claude/agents/`) cannot spawn agent teams. Agent teams cannot nest. Only the top-level session — or, equivalently, the agent-team lead — can fan out.
11	- **Agent teams are expensive** (≈ N+1× per-session token cost) and *experimental*; use them where the parallelism benefit clears the cost, not by default.
12	- **Same-file parallel work is unsafe** in agent teams: teammates don't get auto-worktrees and file-locking is at the task-list level, not the file level. Partition by file or use `/batch` worktree-isolated subagents.
13	- **Plan-shape check is at *planning* time, not implementation time** — otherwise F1 (silent architectural deviation) recurs.
14	
15	---
16	
17	## Part 1 — Six axes that decide the pattern
18	
19	After looking at recent cycles (ABA-115 7-slice UI feature; ABA-104 single-ticket spike; ABA-117/116/121/etc. queue of playbook writes; ABA-132 3-slice backend recalibration), these six questions discriminate. Anything more is decoration.
20	
21	| # | Axis | Why it matters | How to answer in 10 seconds |
22	|---|---|---|---|
23	| **A1** | **Single ticket or queue of N?** | Queue → batch primitive; single → focused primitive. | Count the sub-tasks. 1 = single; 2–3 with shared dependency chain = mini-pipeline; 4+ that share a template = queue. |
24	| **A2** | **Sequential dependency or parallel-safe?** | Slice N consumes JSON schema, file, or output that slice N-1 produces → must serialize. Otherwise can fan out. | Read the dependency graph in `plan.md`. Arrows = serialize. No arrows = parallel candidates. |
25	| **A3** | **Same-file edits across sub-tasks?** | Even parallel-safe work that touches the same file produces merge conflicts unless serialized — and agent teams have no per-file lock. | Skim the "Files to touch" line on each sub-task. Any overlap = treat as serial regardless of A2, or move to `/batch` (worktree-isolated). |
26	| **A4** | **One-way door?** | Schema change, public API, methodology constant, namespace change, vendor lock-in. Reversal is expensive — needs a plan-review gate *before* implementation, not "we'll fix it in review." | Does the change land in `DESIGN.md`, a JSON contract field, a `playbooks/*.md` frontmatter key, a `SKILL.md` schema block, or a public command name? Yes → one-way door. |
27	| **A5** | **Mechanical or subjective acceptance?** | Mechanical = agent self-checks against the criteria (number matches, JSON field present, test passes). Subjective = legibility, narrative quality, "does the chart pass the squint test" — needs a *differently-prompted* second pass to catch F3. | If every acceptance criterion can be checked by `grep`, `jq`, a unit test, or "function returns expected value" — mechanical. If any criterion is "reads well," "looks right," "is convincing" — subjective. |
28	| **A6** | **Implementation or research/spike?** | A spike's deliverable is a *written recommendation* — wrong skill if you treat it like implementation. The skill `pde-backend-spike` exists exactly for this. | Does the sub-task end in code in the tree, or in a written recommendation that closes with proceed/defer/kill? Latter = spike. |
29	| **A7** | **Does the proposed orchestration shape name a sub-agent that dispatches other sub-agents (a "supervisor"/"coordinator"/"manager" layer)?** | Claude Code does not allow sub-agent recursion. This shape is platform-invalid and is exactly what produced F1 in the recent cycle. **The 60-second ritual must answer A7 explicitly — even when no one-way doors exist.** | Read the plan's orchestration line. Any phrase like "supervisor sub-agent", "coordinator that spawns workers", "manager agent dispatches implementors" → yes. |
30	
31	**Not on the list (deliberately culled):**
32	- "Long-running vs short" — Claude Code sessions persist; runtime isn't a planning-time variable.
33	- "How many engineers" — N/A (single operator).
34	- "Confidence level" — handled by the per-task acceptance criteria, not by orchestration choice.
35	
36	---
37	
38	## Part 2 — Decision tree (leaves point at concrete primitives from `landscape.md`)
39	
40	Read top-down. First match wins. Each leaf names: the **shape**, the **pattern**, the **activation trigger**, and the **F1/F2/F3 guards** that the pattern must include.
41	
42	### Leaf L0 — Plan-review gate (default for *any* multi-slice plan, mandatory on A4 or A7)
43	**Trigger:** **Any of:** A4 = yes (one-way door), **OR** A7 = yes (platform-invalid orchestration shape), **OR** A1 ≥ 2 (any multi-slice plan, by default).
44	
45	The default-for-multi-slice rule is deliberate: F1 in the recent cycle had *zero* one-way doors — it was a pure plan-shape deviation. Routing only A4 plans through L0 leaves a hole exactly where ABA-115's failure lived. The cost is one `/pde-plan-review` invocation per multi-slice ticket — well inside the 60-second planning budget.
46	
47	**Pattern:** **`/pde-plan-review` skill, single-session sequential, before any implementation begins.**
48	
49	**Activation:** From the top-level Claude Code session, invoke `/pde-plan-review` against the current `tasks/plan.md`. The skill auto-fires on any plan with a one-way-door decision; this leaf extends "auto-fires" to all multi-slice plans.
50	
51	**What the L0 reviewer must check** (in addition to the skill's own checks):
52	1. **Platform feasibility.** Does the orchestration line name any sub-agent that dispatches other sub-agents? Reject — reshape to top-level fan-out (lead-led agent team) before approval.
53	2. **Leaf assignment.** Does the orchestration line resolve to one of L1–L7 in this map? If not, the plan is free-form and must be rewritten in the leaf vocabulary. Free-form plans are the surface F1 originated on.
54	3. **Phase transitions for L7.** If the leaf is L7, are the phase boundaries named, with a pattern label per phase? "Slices 1–3 chain (L4); 4–6 fan-out (L5); 7 chain (L4)" is acceptable. "We'll figure out the fan-out when we get there" is not.
55	
56	**Why this is the F1 guard.** F1 is a deviation between *planned shape* and *platform capability*. That bug is independent of whether the work touches a one-way door. L0 is the only check that runs before anyone touches code — it's the only place a platform-invalid shape can be caught cheaply.
57	
58	**Then route the resulting sub-tasks through L1–L6 below.**
59	
60	---
61	
62	### Leaf L1 — Single ticket, research/spike
63	**Trigger:** A1 = 1, A6 = spike.
64	
65	**Examples this repo:** ABA-104 (base-year-effect detection), ABA-103 (auto-derive WACC).
66	
67	**Pattern:** **Single-session sequential + `pde-backend-spike` skill.**
68	
69	**Activation:** `claude` → invoke the spike skill. No fan-out, no subagent, no team. The deliverable is a written recommendation, not code. Spike skill closes with proceed/defer/kill and a follow-up implementation ticket if proceed.
70	
71	**F-guards:** N/A (no orchestration layer to deviate, no parallel commits, no subjective render).
72	
73	---
74	
75	### Leaf L2 — Single ticket, implementation, mechanical acceptance
76	**Trigger:** A1 = 1, A5 = mechanical, A6 = implementation.
77	
78	**Examples this repo:** ABA-110 (SBC strip), ABA-111 (growth-rate cap), ABA-130 (coverage bypass), ABA-135/136/137 individually.
79	
80	**Pattern:** **Single-session sequential + `agent-skills:build`.**
81	
82	**Activation:** `claude` → `agent-skills:build` or the relevant skill. Sub-agent is overkill — the criteria are checkable in-session, and the cost of spawning a subagent isn't repaid.
83	
84	**Optional optimisation:** If the ticket would blow context (large file scan, log dive), spawn one `Explore`-type subagent inline via the Agent tool — cheap, ephemeral, and the result returns to the main thread.
85	
86	**F-guards:** N/A.
87	
88	---
89	
90	### Leaf L3 — Single ticket, implementation, subjective acceptance
91	**Trigger:** A1 = 1, A5 = subjective.
92	
93	**Examples this repo:** ABA-138 in isolation (CAGR glidepath — *if* it had been the whole ticket). The Y5-label collision (F3) lived in this leaf's territory.
94	
95	**Pattern:** **Single-session sequential for implementation, then a fresh-context second pass via `/crit` (or a `code-reviewer` custom subagent) with one of the canonical squint prompts in Part 3.5.**
96	
97	**Activation:**
98	1. `agent-skills:build` for implementation.
99	2. Immediately after the implementor's first verification, invoke `/crit` (or spawn a custom `code-reviewer` subagent via the Agent tool) with the squint prompt that matches the output type (chart / narrative / tabular — see Part 3.5). **Pick from the canonical list; do not write a new prompt from scratch under cycle time pressure** — that's how F3 reproduces in the reviewer.
100	
101	**Why this is the F3 guard.** The reviewer must *not* see the original AC. The label-collision bug passed all five mechanical criteria. If the reviewer uses the same criteria, it passes again. The squint prompts in Part 3.5 are deliberately *different* checklists, designed to fail on the kinds of bugs mechanical AC misses.
102	
103	**F-guards:**
104	- F3 — guarded by the differently-shaped second pass.
105	- F1, F2 — N/A.
106	
107	---
108	
109	### Leaf L4 — Multi-slice with sequential dependency (chained gates)
110	**Trigger:** A1 = 2–N, A2 = sequential.
111	
112	**Examples this repo:** ABA-115 slices 1→2→3 (UI shell → SKILL schema change → re-run reports). ABA-132 slices 1→2→3 (calibration design → impl → verification).
113	
114	**Pattern:** **Single-session sequential, one sub-task per turn, explicit gate read at each boundary.**
115	
116	**Activation:** No fan-out, no team, no subagent layer between slices. After each sub-task lands, the *operator* reads the gate criterion at the end of that slice's plan.md section and confirms before kicking off the next slice. Use `agent-skills:build` per task.
117	
118	**Why agent-teams is wrong here.** The slices share state (schema, JSON output, file contents). Agent teams' file-locking is task-level not file-level, and the upstream slice's output is the downstream slice's input — there's no parallelism gain to amortise the +1× token cost.
119	
120	**F-guards:**
121	- F1 — N/A.
122	- F2 — **partially mitigated, not prevented.** *This is the F2-hot leaf.* The written rule (slice-N+1 finds a bug from slice N → separate commit attributed to slice N, before slice N+1 work resumes) is the same shape as the rule that already failed in ABA-138 → silently-folded-into-ABA-139. Add a **human commit gate**: before approving any commit in a multi-slice chain, the *operator* (not an agent) runs `git diff --stat` on the staged change, compares the file list against the current slice's "Files to touch" line in `plan.md`, and asks "is anything in this diff from a prior slice?" If yes — abort the commit, extract the prior-slice fix as a separate commit attributed to its originating ticket, then re-stage the current slice. This is 15 seconds of human-eyeball-on-the-diff per commit; it costs less than the next F2 incident. Full mechanical prevention requires the `PreToolUse` hook listed in Part 5 — deferred to the user.
123	- F3 — apply L3's second-pass review at the chain's end if any slice produced subjective output.
124	
125	---
126	
127	### Leaf L5 — Multi-slice, parallel-safe slices after a shared prefix
128	**Trigger:** A1 = 2–N, A2 = parallel after some shared prefix, A3 = different files.
129	
130	**Examples this repo:** ABA-115 slices 4/5/6 (three independent chart components, each `ui/src/components/charts/*.jsx`, all consuming the same JSON contract from Slice 3).
131	
132	**Pattern:** **Agent teams (the `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1` primitive) — top-level lead + N teammates, one slice per teammate.**
133	
134	**Activation:** From the top-level Claude Code session, spawn an agent team (3–5 teammates is the Anthropic-suggested sweet spot, matches the chart trio exactly). Lead writes the task list, each teammate claims one slice's task, all teammates report to the lead. **The lead must be a top-level session, not a subagent** — agent teams cannot nest.
135	
136	**Fallback if agent teams unavailable** (flag removed, Claude Code <2.1.32, or org policy gates it): demote L5 to N sequential L2/L3 single-sessions. F-guards still apply; only wallclock changes. The cost note below covers when this is actually a downgrade vs. when it's a wash.
137	
138	**Pre-flight checklist before fan-out** (lead runs this once before assigning tasks):
139	1. Confirm each slice touches a *different file set* (A3). If two slices touch the same file, demote that pair to L4 chain — agent teams' file-locking won't save you.
140	2. Confirm the shared upstream dependency has landed (the gate from L4 → L5 transition). The agent team should start *after* the chain ends, not in parallel with it.
141	3. Each slice gets its own commit. Restate the F2 rule in the lead's task assignment to each teammate.
142	
143	**F-guards:**
144	- F1 — caught at L0 plan review (now mandatory on A7 = yes and on any multi-slice plan).
145	- F2 — **partially mitigated, not prevented** — same caveat as L4. The "separate commit, escalate, do not silently fold" rule is written into each teammate's brief, but it's the same shape that already failed. Add the **lead commit gate**: when a teammate reports back with a commit ready to merge, the *lead* (the top-level operator, not a sub-agent) reads `git diff --stat` against that teammate's assigned "Files to touch" list before approving. Any file outside the list = abort the merge, extract the cross-slice fix as its own commit attributed to the originating ticket. Full mechanical prevention requires the `PreToolUse` hook in Part 5.
146	- F3 — at the end of the fan-out, the lead invokes L3's second-pass review (`/crit` with one of the canonical squint prompts in Part 3.5) across the *aggregated* output, not per-slice. The label-collision bug was visible at aggregated render, not in any single component.
147	
148	**Cost note.** Agent teams are ≈ N+1× a single-session cost. Three chart teammates ≈ 4× a single session. **Heuristic:** use agent teams when **(a)** each slice has ≥1 hour of implementation work AND **(b)** the operator actually wants the wallclock back. Otherwise run sequentially in L4 — same total token cost across all slices, simpler shape, no experimental-flag dependency. The 4× spend only buys parallelism in calendar time; it does not reduce work.
149	
150	**When `/batch` is better than agent teams here.** If the parallel slices are truly *mechanical* (codemod-style — same instruction over many files), use the bundled `/batch` command instead. It auto-creates worktrees per unit and opens one PR per unit. The recent cycle's chart slices are *not* mechanical (each chart has bespoke data flow), so agent teams is the right fit.
151	
152	---
153	
154	### Leaf L6 — Queue of N file-isolated tickets sharing one template
155	**Trigger:** A1 = 4+, A2 = independent, A3 = each touches a different file.
156	
157	**Examples this repo:** ABA-120/121/122/123/124 (write playbooks: META, NVDA, AMZN, NFLX, ADYEN) — each touches `playbooks/TICKER.md`, format is locked, no inter-dependencies.
158	
159	**Pattern:** **Two viable paths, picked by the *per-unit input size* test below — not by gut-feel "is this mechanical?"**
160	
161	**Path test (deterministic):**
162	- **Per-unit input fits in <100 tokens** (a filename, a ticker symbol, a regex, an import string)? → **Path B (`/batch`).** The instruction is the same across all units; only a small parameter varies.
163	- **Per-unit input is ≥200 tokens** (a brief, a narrative spec, a ticker's sell-side debate axes, a per-unit context dossier)? → **Path A (Agent teams).** Each unit needs enough context that worktree-isolated subagents would lose nuance.
164	- 100–200 tokens is the gray zone; default to Path A unless the work is truly templated.
165	
166	**Path A — Agent teams** (per the test above).
167	- Same activation as L5: top-level lead + N teammates, one ticket per teammate. The template prompt is identical; the per-ticket context is what varies.
168	
169	**Path B — `/batch`** (per the test above).
170	- `/batch "rewrite all imports of X to Y"` style. Each unit gets its own worktree and PR. Best when the per-unit parameter is small and the instruction is identical.
171	
172	**For the playbook queue specifically: Path A.** Each playbook's brief is on the order of 500+ tokens (business architecture, capex cycle, sell-side debate axes, failure modes, narrative tone). That's well above the 200-token threshold — Path A by the rule, not just by intuition.
173	
174	**F-guards:**
175	- F1, F2 — same as L5.
176	- F3 — apply *once across the batch*, not per-playbook. Cross-playbook vocabulary drift and narrative inconsistency is invisible in any single playbook. One reviewer reads all N back-to-back.
177	
178	---
179	
180	### Leaf L7 — Mixed: feature with a shared prefix and a fan-out tail (composition of L4 + L5)
181	**Trigger:** ABA-115-shaped: chain → fan-out → close-out.
182	
183	**Pattern:** **Compose L4 and L5 explicitly in the plan. Write the orchestration plan as "Slices 1–3 chain (L4); Slices 4–6 fan-out (L5); Slice 7 chain (L4)." Each phase uses its own primitive.**
184	
185	**Activation:** Don't try to make a single orchestration primitive cover all of it. The phase transitions are where F1 and F2 surface most often — pause between phases, confirm gates, then choose the next primitive.
186	
187	**Phase-transition checklist** (run at *every* L4→L5, L5→L4, L4→L3, L5→L3 boundary — this is the procedure, not just a label):
188	1. **Gate read.** Open the prior phase's last slice in `plan.md`; read its acceptance criteria aloud. Each one met? If no — pause; do not transition.
189	2. **Cross-slice diff scan.** `git log <prior-phase-start>..HEAD --stat`. Did any commit touch files attributed to a slice in a *different* phase? If yes — that's an F2 hit; extract those changes into a separately-attributed commit before continuing.
190	3. **Next-phase pattern restated.** Say the next phase's leaf name (L4, L5, L6, or L3) out loud. If the answer is "I'm not sure" — go back to Part 4 ritual step 6 and re-derive from axes.
191	4. **Dispatch.** Only now spawn the next phase's primitive.
192	
193	This is ~90 seconds at each transition. It's the difference between L7 being a procedure and L7 being a sticker on the plan that means nothing in execution.
194	
195	**This is the most common shape this repo will produce** given the multi-stage skill architecture (UI ↔ SKILL ↔ reports). Plan must *name the phase transitions* so the operator knows when to switch primitives — and run the checklist above at each one.
196	
197	---
198	
199	## Part 3 — Three worked examples from this repo
200	
201	### Example 1: ABA-115 — Model tab v1 (L7 composition: L0 → L4 → L5 → L4 → L3)
202	**Sub-tasks (7 slices, see `tasks/plan.md`):**
203	1. UI shell (no SKILL change)
204	2. SKILL schema: emit `historical_fcf_margins[]`
205	3. Re-run covered tickers
206	4. CAGR glidepath chart
207	5. FCF margin trajectory chart
208	6. Scenario fan chart
209	7. Captions / styling / docs
210	
211	**Axis read:**
212	- A1: 7 slices.
213	- A2: 1→2→3 sequential; 4/5/6 parallel-safe; 7 final.
214	- A3: 4/5/6 each touch their own file (`charts/*.jsx`).
215	- A4: yes — Slice 2 changes a JSON contract (`historical_fcf_margins[]`).
216	- A5: slices 1–3 mechanical (JSON field present, audit row matches); slices 4–7 subjective (chart legibility).
217	- A6: implementation.
218	
219	**Pattern: L0 (`/pde-plan-review`) → L4 single-session chain (1→2→3) → L5 agent team of 3 teammates (4‖5‖6) → L4 single-session (7) → L3 `/crit` squint-test pass across the aggregated UI.**
220

```
270	- The reviewer must *not* see the original acceptance criteria. Send the squint prompt and the rendered artefact only.
271	- Run after the implementor reports green on mechanical AC, *before* merge.
272	
273	---
274	
275	**Squint prompt — CHARTS / visualisations** (the F3-original failure mode)
276	
277	> "You have not seen the acceptance criteria for this chart. Open the rendered chart at its canonical viewport size (the size a reader would actually see — assume desktop browser unless told otherwise).
278	>
279	> Answer these questions, in order. Do not skip any.
280	>
281	> 1. **One-sentence thesis test.** Without reading the underlying data or any caption, can you state in one sentence what this chart is arguing? If no — what's confusing?
282	> 2. **Label legibility.** Are any labels overlapping, clipped at chart edges, or unreadable at the rendered size? Look in particular at: axis tick labels, scenario/series endpoints (right edge, top edge), data-point callouts, and any annotation that points at a specific location on the chart.
283	> 3. **Axis sanity.** Does the y-axis range make sense — or is one extreme outlier blowing out the scale and flattening the rest? Does the x-axis tick density match the rendered width (too dense = ticks collide; too sparse = unclear time scale)?
284	> 4. **Colour and ordering.** If there's an implied ordering (bear < base < bull, low to high, etc.), does the visual ordering match? Do any two series share enough colour/style that a reader could swap them?
285	> 5. **Edge cases.** What happens at the chart's edges — left edge (start of series), right edge (most-recent or projected-Y5), top edge (chart maximum)? List anything that looks wrong, ugly, overlapping, or unclear at the edges specifically. Edges are where label collisions hide.
286	> 6. **What's missing.** Is there a current-state reference line, a callout for a fired condition (e.g. a growth cap), or a unit/scale indicator that a reader needs and isn't there?
287	>
288	> Name every issue, even small ones. Do not stop at the first issue — list them all. An issue that 'technically passes the spec' but produces a worse reader experience is in-scope for this review."
289	
290	This prompt would have caught the Y5 endpoint-label collision (question 5) and the AMZN negative-fcf y-axis blowout (question 3).
291	
292	---
293	
294	**Squint prompt — NARRATIVE OUTPUT** (playbooks, methodology notes, signal/model rationale prose)
295	
296	> "You have not seen the acceptance criteria for this document. Read it once at normal speed — do not study it.
297	>
298	> Answer these questions in order:
299	>
300	> 1. **One-paragraph thesis test.** In one paragraph, what is this document arguing? If you can't state the thesis after one read-through, the document is unclear.
301	> 2. **Vocabulary consistency.** Are the same concepts named with the same words throughout? List any term that drifts (e.g. 'segment' in one section, 'business line' in another, 'vertical' in a third — three names for the same thing is a smell).
302	> 3. **Confident-but-unsupported claims.** Are there assertions that a sceptical reader would push back on — and is the supporting evidence in the document? Quote one such claim verbatim if you find one.
303	> 4. **Audience leakage.** Is this written for the same reader throughout? If sections switch register (technical → marketing → internal-jargon), name the shifts.
304	> 5. **Structural mismatch.** Does the document's section ordering match what a reader looking for the answer would expect? Or do they have to hunt?
305	> 6. **Comparison to siblings (only if applicable — multi-document review).** If you are reviewing this alongside sibling documents (e.g. multiple playbooks), what's *different* between them in style, depth, structure, or vocabulary that isn't justified by the subject matter?
306	>
307	> Name every issue. The goal is to find the gap between 'technically correct' and 'a reader would actually use this'."
308	
309	This prompt is the right shape for the L6 cross-playbook second pass — question 6 is specifically the cross-doc vocabulary-drift check.
310	
311	---
312	
313	**Squint prompt — TABULAR / STRUCTURED REPORT OUTPUT** (Signal/Model/Screen JSON, the report viewer's tabs)
314	
315	> "You have not seen the acceptance criteria for this report or JSON output. Open the rendered view (UI tab if applicable, or the JSON itself).
316	>
317	> Answer in order:
318	>
319	> 1. **At-a-glance verdict.** What is this report *telling* a reader? State the verdict and primary number in one sentence. If you have to read three sections to know, the report is buried.
320	> 2. **Number sanity.** For every numeric field: does the magnitude look right (units in the right place — billions vs millions, percent vs decimal)? Does any number conflict with another (e.g. P/E and PEG implying different growth rates without explanation)?
321	> 3. **Missing fields, silent.** Are there fields you'd expect for this kind of report that are absent — and is the absence acknowledged (e.g. 'N/A — pre-profit') or just silently missing?
322	> 4. **Audit trail.** If overrides, caps, or substitutions were applied, are they named — or did some assumption silently swap in?
323	> 5. **Reader's next question.** What's the most likely next question a reader has after seeing this? Is the answer in the report?
324	>
325	> Name every issue you find."
326	
327	---
328	
329	**How to pick.** A5 = subjective with a chart artefact → use the charts prompt. A5 = subjective with prose artefact → narrative prompt. A5 = subjective with a structured report → tabular prompt. If the artefact is mixed (e.g. ABA-115 Model tab has charts + summary text), run the charts prompt for the chart components and the tabular prompt for the summary strip — they take ~2 minutes each.
330	
331	---
332	
333	## Part 4 — The cycle-planning ritual
334	
335	A six-question checklist applied to each parent ticket during planning. Output is the orchestration plan, written one line into the parent's `plan.md`.
336	
337	```
338	1. Count sub-tasks (A1). One → L1 / L2 / L3 by axes A5/A6. Several → continue.
339	2. Draw the dependency graph (A2). All arrows? L4. No arrows after a prefix? L5/L7. Pure independent → L6.
340	3. Highlight same-file overlaps (A3). Any overlap → serialize that pair regardless of step 2 (or move to `/batch` if truly mechanical).
341	4. Mark one-way doors (A4). Any → run `/pde-plan-review` (L0) before kicking off any sub-task implementation.
342	5. Mark subjective acceptance (A5). Any → schedule L3 second pass (`/crit` with squint-test prompt) after implementation. Write the squint-test prompt now, not at review time.
343	6. Write the one-line orchestration plan into `plan.md`, e.g.: "L0 → L4(1-3) → L5 team-of-3(4‖5‖6) → L4(7) → L3 squint pass."
344	```
345	
346	**Add to the bottom of `plan.md` for every multi-slice ticket:**
347	- The orchestration plan from step 6 above.
348	- The squint-test prompt (if step 5 fired) — pre-written, ready for the reviewer.
349	- The F2 rule restated verbatim: *"If an agent finds a bug from a prior slice, commit the fix as a separate commit attributed to the originating ticket before continuing. Do not silently fold."*
350	
351	**Total time: ≈ 60 seconds per parent ticket** once the axes are memorised.
352	
353	---
354	
355	## Part 5 — Open questions / follow-ups (out of scope for this map)
356	
357	- **F2 enforcement hook.** Currently F2 is a rule in the teammate brief. A `PreToolUse` hook on `git commit` that compares the staged diff against the teammate's "Files to touch" list and exits 2 on out-of-scope edits would mechanically prevent the failure. One small script, one config entry. Worth one cycle of setup if F2 recurs.
358	- **Custom `squint-reviewer` subagent.** L3's second pass currently uses `/crit`. A custom `.claude/agents/squint-reviewer.md` with the squint-test prompt baked into the system prompt would be a one-time cost that pays back across every L3 leaf — and removes the risk of the operator forgetting to pre-write the prompt at planning time.
359	- **Agent-teams cost calibration.** `landscape.md` flags that Anthropic hasn't published per-pattern token numbers. After the next L5 fan-out, log actual token consumption and compare against the "≈ N+1×" rough mental model. If costs are 2× the estimate, the L5 threshold for "is parallelism worth it?" needs revisiting.
```

# Orchestration recommendation — synthesis

The research team converged. Here's the short version, then the actionable bit, then the honest caveats.

## The recommendation in one sentence

**Memorise seven axes and seven leaves, run a 60-second checklist at cycle planning, write the orchestration plan as one line in `plan.md`, then execute it.** The full reference is at `/tmp/orchestration-research/pattern-map.md`; the orchestration landscape sits beside it at `landscape.md`; the stress-test verdict at `critique.md`.

## What you do on cycle Monday — your first keystroke

For every parent ticket in the next cycle, in your terminal:

```
$ claude
> read tasks/<ticket>/plan.md and apply the cycle-planning ritual from
> /tmp/orchestration-research/pattern-map.md Part 4. Write the one-line
> orchestration plan to the bottom of plan.md.
```

That's it — no skill yet, no hook yet, no new tooling. You're testing whether the pattern map alone changes the failure rate. If it does, **then** invest in the skill/hook layer (Part 5 of the map).

## The seven axes (A1–A7)

A1 count of sub-tasks · A2 sequential/parallel · A3 same-file overlap · A4 one-way door · A5 mechanical/subjective acceptance · A6 implementation/spike · **A7 does the plan name a "supervisor sub-agent that spawns workers"?** A7 is the one that would have caught last cycle's F1 deviation.

## The eight leaves (L0–L7)

| Leaf | Shape | Primitive |
|------|-------|-----------|
| L0 | Plan-review gate — **fires on any multi-slice plan, not just one-way doors** | `/pde-plan-review` |
| L1 | Single ticket, spike | `pde-backend-spike` skill |
| L2 | Single ticket, mechanical | `agent-skills:build` |
| L3 | Single ticket, subjective | Build → `/crit` with a canonical squint prompt |
| L4 | Multi-slice sequential chain | Single-session, operator reads gate at each boundary |
| L5 | Multi-slice parallel after a shared prefix | Agent teams (3–5 teammates) — when each slice ≥1h AND wallclock matters |
| L6 | Queue of N file-isolated tickets | `<100 tok per-unit input → /batch`; `≥200 tok → agent teams` |
| L7 | Composition (chain → fan-out → chain) | Compose L4+L5 explicitly, run phase-transition checklist at each boundary |

## What the team shipped that's directly usable

1. **Three canonical squint prompts** (charts / narrative / tabular) in `pattern-map.md` Part 3.5 — copy-paste, no fresh writing under cycle pressure. The chart one would have caught both Y5 collision and AMZN axis blowout.
2. **L7 phase-transition checklist** — 90 seconds at each phase boundary: gate read → cross-slice diff scan → next-phase name → dispatch. This is what was missing during ABA-138→139.
3. **60-second planning ritual** at Part 4 — six questions, one-line output into `plan.md`.

## Three honest caveats you should know going in

These are devils-advocate's MUST-surface items — not soft footnotes:

1. **F2 is partially mitigated, not prevented.** The "separate commit per slice" rule is the same shape as the rule that already failed in ABA-138→139. The pattern map adds a **15-second human commit gate** (you run `git diff --stat`, eyeball it against the slice's "Files to touch" line) as the primary defence. Full mechanical prevention needs a `PreToolUse` hook — that's the first follow-up worth building if F2 recurs.

2. **Agent-teams cost is uncalibrated.** "≈ N+1× per teammate" is a rough mental model, not a measurement. Anthropic hasn't published numbers. The L5 leaf now has a **wallclock heuristic**: only fan out when each slice is ≥1h of implementation work AND you actually want the wallclock back. Otherwise sequential L4 is the same total tokens at a simpler shape.

3. **Experimental-flag fragility.** L5 depends on `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1`. If the flag goes away or your version is too old, L5 fallback is **N sequential L2/L3 single-sessions** — F-guards still apply, only wallclock changes.

## One time-sensitive item

**27 days from today (2026-06-15):** Agent SDK and `claude -p` usage on subscription plans starts drawing from a separate monthly credit pool, not interactive limits. The pattern map doesn't recommend SDK for any leaf — but if you were tempted to build an SDK-based orchestrator this cycle, that's the TCO change to budget for first.

## What I'm doing next

- Tearing down the orchestration-research agent team (work complete, files persist under `/tmp/orchestration-research/`).
- Not building the skill/hook layer until you've run the pattern map for at least one cycle and decided it works.

If you want me to move the three files into the repo (e.g. `docs/orchestration.md`) so they're not in `/tmp`, say the word — I held back to keep the artefacts inspectable first.

# Executive summary — orchestration recommendation

## The decision

**Adopt the L0–L7 pattern map as your cycle-planning ritual.** Don't build new tooling yet. Run it manually for one cycle, see if it changes the failure rate, then decide whether to invest in the skill/hook layer.

## What changed vs. last cycle

Last cycle produced three avoidable failures:
- **F1** — planned a "supervisor sub-agent that spawns workers" (platform-invalid; you only learned in the final report).
- **F2** — a bug from ticket 1 was silently folded into ticket 2's commit. Git history is poisoned.
- **F3** — Y5 axis labels collided in the rendered chart. You caught it, the agent didn't.

The pattern map shifts each failure from "post-hoc retrospective" to "caught at planning time":

| Failure | Where it's caught now |
|---------|----------------------|
| F1 architectural deviation | New **A7 axis** + **L0** plan-review gate fires on every multi-slice plan |
| F2 silent commit fold | 15-second human `git diff --stat` gate at each commit + cross-slice diff scan at L7 phase boundaries |
| F3 missed visual bug | **L3 squint-prompt review** with three pre-written canonical prompts (chart / narrative / tabular) — reviewer never sees the original acceptance criteria |

## What it costs you

~60 seconds per parent ticket at planning time. One six-question checklist; one line written into `plan.md`. The squint prompts are pre-written — you don't compose anything under time pressure.

## Three caveats you should treat as live

1. **F2 is mitigated, not prevented.** Until you build the `PreToolUse` hook (one cycle of setup, deferred), you're relying on the same "agent reads the rule and follows it" discipline that already failed. The 15-second human eyeball on `git diff --stat` is the primary defence; the hook is the durable fix when F2 recurs.
2. **Agent-teams cost is uncalibrated.** "≈N+1× single-session" is a back-of-envelope hint, not a budget number. Use agent teams **only** when each slice is ≥1h of implementation work AND you want the wallclock back. Otherwise sequential L4 is the same total tokens, simpler shape, no experimental-flag dependency.
3. **June 15 2026 (27 days):** Agent SDK and `claude -p` shift to a separate credit pool on subscription plans. The pattern map doesn't recommend SDK for any leaf — so this is informational, not blocking. Note it if you were planning to build an SDK orchestrator.

## First keystroke next cycle

For each parent ticket:
```
$ claude
> apply docs/operations/orchestration/pattern-map.md Part 4 to
> tasks/<ticket>/plan.md and write the one-line orchestration plan
```

## Artefacts (now in repo)

- `docs/operations/orchestration/pattern-map.md` — the load-bearing reference. Six axes, eight leaves, three canonical squint prompts, 60-second ritual, three worked examples.
- `docs/operations/orchestration/landscape.md` — catalogue of every orchestration primitive in Claude Code with honest cost framing.
- `docs/operations/orchestration/critique.md` — the stress-test that pressure-tested both docs. Read this if you want the "what could go wrong" view.

## What I'm not doing yet

Not building the `PreToolUse` F2 hook, not building a `squint-reviewer` custom subagent, not turning the ritual into a slash command. All three are reasonable follow-ups in `pattern-map.md` Part 5 — but they're only worth building if the manual ritual actually moves the failure rate. Run one cycle, then decide.

# Executive summary — orchestration

## The problem we're solving

Last cycle I ran a small team of agents to ship three Linear tickets. The build completed, but three things went wrong that we only noticed afterwards.

First, I designed a coordination structure — a "supervisor" agent that spawned worker agents — that Claude Code doesn't actually support. The supervisor agent improvised something different and only mentioned it in the final report. We learned about the deviation after the work was done.

Second, a bug introduced by the first ticket's code was discovered while working on the second ticket, and the fix was quietly bundled into the second ticket's commit. Looking at the git history now, you can't tell which ticket caused which bug.

Third, a chart shipped with overlapping labels at the right edge. The agent's verification passed because every acceptance check was mechanical — "are there three lines drawn," "is the cap callout present" — and none of them asked "is this chart actually readable." You spotted the bug in the screenshot. The agent didn't.

The point of the research was to produce something that catches these things at planning time, before any agent touches code.

## What the team came back with

A short checklist you run during cycle planning, for each parent ticket. It takes about a minute once you've memorised it.

You ask six questions about the ticket:

- How many sub-tasks does it break into?
- Do they have to happen in order, or can they run in parallel?
- Do any of them touch the same file?
- Does anything change a public contract — a schema, an API, a file format, a methodology constant? Those are expensive to reverse.
- Are the acceptance checks mechanical (a test passes, a number matches) or subjective (a chart looks right, prose reads well)?
- Is the deliverable code in the tree, or a written recommendation?

The answers point you at one of seven execution shapes, ranging from "single agent in one session" up to "small team running in parallel with explicit gates between phases." Each shape says which Claude Code building block to use — single session, the `/pde-plan-review` skill, an agent team, the `/batch` command — and which of the three failures above it has to guard against.

The team also wrote out three reusable review prompts: one for charts, one for narrative output, one for tabular reports. You paste the appropriate one into the ticket plan when you're planning, so the reviewer agent has it ready when its turn comes. The prompts deliberately do not show the reviewer the original acceptance criteria, so the reviewer can't just re-check the same things the implementer already checked. The chart prompt explicitly asks about overlapping edge labels and axis blow-out — the bugs we missed last cycle.

## What it costs

About a minute per parent ticket at planning time. Nothing else changes — same skills, same agent-team primitive, same workflow. You're moving the check forward from retrospective to planning.

## What it doesn't fully solve

The honest read from the team's own critique:

**The "bundled fix" problem is mitigated, not eliminated.** The checklist tells the agent to commit cross-ticket fixes as separate attributed commits, but that's a written rule the agent has to follow — which is exactly the kind of rule that failed last cycle. The realistic short-term mitigation is to spend fifteen seconds reading the staged diff yourself before approving each commit. The proper fix is a hook that automatically rejects a commit if it touches files outside the assigned ticket's scope. That hook is roughly a cycle's worth of work. Worth building if the manual diff-read isn't catching things after a couple of cycles.

**Agent-team costs are not measured.** Anthropic hasn't published per-token figures. The rough mental model is "running an agent team with N teammates costs about N+1× a single session" but that is a guess, not a number. The rule of thumb the team settled on: only run agents in parallel when each parallel slice would take an hour or more of work on its own. Otherwise run them sequentially. The token cost is roughly the same either way — you only buy back wall-clock time by parallelising.

**Agent teams use an experimental flag** that Anthropic could remove or rename without warning. If it does disappear, the fallback is straightforward: run the same slices sequentially in single sessions. The checklist still works; only the parallelism goes away.

## One time-sensitive note

On June 15, about four weeks from today, Anthropic is changing how Agent SDK usage is billed on subscription plans — it moves to a separate monthly credit pool. The checklist does not recommend the Agent SDK for any of the seven shapes, so this does not change the recommendation. It is only relevant if you were considering building a custom SDK-based orchestrator this cycle.

## What to do next cycle

When you sit down with each parent ticket during planning:

1. Open `docs/operations/orchestration/pattern-map.md`.
2. Run the six questions against the ticket.
3. Write one line at the bottom of the ticket's plan saying which shape the work takes — for example, "three sequential slices in a single session, then a chart review at the end."
4. If any sub-task has a subjective acceptance check, paste the matching review prompt from the pattern map's reviewer-prompts section into the plan now, so the reviewer agent has it when its turn comes.

Do not build the hook, custom skill, or slash-command versions of any of this yet. Run the manual checklist for one cycle. If it changes the failure rate, the durable versions are worth the investment. If it doesn't, you've spent six minutes total across the cycle, not a week building tooling that wasn't earning its place.

## The three files now in the repo

- `docs/operations/orchestration/pattern-map.md` — the checklist itself, the seven shapes with worked examples from recent tickets, and the three reviewer prompts.
- `docs/operations/orchestration/landscape.md` — a reference catalogue of every orchestration option Claude Code offers, with honest cost and setup notes. You do not need to read this to use the checklist.
- `docs/operations/orchestration/critique.md` — the team's own stress-test of the recommendation, including the items that didn't fully close. Read this if you want the "what could still go wrong" view before committing.

20

22	
23	
24	| #      | Axis                                                                                                                                        | Why it matters                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | How to answer in 10 seconds                                                                                                                                                                                  |
25	| ------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
26	| **A1** | **Single ticket or queue of N?**                                                                                                            | Queue → batch primitive; single → focused primitive.                                                                                                                                                                                               | Count the sub-tasks. 1 = single; 2–3 with shared dependency chain = mini-pipeline; 4+ that share a template = queue.                                                                                         |

Otherwise can fan out.                                                                                                                                     | Read the dependency graph in `plan.md`. Arrows = serialize. No arrows = parallel candidates.                                                                                                                 |
28	| **A3** | **Same-file edits across sub-tasks?**                                                                                                       | Even parallel-safe work that touches the same file produces merge conflicts unless serialized — and agent teams have no per-file lock.                                                                                                             | Skim the "Files to touch" line on each sub-task. Any overlap = treat as serial regardless of A2, or move to `/batch` (worktree-isolated).                                                                    |
29	| **A4** | **One-way door?**                                                                                                                           | Schema change, public API, methodology constant, namespace change, vendor lock-in. Reversal is expensive — needs a plan-review gate *before* implementation, not "we'll fix it in review."                                                         | Does the change land in `DESIGN.md`, a JSON contract field, a `playbooks/*.md` frontmatter key, a `SKILL.md` schema block, or a public command name? Yes → one-way door.                                     |
30	| **A5** | **Mechanical or subjective acceptance?**                                                                                                    | Mechanical = agent self-checks against the criteria (number matches, JSON field present, test passes). Subjective = legibility, narrative quality, "does the chart pass the squint test" — needs a *differently-prompted* second pass to catch F3. | If every acceptance criterion can be checked by `grep`, `jq`, a unit test, or "function returns expected value" — mechanical. If any criterion is "reads well," "looks right," "is convincing" — subjective. |
31	| **A6** | **Implementation or research/spike?**                                                                                                       | A spike's deliverable is a *written recommendation* — wrong skill if you treat it like implementation. The skill `pde-backend-spike` exists exactly for this.                                                                                      | Does the sub-task end in code in the tree, or in a written recommendation that closes with proceed/defer/kill? Latter = spike.                                                                               |

335	> 3. **Confident-but-unsupported claims.** Are there assertions that a sceptical reader would push back on — and is the supporting evidence in the document? Quote one such claim verbatim if you find one.

365	
366	## Part 4 — The cycle-planning ritual
367	
368	A six-question checklist applied to each parent ticket during planning. Output is the orchestration plan, written one line into the parent's `plan.md`.
369	
370	```
371	1. Count sub-tasks (A1). One → L1 / L2 / L3 by axes A5/A6. Several → continue.
372	2. Draw the dependency graph (A2). All arrows? L4. No arrows after a prefix? L5/L7. Pure independent → L6.
373	3. Highlight same-file overlaps (A3). Any overlap → serialize that pair regardless of step 2 (or move to `/batch` if truly mechanical).
374	4. Mark one-way doors (A4). Any → run `/pde-plan-review` (L0) before kicking off any sub-task implementation.
375	5. Mark subjective acceptance (A5). Any → schedule L3 second pass (`/crit` with squint-test prompt) after implementation. Write the squint-test prompt now, not at review time.
376	6. Write the one-line orchestration plan into `plan.md`, e.g.: "L0 → L4(1-3) → L5 team-of-3(4‖5‖6) → L4(7) → L3 squint pass."
377	```
378	
379	**Add to the bottom of `plan.md` for every multi-slice ticket:**
380	
381	- The orchestration plan from step 6 above.
382	- The squint-test prompt (if step 5 fired) — pre-written, ready for the reviewer.
383	- The F2 rule restated verbatim: *"If an agent finds a bug from a prior slice, commit the fix as a separate commit attributed to the originating ticket before continuing. Do not silently fold."*
384	
385	**Total time: ≈ 60 seconds per parent ticket** once the axes are memorised.

391	- **F2 enforcement hook.** Currently F2 is a rule in the teammate brief. A `PreToolUse` hook on `git commit` that compares the staged diff against the teammate's "Files to touch" list and exits 2 on out-of-scope edits would mechanically prevent the failure. One small script, one config entry. Worth one cycle of setup if F2 recurs.
392	- **Custom `squint-reviewer` subagent.** L3's second pass currently uses `/crit`. A custom `.claude/agents/squint-reviewer.md` with the squint-test prompt baked into the system prompt would be a one-time cost that pays back across every L3 leaf — and removes the risk of the operator forgetting to pre-write the prompt at planning time.
393	- **Agent-teams cost calibration.** `landscape.md` flags that Anthropic hasn't published per-pattern token numbers. After the next L5 fan-out, log actual token consumption and compare against the "≈ N+1×" rough mental model. If costs are 2× the estimate, the L5 threshold for "is parallelism worth it?" needs revisiting.
394	- **Channels for async hand-off** between phase transitions in L7. Currently the operator is the courier between L4 and L5 phases. Perfect. Now let me create a brief factual report on the documentation conventions:

## Documentation Style & Infrastructure Report

### **1. Heading & Structure Conventions**

**Depth:** Docs use H1 (`#`) for title, H2 (`##`) for major sections, H3 (`###`) for subsections. H4 rarely appears. The pattern is shallow (2–3 levels typically).

**Section length:** Operational / design docs (engagement-kpi-drift-ci.md: 76 lines, design-docs: 220–350 lines) stay under 400 lines. The roadmap (227 lines) and charter/design files (50+ lines each shown, likely longer full) are reference-shaped, not narrative. Playbooks (ASML/GOOG format) layer YAML front-matter with 5–10 line summaries, then H2 sections for Business Architecture, Failure Modes, Scenario Axes.

**Front matter:** Design docs use YAML blocks (name, status, authors, created, last_updated, linear ticket, supersedes, plan_review, spike_decision). Playbooks also use YAML (ticker, last_updated, base_wacc, growth_ceiling, terminal_margin, confidence_anchor, bear_narrative, base_narrative, bull_narrative, failure_modes, scenario_axes). Charter/design/roadmap docs lack structured front-matter — they are reference pages.

### **2. Visual Conventions**

**Tables:** Used extensively for structured data (e.g., roadmap's "Capacity allocation" table, "Theme distribution" table; engagement-kpi doc's "Engagement-KPI reality check"). Simple markdown `| — |` format.

**ASCII/code blocks:** Design docs use ASCII box diagrams for architecture (`/stock-equity → Router`) and code snippets for schema. No Mermaid diagrams observed in sampled docs.

**Markdown rendering pipeline:** No markdown-rendering dependencies detected in grep of ui/package.json or node_modules. The `ui/` directory exists (Vite + React per CLAUDE.md) but no markdown-rendering library (marked, markdown-it, react-markdown) is currently installed or in use in the repo.

### **3. Guide-Style References**

**Playbooks** (ASML.md, GOOG.md) serve as the closest "guide" template: YAML front-matter, 1-line thesis, numbered section headers (Business Architecture, Failure Modes, Scenario Axes), embedded tables, 5–10 line descriptive paragraphs, "TODO" inline callouts for research gaps. Tone is analytical, assumption-transparent, ticker-specific.

**Operational guides** (engagement-kpi-drift-ci.md) use plain-English numbered sections ("What it is," "Job 1 — drift_check," "What it does NOT do," "How to test"). Conversational but structured; no narrative flourishes.

### **4. Top-Level Docs**

**CHARTER.md, DESIGN.md, COVERAGE.md, README.md:** Reference architecture, not guides. Emphasis on unlocked/locked decisions, boundaries, namespace conventions, principles. Heavy cross-referencing (e.g., "see DESIGN.md → *Playbook Layer*"). Tone is prescriptive.

---

**Key fact for your rewrite:** The repo has no HTML rendering pipeline active. Markdown exists as source-of-truth, not as an intermediate format for rendering. This is your greenfield to design — no existing conventions to override, but also no tooling to leverage yet.
```
