## Approved Plan:
# Orchestration user-guide rewrite

## Context

The orchestration research produced three documents in `docs/operations/orchestration/`:

- `pattern-map.md` — the checklist itself with seven axes, eight execution shapes, three reviewer prompts (~360 lines)
- `landscape.md` — catalogue of every Claude Code orchestration primitive (~112 lines)
- `critique.md` — stress-test of both documents (~218 lines)

These are research-grade artefacts — dense, full of internal codes (F1/F2/F3, L0–L7, A1–A7), and structured for the writer rather than the reader. The user wants a synthesised user guide written in the executive-summary tone from earlier in this session: plain English, self-contained, no internal jargon as load-bearing vocabulary, good navigation, helpful visuals. The eventual destination is an HTML page, but the markdown source is the deliverable for this task.

A second issue surfaced while answering a user question: `pattern-map.md` Part 1 is titled "Six axes" but actually lists seven, and Part 4 is titled "six-question checklist" but the missing seventh question (does the plan name a coordinator agent that dispatches workers — the platform-invalid shape that caused last cycle's failure) is the most important new addition. The internal inconsistency needs a small targeted fix so the guide can quote the source confidently.

## Approach

Create one new file alongside the three research artefacts: `docs/operations/orchestration/guide.md`. Keep the three originals untouched as the depth-on-demand reference layer. The guide links into them where a reader might want detail.

Apply one small, targeted edit to `pattern-map.md` to fix the six-vs-seven inconsistency.

## Guide structure

Top-down: what to do → why → caveats → reference. Reader can stop reading at any section once they have what they came for.

1. **Top of file: navigation block.** Table-of-contents links to every H2 below. Markdown anchor links work in any HTML renderer and stay readable as plain text.

2. **Section 1 — What this is.** Two short paragraphs. What problem the checklist solves. Names the three failure modes from last cycle in plain English (the supervisor-agent platform deviation; the silently-bundled cross-ticket bug fix; the chart with overlapping labels the agent didn't catch).

3. **Section 2 — The seven questions.** Numbered list with a short paragraph each. Plain English, no axis codes. The seventh question (coordinator-agent shape) is included as a peer, not a footnote. Each question has a "how to answer in ten seconds" sentence.

4. **Section 3 — The seven execution shapes.** A single table mapping answers to shapes (single session, sequential chain, parallel team, batch command, composition, plan-review gate, reviewer pass). Each shape gets one paragraph below the table with: when it applies, what to do, which Claude Code primitive (with link to `landscape.md`).

5. **Section 4 — The 60-second ritual.** Six numbered steps, exactly the workflow the user runs at cycle planning. Each step references the question and shape sections by anchor link. Output is one line written into the ticket's `plan.md`.

6. **Section 5 — The three reviewer prompts.** Charts, narrative, tabular. Verbatim from `pattern-map.md` Part 3.5 — these are the load-bearing artefacts a reviewer agent receives, so they should be copy-paste-ready in the guide too. Each prompt has a one-sentence "when to use" lead-in.

7. **Section 6 — Worked example.** One end-to-end walkthrough of last cycle's seven-slice UI feature (ABA-115), showing how the checklist generates the orchestration plan and where each guard fires. This is the section that makes the abstract concrete.

8. **Section 7 — What this doesn't fix.** Three honest caveats in the second-exec-summary tone: the bundled-fix problem is mitigated not eliminated; agent-team costs are uncalibrated; experimental flag is fragile. Each caveat names the manual short-term mitigation and the durable follow-up.

9. **Section 8 — Reference.** Links to the three source documents under `docs/operations/orchestration/` with one-line "read this if…" hooks for each. Brief glossary if any term resists plain-English rewrite.

## Visual conventions

Matches repo convention from `docs/operations/engagement-kpi-drift-ci.md` and the playbooks: markdown tables for structured data, ASCII diagrams for flow. No mermaid (not used elsewhere in this repo and the markdown→HTML pipeline doesn't exist yet — ASCII renders cleanly in both raw markdown and any HTML renderer).

Specific visuals to add:

- **Decision-tree ASCII diagram** in Section 3 showing how the seven questions narrow to a shape (similar to the agent-skills flowchart in the CLAUDE.md hook output the user has seen).
- **Phase-transition diagram** for the composition shape (Section 3), showing where the manual gates fire between chain and parallel phases.
- **A small "F1/F2/F3 → which question catches it" mapping table** in Section 7, so the user can see which question prevents which failure without having to chase footnotes.

No emojis, no callout-block syntax not already in repo, no rendering-specific markdown.

## Navigation

Markdown anchor links throughout. Two passes:

1. Top-of-file TOC links to each H2 section.
2. Within sections, link forward and back: Section 4's ritual references Section 2's questions by anchor; Section 7's caveats link to Section 5's reviewer prompts and Section 3's shapes.

This pattern degrades gracefully — plain text in markdown, real links in any HTML conversion.

## Targeted fix to `pattern-map.md`

Two-line edit, both heading-level:

1. Line 19: change `## Part 1 — Six axes that decide the pattern` to `## Part 1 — Seven axes that decide the pattern`.
2. Line 21: change `these six questions discriminate` to `these seven questions discriminate`.
3. Line 368: change `A six-question checklist applied to each parent ticket during planning` to `A seven-question checklist applied to each parent ticket during planning`.
4. Add A7 as an explicit ritual step in Part 4 (currently A7 is implicit — caught by step 4's `/pde-plan-review` invocation but not asked directly). Insert between current steps 4 and 5: `5. Read the plan's orchestration line (A7). Does it name a sub-agent that dispatches other sub-agents? Yes → reject the plan; reshape to top-level fan-out before continuing.` Renumber remaining steps.

These are mechanical fixes that bring the document's own counts in line with what it actually contains. No content drift — A7 already exists in Part 1 and L0's trigger; this just makes Part 4 consistent.

## Files modified

- **Created:** `docs/operations/orchestration/guide.md` (new file, ~300–400 lines based on section count and second-exec-summary density).
- **Edited:** `docs/operations/orchestration/pattern-map.md` (four small heading/list-item edits as described).

## Verification

1. Open `guide.md` in a markdown previewer (any one of the user's editors will do). Confirm every TOC link and in-page anchor resolves.
2. Read top-to-bottom in one pass. Time it — should be under 5 minutes for a first read.
3. Read just Sections 1, 2, and 4. Confirm that is enough to run the ritual on a real ticket without referring to any other section. If not, Section 4 isn't self-contained enough.
4. Pick one in-flight Linear ticket and run the seven questions against it from the guide alone. Write the one-line orchestration plan into the ticket's `plan.md`. Confirm the guide gave a clear answer at every step.
5. Open `pattern-map.md` Part 1 and Part 4. Confirm counts (seven questions / seven-question checklist) match the body. Confirm the new step in Part 4 about A7 reads naturally between the surrounding steps.
6. Sanity-check that nothing in `landscape.md` or `critique.md` quotes "six axes" by exact phrase — if it does, leave it alone (those documents are research-grade and the version drift is part of the record).

## Out of scope

- HTML conversion itself — the user will do this separately.
- Building the `PreToolUse` git-commit hook (deferred follow-up in `pattern-map.md` Part 5).
- A custom `squint-reviewer` subagent (deferred follow-up).
- Any change to `landscape.md` or `critique.md` — they stay as the research record.

{
  "file_path": "/Users/anton/src/stock-review/docs/operations/orchestration/guide.md",
  "content": "# Orchestration guide\n\nA short, self-contained reference for deciding how to break a Linear ticket into agent work. Run the seven-question checklist at cycle planning; write one line into the ticket's `plan.md`; execute.\n\nIf this is your first read, work top-to-bottom. Once you have run the ritual on a couple of tickets, you'll come back only to Section 2 (the questions) and Section 5 (the reviewer prompts).\n\n## Contents\n\n1. [What this is](#1-what-this-is)\n2. [The seven questions](#2-the-seven-questions)\n3. [The seven execution shapes](#3-the-seven-execution-shapes)\n4. [The 60-second ritual](#4-the-60-second-ritual)\n5. [Reviewer prompts](#5-reviewer-prompts)\n6. [Worked example — ABA-115](#6-worked-example--aba-115)\n7. [What this doesn't fix](#7-what-this-doesnt-fix)\n8. [Reference](#8-reference)\n\n---\n\n## 1. What this is\n\nA planning-time checklist that decides how a ticket gets executed by agents. The point is to make the call before any agent touches code, not at retrospective.\n\nLast cycle, three things went wrong and we caught all three only afterwards:\n\n- I designed a coordination structure — a \"supervisor\" agent that spawned worker agents — that Claude Code doesn't actually support. The agent improvised silently and only mentioned the deviation in the final report.\n- A bug introduced by the first ticket's code was discovered while working on the second ticket, and the fix was quietly bundled into the second ticket's commit. The git history can no longer tell you which ticket caused which bug.\n- A chart shipped with overlapping labels at the right edge. The agent's verification passed because every acceptance check was mechanical (\"are there three lines drawn\") and none of them asked \"is this chart readable?\" You spotted the bug in the screenshot. The agent didn't.\n\nThe checklist below turns each of those failures into a planning-time question. If you answer the questions and write the resulting orchestration line into the ticket's plan, the failure has somewhere to be caught before any code is written.\n\n---\n\n## 2. The seven questions\n\nAsk these of every parent ticket during cycle planning. Most have a 10-second answer once the ticket plan is in front of you.\n\n### Question 1 — How many sub-tasks does this ticket break into?\n\nCount the items in the ticket's `plan.md`. One is a single-session job. Two or three with arrows between them is a chain. Four or more that share a template is a queue.\n\n### Question 2 — Do they have to happen in order, or can they run in parallel?\n\nLook at the dependency graph in `plan.md`. If sub-task two consumes a JSON schema, file, or output that sub-task one produces, you have to serialise. If they don't share inputs, they're candidates for parallel work.\n\n### Question 3 — Do any of them touch the same file?\n\nSkim each sub-task's \"files to touch\" line. Any overlap means you serialise that pair regardless of what Question 2 said — agent teams don't lock at the file level, and parallel edits on the same file produce merge conflicts.\n\n### Question 4 — Does anything change a public contract?\n\nBy \"public contract\" I mean a JSON schema field, a `SKILL.md` schema block, a `playbooks/*.md` frontmatter key, a public command name, or anything in `DESIGN.md`. These are expensive to reverse — they need a plan-review pass before implementation, not \"we'll fix it in review.\"\n\n### Question 5 — Are the acceptance checks mechanical or subjective?\n\nMechanical means a test passes, a number matches, a JSON field is present, a function returns the expected value. Subjective means a chart looks right, prose reads well, a report is legible. Any subjective check needs a second-pass reviewer who has not seen the original acceptance criteria — otherwise the reviewer just re-checks the same things the implementer already checked.\n\n### Question 6 — Is the deliverable code in the tree, or a written recommendation?\n\nIf the ticket ends in a \"proceed / defer / kill\" memo with no code change, it's a spike. Spikes use a different skill (`pde-backend-spike`) and have different acceptance: the deliverable is the recommendation, not running code.\n\n### Question 7 — Does the plan describe an agent dispatching other agents?\n\nRead the orchestration line in `plan.md` (the one you're about to write — or one you've drafted in your head). Does it contain any phrase like \"supervisor sub-agent,\" \"coordinator that spawns workers,\" or \"manager agent dispatches implementers\"? Claude Code does not allow sub-agents to spawn other sub-agents. This shape is platform-invalid and was the cause of last cycle's silent architectural deviation. Reject the plan; reshape it so the top-level Claude Code session — or an agent-team lead, which is functionally a top-level session — does the dispatching. Only one layer of fan-out is supported.\n\n---\n\n## 3. The seven execution shapes\n\nThe seven questions narrow to one of these shapes. Pick the first match top-to-bottom.\n\n| Shape | When it applies | Building block |\n|-------|-----------------|----------------|\n| **Plan-review gate** | Any multi-slice plan, or a one-way door (Q4 = yes), or a coordinator-agent shape (Q7 = yes) | `/pde-plan-review` skill, before any other work |\n| **Single session, spike** | One sub-task, deliverable is a recommendation (Q6 = spike) | `pde-backend-spike` skill |\n| **Single session, mechanical** | One sub-task, acceptance is mechanical (Q5 = mechanical) | `agent-skills:build` (or the relevant skill) |\n| **Single session, subjective** | One sub-task, acceptance is subjective (Q5 = subjective) | Build skill, then `/crit` with a [reviewer prompt](#5-reviewer-prompts) |\n| **Sequential chain** | Multiple sub-tasks with arrows between them (Q2 = sequential) | Single session, one sub-task per turn, explicit gate at each boundary |\n| **Parallel team** | Multiple sub-tasks, parallel-safe, different files (Q2 = parallel, Q3 = no overlap) | Agent teams (`CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1`), lead plus 3–5 teammates |\n| **Composition** | Mix of chain and parallel within one ticket (most common shape this repo produces) | Compose chain + team explicitly in the plan, with named phase transitions |\n\n### Decision flow\n\n```\n                  ┌──────────────────────────────┐\n                  │ Q7: coordinator-agent shape? │──yes──┐\n                  └──────────────┬───────────────┘       │\n                                 no                      ▼\n                                 │              [Reject; reshape]\n                                 ▼\n                  ┌──────────────────────────────┐\n                  │ Q4: one-way door?            │──yes──┐\n                  └──────────────┬───────────────┘       │\n                                 no                      ▼\n                                 │           [Plan-review gate first,\n                                 │              then continue]\n                                 ▼\n                  ┌──────────────────────────────┐\n                  │ Q1: how many sub-tasks?      │\n                  └──────────────┬───────────────┘\n                                 │\n                  ┌──────────────┴───────────────┐\n                  ▼                              ▼\n              [one]                        [several]\n                  │                              │\n                  ▼                              ▼\n       ┌──────────────────┐         ┌─────────────────────────┐\n       │ Q6: spike?       │         │ Q2: arrows between them?│\n       └──┬───────────┬───┘         └────┬─────────────┬──────┘\n        yes          no                  yes           no\n          │           │                   │             │\n          ▼           ▼                   ▼             ▼\n     [Spike]    ┌──────────┐       [Sequential    ┌──────────┐\n                │ Q5:      │        chain]        │ Q3: same │\n                │ mech or  │                      │  file?   │\n                │ subj?    │                      └──┬───────┘\n                └─┬──────┬─┘                       yes    no\n                mech   subj                         │      │\n                  │     │                           ▼      ▼\n                  ▼     ▼                       [Demote  [Parallel\n              [Single  [Single                   to       team]\n               mech]    subj]                    chain]\n```\n\nIf you land on the composition shape (chain + team mixed), write the orchestration line as a sequence of shape names — e.g. `chain(1-3) → team(4‖5‖6) → chain(7)` — and run a phase-transition check at each boundary.\n\n### Phase transitions inside a composition\n\n```\n   ┌─────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────┐\n   │ chain phase     │──→│ gate check       │──→│ team phase      │\n   │ (sub-tasks 1-3) │   │ — read AC of last│   │ (sub-tasks 4-6) │\n   │                 │   │   slice          │   │   in parallel   │\n   └─────────────────┘   │ — git diff scan  │   └─────────┬───────┘\n                         │   for cross-     │             │\n                         │   slice bug folds│             ▼\n                         │ — name next      │   ┌──────────────────┐\n                         │   shape out loud │   │ gate check       │\n                         └──────────────────┘   │ (same three      │\n                                                │  steps)          │\n                                                └─────────┬────────┘\n                                                          ▼\n                                                ┌─────────────────┐\n                                                │ chain phase     │\n                                                │ (sub-task 7)    │\n                                                └─────────────────┘\n```\n\nThe gate check takes about 90 seconds. It's the difference between composition being a real procedure and being a label on the plan that means nothing in execution.\n\n---\n\n## 4. The 60-second ritual\n\nFor each parent ticket during cycle planning:\n\n1. **Count** the sub-tasks ([Question 1](#question-1--how-many-sub-tasks-does-this-ticket-break-into)). One sub-task → jump to [single-session shapes](#3-the-seven-execution-shapes). Several → continue.\n2. **Draw the dependencies** ([Question 2](#question-2--do-they-have-to-happen-in-order-or-can-they-run-in-parallel)). All arrows → chain. No arrows after a shared prefix → composition. Pure independent → queue.\n3. **Look for same-file overlaps** ([Question 3](#question-3--do-any-of-them-touch-the-same-file)). Any overlap means that pair serialises regardless of what step 2 said.\n4. **Mark one-way doors** ([Question 4](#question-4--does-anything-change-a-public-contract)). Any → run `/pde-plan-review` before kicking off any sub-task implementation.\n5. **Check the orchestration line for coordinator-agent shapes** ([Question 7](#question-7--does-the-plan-describe-an-agent-dispatching-other-agents)). Any \"supervisor sub-agent that spawns workers\" wording → reject the plan and reshape before continuing. This is independent of one-way-door status — last cycle's failure had zero one-way doors.\n6. **Mark subjective acceptance** ([Question 5](#question-5--are-the-acceptance-checks-mechanical-or-subjective)). Any → schedule a reviewer pass and paste the matching [reviewer prompt](#5-reviewer-prompts) into the plan now, not at review time.\n7. **Write one line into `plan.md`** describing the orchestration. E.g. `plan-review → chain(1-3) → team-of-3(4‖5‖6) → chain(7) → reviewer pass on UI`.\n\nAlso add to the bottom of `plan.md` for every multi-slice ticket:\n\n- The orchestration line from step 7.\n- The reviewer prompt (if step 6 fired) — pre-written, ready for the reviewer to receive.\n- This sentence, verbatim: *\"If an agent finds a bug from a prior sub-task, commit the fix as a separate commit attributed to the originating ticket before continuing. Do not silently fold.\"*\n\nAbout a minute per parent ticket once the questions are memorised. Question 6 (spike or implementation) is implicit in step 1 above — if the deliverable is a recommendation, you took the single-session-spike branch and never reached step 2.\n\n---\n\n## 5. Reviewer prompts\n\nThree prompts the reviewer agent receives in place of the original acceptance criteria. The point is to ask a *different* set of questions than the implementer answered — otherwise the reviewer reproduces the implementer's blind spot.\n\nPick by output type. Paste the prompt into the ticket's `plan.md` at planning time so the reviewer has it ready when its turn comes.\n\n### Chart prompt\n\nUse this for any ticket that produces a rendered chart or visualisation (e.g. anything under `ui/src/components/charts/`).\n\n> You have not seen the acceptance criteria for this chart. Open the rendered chart at its canonical viewport size (the size a reader would actually see — assume desktop browser unless told otherwise).\n>\n> Answer these questions, in order. Do not skip any.\n>\n> 1. **One-sentence thesis test.** Without reading the underlying data or any caption, can you state in one sentence what this chart is arguing? If no — what's confusing?\n> 2. **Label legibility.** Are any labels overlapping, clipped at chart edges, or unreadable at the rendered size? Look in particular at: axis tick labels, scenario/series endpoints (right edge, top edge), data-point callouts, and any annotation that points at a specific location on the chart.\n> 3. **Axis sanity.** Does the y-axis range make sense — or is one extreme outlier blowing out the scale and flattening the rest? Does the x-axis tick density match the rendered width (too dense = ticks collide; too sparse = unclear time scale)?\n> 4. **Colour and ordering.** If there's an implied ordering (bear < base < bull, low to high, etc.), does the visual ordering match? Do any two series share enough colour/style that a reader could swap them?\n> 5. **Edge cases.** What happens at the chart's edges — left edge (start of series), right edge (most-recent or projected-Y5), top edge (chart maximum)? List anything that looks wrong, ugly, overlapping, or unclear at the edges specifically. Edges are where label collisions hide.\n> 6. **What's missing.** Is there a current-state reference line, a callout for a fired condition (e.g. a growth cap), or a unit/scale indicator that a reader needs and isn't there?\n>\n> Name every issue, even small ones. Do not stop at the first issue — list them all.\n\nQuestion 5 is what would have caught the Y5 label collision last cycle. Question 3 would have caught the AMZN axis blowout.\n\n### Narrative prompt\n\nUse this for any ticket that produces prose — playbooks, methodology notes, signal/model rationale text. Particularly useful when reviewing several documents together (cross-document vocabulary drift is invisible in any single document).\n\n> You have not seen the acceptance criteria for this document. Read it once at normal speed — do not study it.\n>\n> Answer in order:\n>\n> 1. **One-paragraph thesis test.** In one paragraph, what is this document arguing? If you can't state the thesis after one read-through, the document is unclear.\n> 2. **Vocabulary consistency.** Are the same concepts named with the same words throughout? List any term that drifts (e.g. \"segment\" in one section, \"business line\" in another, \"vertical\" in a third — three names for the same thing is a smell).\n> 3. **Confident-but-unsupported claims.** Are there assertions that a sceptical reader would push back on — and is the supporting evidence in the document? Quote one such claim verbatim if you find one.\n> 4. **Audience leakage.** Is this written for the same reader throughout? If sections switch register (technical → marketing → internal-jargon), name the shifts.\n> 5. **Structural mismatch.** Does the document's section ordering match what a reader looking for the answer would expect? Or do they have to hunt?\n> 6. **Comparison to siblings (only if applicable).** If you are reviewing this alongside sibling documents (e.g. multiple playbooks), what's *different* between them in style, depth, structure, or vocabulary that isn't justified by the subject matter?\n>\n> Name every issue.\n\n### Tabular prompt\n\nUse this for any ticket that produces a structured report — Signal/Model/Screen JSON output, a tab in the report viewer, a results table.\n\n> You have not seen the acceptance criteria for this report or JSON output. Open the rendered view (UI tab if applicable, or the JSON itself).\n>\n> Answer in order:\n>\n> 1. **At-a-glance verdict.** What is this report telling a reader? State the verdict and primary number in one sentence. If you have to read three sections to know, the report is buried.\n> 2. **Number sanity.** For every numeric field: does the magnitude look right (units in the right place — billions vs millions, percent vs decimal)? Does any number conflict with another (e.g. P/E and PEG implying different growth rates without explanation)?\n> 3. **Missing fields, silent.** Are there fields you'd expect for this kind of report that are absent — and is the absence acknowledged (e.g. \"N/A — pre-profit\") or just silently missing?\n> 4. **Audit trail.** If overrides, caps, or substitutions were applied, are they named — or did some assumption silently swap in?\n> 5. **Reader's next question.** What's the most likely next question a reader has after seeing this? Is the answer in the report?\n>\n> Name every issue you find.\n\n---\n\n## 6. Worked example — ABA-115\n\nThe Model tab v1 feature shipped last cycle. Seven sub-tasks:\n\n1. UI shell (no skill change)\n2. Skill schema change — emit `historical_fcf_margins[]`\n3. Re-run covered tickers\n4. CAGR glidepath chart\n5. FCF margin trajectory chart\n6. Scenario fan chart\n7. Captions and styling\n\n### Running the seven questions\n\n- **Q1 (count):** Seven sub-tasks. Multi-slice.\n- **Q2 (sequential or parallel):** Sub-tasks 1, 2, 3 are arrows (UI consumes schema; schema requires re-run). Sub-tasks 4, 5, 6 are parallel-safe — each consumes the same JSON contract from sub-task 3. Sub-task 7 follows the rest.\n- **Q3 (same-file overlap):** 4, 5, 6 each touch their own chart component file. No overlap.\n- **Q4 (one-way door):** Yes — sub-task 2 changes a JSON contract field. Plan-review gate required.\n- **Q5 (mechanical or subjective):** 1–3 are mechanical (JSON field present, audit row matches). 4–7 are subjective (chart legibility). Reviewer pass scheduled for after sub-task 7.\n- **Q6 (implementation or spike):** Implementation.\n- **Q7 (coordinator-agent shape):** Drafting the orchestration line — does it name an agent that dispatches other agents? No, the lead spawns teammates directly. Fine.\n\n### The orchestration line\n\n`plan-review → chain(1-3) → team-of-3(4‖5‖6) → chain(7) → chart-reviewer pass on UI`\n\n### Where each guard fires\n\n```\n   plan-review (Q4 + Q7)\n        │\n        ▼\n   chain(1-3) ──→ gate check ──→ team-of-3(4‖5‖6) ──→ gate check ──→ chain(7) ──→ chart reviewer (Q5)\n                       │                                   │\n                  Reads AC of                         Reads AC of\n                  sub-task 3.                         sub-task 6.\n                  git diff scan                       git diff scan\n                  for any commit                      against teammate\n                  touching files                      \"files to touch\"\n                  outside sub-task                    lists. Cross-slice\n                  3's scope.                          bug fold = abort.\n```\n\nThe chart reviewer at the end is what would have caught the Y5 label collision before merge. The gate check between team and chain is what would have caught the latent NaN crash from sub-task 1 being silently folded into sub-task 2's commit.\n\n---\n\n## 7. What this doesn't fix\n\nThe checklist is not a complete fix for last cycle's three failures. Honest read:\n\n### Failure → which question catches it\n\n| Failure from last cycle | Caught by | Strength |\n|-------------------------|-----------|----------|\n| Supervisor-agent platform deviation | [Question 7](#question-7--does-the-plan-describe-an-agent-dispatching-other-agents) + plan-review gate | Strong — the question is mechanical and the gate runs before any code |\n| Cross-ticket bug silently bundled into wrong commit | The \"do not silently fold\" rule in the ticket plan + 15-second `git diff --stat` eyeball before each commit | Partial — same kind of written rule that failed once already |\n| Chart shipped with overlapping labels | [Question 5](#question-5--are-the-acceptance-checks-mechanical-or-subjective) + the [chart reviewer prompt](#chart-prompt) | Strong — reviewer never sees the original acceptance criteria, so it can't re-pass the same checks |\n\n### The honest caveats\n\n**The bundled-fix problem is mitigated, not eliminated.** The rule about separate attributed commits is in the ticket plan, but it's still a written rule the agent has to remember to follow. The short-term mitigation is fifteen seconds of you reading the staged diff yourself before each commit lands. The durable fix is a `PreToolUse` hook that rejects any commit touching files outside the assigned sub-task's scope. The hook is about a cycle's worth of work and is worth building if the manual diff-read isn't catching things after a couple of cycles.\n\n**Agent-team costs are not measured.** Anthropic hasn't published per-token figures. The rough mental model is roughly N+1× a single session for N parallel teammates, but that's a guess, not a number. Rule of thumb: only run agents in parallel when each parallel sub-task would take an hour or more of work on its own. Otherwise run them sequentially. The token cost is roughly the same either way — you only buy back wall-clock time by parallelising.\n\n**Agent teams use an experimental flag.** `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1` could be removed or renamed without warning. If it goes away, the fallback is to run the same sub-tasks sequentially in single sessions. The checklist still works; only the parallelism goes away.\n\n### One time-sensitive note\n\nOn June 15 (about four weeks from today), Anthropic is changing how Agent SDK usage is billed on subscription plans — it moves to a separate monthly credit pool. The checklist does not recommend the Agent SDK for any of the seven shapes, so this does not change the recommendation. It is only relevant if you were considering building a custom SDK-based orchestrator this cycle.\n\n---\n\n## 8. Reference\n\nIf this guide doesn't answer a question, the depth is in three companion files in the same folder:\n\n- **[`pattern-map.md`](pattern-map.md)** — the research-grade version of this guide. Read this if you want the formal axis definitions (A1–A7), the decision-tree leaves (L0–L7), the activation triggers, and the three worked examples in their original level of detail. It's denser and uses internal codes, but it's authoritative.\n\n- **[`landscape.md`](landscape.md)** — the catalogue of every Claude Code orchestration primitive (single session, sub-agents via the Agent tool, custom sub-agents, agent teams, hooks, skills, slash commands, the `/batch` command, MCP servers, channels, Agent SDK, worktrees, the agent view, plus third-party tools). Includes platform constraints, cost notes, and setup friction. Read this when you need to know what an agent team actually is or what `/batch` does.\n\n- **[`critique.md`](critique.md)** — the stress-test of the recommendation. Read this if you want the \"what could still go wrong\" view, the items that didn't fully close, or the reasoning behind why certain things were deferred.\n\nThe three reference files are intentionally rougher — they were produced by an agent team during research and they're the record of how the recommendation got there. This guide is what you actually run during cycle planning.\n\n### Quick glossary\n\n| Term in this guide | What it is |\n|--------------------|------------|\n| Sub-task | One numbered item in a Linear ticket's `plan.md` |\n| One-way door | A change that's expensive to reverse (schema field, public command name, methodology constant, frontmatter key, anything in `DESIGN.md`) |\n| Chain | Multiple sub-tasks in a single session with explicit gates between them |\n| Team | Agent teams primitive — top-level lead plus 3–5 teammates running in parallel |\n| Composition | A ticket that mixes chain and team phases — most common shape in this repo |\n| Reviewer pass | A fresh-context second look at subjective output using one of the prompts in [Section 5](#5-reviewer-prompts) |\n| Gate check | The three-step procedure at each phase boundary: read the prior phase's acceptance criteria, scan `git log` for cross-phase bug folds, name the next shape out loud |\n"
}
```

1	# Orchestration guide
2	
3	A short, self-contained reference for deciding how to break a Linear ticket into agent work. Run the seven-question checklist at cycle planning; write one line into the ticket's `plan.md`; execute.
4	
5	If this is your first read, work top-to-bottom. Once you have run the ritual on a couple of tickets, you'll come back only to Section 2 (the questions) and Section 5 (the reviewer prompts).
6	
7	## Contents
8	
9	1. [What this is](#1-what-this-is)
10	2. [The seven questions](#2-the-seven-questions)
11	3. [The seven execution shapes](#3-the-seven-execution-shapes)
12	4. [The 60-second ritual](#4-the-60-second-ritual)
13	5. [Reviewer prompts](#5-reviewer-prompts)
14	6. [Worked example — ABA-115](#6-worked-example--aba-115)
15	7. [What this doesn't fix](#7-what-this-doesnt-fix)
16	8. [Reference](#8-reference)
17	
18	---
19	
20	## 1. What this is
21	
22	A planning-time checklist that decides how a ticket gets executed by agents. The point is to make the call before any agent touches code, not at retrospective.
23	
24	Last cycle, three things went wrong and we caught all three only afterwards:
25	
26	- I designed a coordination structure — a "supervisor" agent that spawned worker agents — that Claude Code doesn't actually support. The agent improvised silently and only mentioned the deviation in the final report.
27	- A bug introduced by the first ticket's code was discovered while working on the second ticket, and the fix was quietly bundled into the second ticket's commit. The git history can no longer tell you which ticket caused which bug.
28	- A chart shipped with overlapping labels at the right edge. The agent's verification passed because every acceptance check was mechanical ("are there three lines drawn") and none of them asked "is this chart readable?" You spotted the bug in the screenshot. The agent didn't.
29	
30	The checklist below turns each of those failures into a planning-time question. If you answer the questions and write the resulting orchestration line into the ticket's plan, the failure has somewhere to be caught before any code is written.
31	
32	---
33	
34	## 2. The seven questions
35	
36	Ask these of every parent ticket during cycle planning. Most have a 10-second answer once the ticket plan is in front of you.
37	
38	### Question 1 — How many sub-tasks does this ticket break into?
39	
40	Count the items in the ticket's `plan.md`. One is a single-session job. Two or three with arrows between them is a chain. Four or more that share a template is a queue.
41	
42	### Question 2 — Do they have to happen in order, or can they run in parallel?
43	
44	Look at the dependency graph in `plan.md`. If sub-task two consumes a JSON schema, file, or output that sub-task one produces, you have to serialise. If they don't share inputs, they're candidates for parallel work.
45	
46	### Question 3 — Do any of them touch the same file?
47	
48	Skim each sub-task's "files to touch" line. Any overlap means you serialise that pair regardless of what Question 2 said — agent teams don't lock at the file level, and parallel edits on the same file produce merge conflicts.
49	
50	### Question 4 — Does anything change a public contract?
51	
52	By "public contract" I mean a JSON schema field, a `SKILL.md` schema block, a `playbooks/*.md` frontmatter key, a public command name, or anything in `DESIGN.md`. These are expensive to reverse — they need a plan-review pass before implementation, not "we'll fix it in review."
53	
54	### Question 5 — Are the acceptance checks mechanical or subjective?
55	
56	Mechanical means a test passes, a number matches, a JSON field is present, a function returns the expected value. Subjective means a chart looks right, prose reads well, a report is legible. Any subjective check needs a second-pass reviewer who has not seen the original acceptance criteria — otherwise the reviewer just re-checks the same things the implementer already checked.
57	
58	### Question 6 — Is the deliverable code in the tree, or a written recommendation?
59	
60	If the ticket ends in a "proceed / defer / kill" memo with no code change, it's a spike. Spikes use a different skill (`pde-backend-spike`) and have different acceptance: the deliverable is the recommendation, not running code.
61	
62	### Question 7 — Does the plan describe an agent dispatching other agents?
63	
64	Read the orchestration line in `plan.md` (the one you're about to write — or one you've drafted in your head). Does it contain any phrase like "supervisor sub-agent," "coordinator that spawns workers," or "manager agent dispatches implementers"? Claude Code does not allow sub-agents to spawn other sub-agents. This shape is platform-invalid and was the cause of last cycle's silent architectural deviation. Reject the plan; reshape it so the top-level Claude Code session — or an agent-team lead, which is functionally a top-level session — does the dispatching. Only one layer of fan-out is supported.
65	
66	---
67	
68	## 3. The seven execution shapes
69	
70	The seven questions narrow to one of these shapes. Pick the first match top-to-bottom.
71	
72	| Shape | When it applies | Building block |
73	|-------|-----------------|----------------|
74	| **Plan-review gate** | Any multi-slice plan, or a one-way door (Q4 = yes), or a coordinator-agent shape (Q7 = yes) | `/pde-plan-review` skill, before any other work |
75	| **Single session, spike** | One sub-task, deliverable is a recommendation (Q6 = spike) | `pde-backend-spike` skill |
76	| **Single session, mechanical** | One sub-task, acceptance is mechanical (Q5 = mechanical) | `agent-skills:build` (or the relevant skill) |
77	| **Single session, subjective** | One sub-task, acceptance is subjective (Q5 = subjective) | Build skill, then `/crit` with a [reviewer prompt](#5-reviewer-prompts) |
78	| **Sequential chain** | Multiple sub-tasks with arrows between them (Q2 = sequential) | Single session, one sub-task per turn, explicit gate at each boundary |
79	| **Parallel team** | Multiple sub-tasks, parallel-safe, different files (Q2 = parallel, Q3 = no overlap) | Agent teams (`CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1`), lead plus 3–5 teammates |
80	| **Composition** | Mix of chain and parallel within one ticket (most common shape this repo produces) | Compose chain + team explicitly in the plan, with named phase transitions |
81	
82	### Decision flow
83	
84	```
85	                  ┌──────────────────────────────┐
86	                  │ Q7: coordinator-agent shape? │──yes──┐
87	                  └──────────────┬───────────────┘       │
88	                                 no                      ▼
89	                                 │              [Reject; reshape]
90	                                 ▼
91	                  ┌──────────────────────────────┐
92	                  │ Q4: one-way door?            │──yes──┐
93	                  └──────────────┬───────────────┘       │
94	                                 no                      ▼
95	                                 │           [Plan-review gate first,
96	                                 │              then continue]
97	                                 ▼
98	                  ┌──────────────────────────────┐
99	                  │ Q1: how many sub-tasks?      │
100	                  └──────────────┬───────────────┘
101	                                 │
102	                  ┌──────────────┴───────────────┐
103	                  ▼                              ▼
104	              [one]                        [several]
105	                  │                              │
106	                  ▼                              ▼
107	       ┌──────────────────┐         ┌─────────────────────────┐
108	       │ Q6: spike?       │         │ Q2: arrows between them?│
109	       └──┬───────────┬───┘         └────┬─────────────┬──────┘
110	        yes          no                  yes           no
111	          │           │                   │             │
112	          ▼           ▼                   ▼             ▼
113	     [Spike]    ┌──────────┐       [Sequential    ┌──────────┐
114	                │ Q5:      │        chain]        │ Q3: same │
115	                │ mech or  │                      │  file?   │
116	                │ subj?    │                      └──┬───────┘
117	                └─┬──────┬─┘                       yes    no
118	                mech   subj                         │      │
119	                  │     │                           ▼      ▼
120	                  ▼     ▼                       [Demote  [Parallel
121	              [Single  [Single                   to       team]
122	               mech]    subj]                    chain]
123	```
124	
125	If you land on the composition shape (chain + team mixed), write the orchestration line as a sequence of shape names — e.g. `chain(1-3) → team(4‖5‖6) → chain(7)` — and run a phase-transition check at each boundary.
126	
127	### Phase transitions inside a composition
128	
129	```
130	   ┌─────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐   ┌─────────────────┐
131	   │ chain phase     │──→│ gate check       │──→│ team phase      │
132	   │ (sub-tasks 1-3) │   │ — read AC of last│   │ (sub-tasks 4-6) │
133	   │                 │   │   slice          │   │   in parallel   │
134	   └─────────────────┘   │ — git diff scan  │   └─────────┬───────┘
135	                         │   for cross-     │             │
136	                         │   slice bug folds│             ▼
137	                         │ — name next      │   ┌──────────────────┐
138	                         │   shape out loud │   │ gate check       │
139	                         └──────────────────┘   │ (same three      │
140	                                                │  steps)          │
141	                                                └─────────┬────────┘
142	                                                          ▼
143	                                                ┌─────────────────┐
144	                                                │ chain phase     │
145	                                                │ (sub-task 7)    │
146	                                                └─────────────────┘
147	```
148	
149	The gate check takes about 90 seconds. It's the difference between composition being a real procedure and being a label on the plan that means nothing in execution.
150	
151	---
152	
153	## 4. The 60-second ritual
154	
155	For each parent ticket during cycle planning:
156	
157	1. **Count** the sub-tasks ([Question 1](#question-1--how-many-sub-tasks-does-this-ticket-break-into)). One sub-task → jump to [single-session shapes](#3-the-seven-execution-shapes). Several → continue.
158	2. **Draw the dependencies** ([Question 2](#question-2--do-they-have-to-happen-in-order-or-can-they-run-in-parallel)). All arrows → chain. No arrows after a shared prefix → composition. Pure independent → queue.
159	3. **Look for same-file overlaps** ([Question 3](#question-3--do-any-of-them-touch-the-same-file)). Any overlap means that pair serialises regardless of what step 2 said.
160	4. **Mark one-way doors** ([Question 4](#question-4--does-anything-change-a-public-contract)). Any → run `/pde-plan-review` before kicking off any sub-task implementation.
161	5. **Check the orchestration line for coordinator-agent shapes** ([Question 7](#question-7--does-the-plan-describe-an-agent-dispatching-other-agents)). Any "supervisor sub-agent that spawns workers" wording → reject the plan and reshape before continuing. This is independent of one-way-door status — last cycle's failure had zero one-way doors.
162	6. **Mark subjective acceptance** ([Question 5](#question-5--are-the-acceptance-checks-mechanical-or-subjective)). Any → schedule a reviewer pass and paste the matching [reviewer prompt](#5-reviewer-prompts) into the plan now, not at review time.
163	7. **Write one line into `plan.md`** describing the orchestration. E.g. `plan-review → chain(1-3) → team-of-3(4‖5‖6) → chain(7) → reviewer pass on UI`.
164	
165	Also add to the bottom of `plan.md` for every multi-slice ticket:
166	
167	- The orchestration line from step 7.
168	- The reviewer prompt (if step 6 fired) — pre-written, ready for the reviewer to receive.
169	- This sentence, verbatim: *"If an agent finds a bug from a prior sub-task, commit the fix as a separate commit attributed to the originating ticket before continuing. Do not silently fold."*
170	
171	About a minute per parent ticket once the questions are memorised. Question 6 (spike or implementation) is implicit in step 1 above — if the deliverable is a recommendation, you took the single-session-spike branch and never reached step 2.
172	
173	---
174	
175	## 5. Reviewer prompts
176	
177	Three prompts the reviewer agent receives in place of the original acceptance criteria. The point is to ask a *different* set of questions than the implementer answered — otherwise the reviewer reproduces the implementer's blind spot.
178	
179	Pick by output type. Paste the prompt into the ticket's `plan.md` at planning time so the reviewer has it ready when its turn comes.
180	
181	### Chart prompt
182	
183	Use this for any ticket that produces a rendered chart or visualisation (e.g. anything under `ui/src/components/charts/`).
184	
185	> You have not seen the acceptance criteria for this chart. Open the rendered chart at its canonical viewport size (the size a reader would actually see — assume desktop browser unless told otherwise).
186	>
187	> Answer these questions, in order. Do not skip any.
188	>
189	> 1. **One-sentence thesis test.** Without reading the underlying data or any caption, can you state in one sentence what this chart is arguing? If no — what's confusing?
190	> 2. **Label legibility.** Are any labels overlapping, clipped at chart edges, or unreadable at the rendered size? Look in particular at: axis tick labels, scenario/series endpoints (right edge, top edge), data-point callouts, and any annotation that points at a specific location on the chart.
191	> 3. **Axis sanity.** Does the y-axis range make sense — or is one extreme outlier blowing out the scale and flattening the rest? Does the x-axis tick density match the rendered width (too dense = ticks collide; too sparse = unclear time scale)?
192	> 4. **Colour and ordering.** If there's an implied ordering (bear < base < bull, low to high, etc.), does the visual ordering match? Do any two series share enough colour/style that a reader could swap them?
193	> 5. **Edge cases.** What happens at the chart's edges — left edge (start of series), right edge (most-recent or projected-Y5), top edge (chart maximum)? List anything that looks wrong, ugly, overlapping, or unclear at the edges specifically. Edges are where label collisions hide.
194	> 6. **What's missing.** Is there a current-state reference line, a callout for a fired condition (e.g. a growth cap), or a unit/scale indicator that a reader needs and isn't there?
195	>
196	> Name every issue, even small ones. Do not stop at the first issue — list them all.
197	
198	Question 5 is what would have caught the Y5 label collision last cycle. Question 3 would have caught the AMZN axis blowout.
199	
200	### Narrative prompt
201	
202	Use this for any ticket that produces prose — playbooks, methodology notes, signal/model rationale text. Particularly useful when reviewing several documents together (cross-document vocabulary drift is invisible in any single document).
203	
204	> You have not seen the acceptance criteria for this document. Read it once at normal speed — do not study it.
205	>
206	> Answer in order:
207	>
208	> 1. **One-paragraph thesis test.** In one paragraph, what is this document arguing? If you can't state the thesis after one read-through, the document is unclear.
209	> 2. **Vocabulary consistency.** Are the same concepts named with the same words throughout? List any term that drifts (e.g. "segment" in one section, "business line" in another, "vertical" in a third — three names for the same thing is a smell).
210	> 3. **Confident-but-unsupported claims.** Are there assertions that a sceptical reader would push back on — and is the supporting evidence in the document? Quote one such claim verbatim if you find one.
211	> 4. **Audience leakage.** Is this written for the same reader throughout? If sections switch register (technical → marketing → internal-jargon), name the shifts.
212	> 5. **Structural mismatch.** Does the document's section ordering match what a reader looking for the answer would expect? Or do they have to hunt?
213	> 6. **Comparison to siblings (only if applicable).** If you are reviewing this alongside sibling documents (e.g. multiple playbooks), what's *different* between them in style, depth, structure, or vocabulary that isn't justified by the subject matter?
214	>
215	> Name every issue.
216	
217	### Tabular prompt
218	
219	Use this for any ticket that produces a structured report — Signal/Model/Screen JSON output, a tab in the report viewer, a results table.
220	
221	> You have not seen the acceptance criteria for this report or JSON output. Open the rendered view (UI tab if applicable, or the JSON itself).
222	>
223	> Answer in order:
224	>
225	> 1. **At-a-glance verdict.** What is this report telling a reader? State the verdict and primary number in one sentence. If you have to read three sections to know, the report is buried.
226	> 2. **Number sanity.** For every numeric field: does the magnitude look right (units in the right place — billions vs millions, percent vs decimal)? Does any number conflict with another (e.g. P/E and PEG implying different growth rates without explanation)?
227	> 3. **Missing fields, silent.** Are there fields you'd expect for this kind of report that are absent — and is the absence acknowledged (e.g. "N/A — pre-profit") or just silently missing?
228	> 4. **Audit trail.** If overrides, caps, or substitutions were applied, are they named — or did some assumption silently swap in?
229	> 5. **Reader's next question.** What's the most likely next question a reader has after seeing this? Is the answer in the report?
230	>
231	> Name every issue you find.
232	
233	---
234	
235	## 6. Worked example — ABA-115
236	
237	The Model tab v1 feature shipped last cycle. Seven sub-tasks:
238	
239	1. UI shell (no skill change)
240	2. Skill schema change — emit `historical_fcf_margins[]`
241	3. Re-run covered tickers
242	4. CAGR glidepath chart
243	5. FCF margin trajectory chart
244	6. Scenario fan chart
245	7. Captions and styling
246	
247	### Running the seven questions
248	
249	- **Q1 (count):** Seven sub-tasks. Multi-slice.
250	- **Q2 (sequential or parallel):** Sub-tasks 1, 2, 3 are arrows (UI consumes schema; schema requires re-run). Sub-tasks 4, 5, 6 are parallel-safe — each consumes the same JSON contract from sub-task 3. Sub-task 7 follows the rest.
251	- **Q3 (same-file overlap):** 4, 5, 6 each touch their own chart component file. No overlap.
252	- **Q4 (one-way door):** Yes — sub-task 2 changes a JSON contract field. Plan-review gate required.
253	- **Q5 (mechanical or subjective):** 1–3 are mechanical (JSON field present, audit row matches). 4–7 are subjective (chart legibility). Reviewer pass scheduled for after sub-task 7.
254	- **Q6 (implementation or spike):** Implementation.
255	- **Q7 (coordinator-agent shape):** Drafting the orchestration line — does it name an agent that dispatches other agents? No, the lead spawns teammates directly. Fine.
256	
257	### The orchestration line
258	
259	`plan-review → chain(1-3) → team-of-3(4‖5‖6) → chain(7) → chart-reviewer pass on UI`
260	
261	### Where each guard fires
262	
263	```
264	   plan-review (Q4 + Q7)
265	        │
266	        ▼
267	   chain(1-3) ──→ gate check ──→ team-of-3(4‖5‖6) ──→ gate check ──→ chain(7) ──→ chart reviewer (Q5)
268	                       │                                   │
269	                  Reads AC of                         Reads AC of
270	                  sub-task 3.                         sub-task 6.
271	                  git diff scan                       git diff scan
272	                  for any commit                      against teammate
273	                  touching files                      "files to touch"
274	                  outside sub-task                    lists. Cross-slice
275	                  3's scope.                          bug fold = abort.
276	```
277	
278	The chart reviewer at the end is what would have caught the Y5 label collision before merge. The gate check between team and chain is what would have caught the latent NaN crash from sub-task 1 being silently folded into sub-task 2's commit.
279	
280	---
281	
282	## 7. What this doesn't fix
283	
284	The checklist is not a complete fix for last cycle's three failures. Honest read:
285	
286	### Failure → which question catches it
287	
288	| Failure from last cycle | Caught by | Strength |
289	|-------------------------|-----------|----------|
290	| Supervisor-agent platform deviation | [Question 7](#question-7--does-the-plan-describe-an-agent-dispatching-other-agents) + plan-review gate | Strong — the question is mechanical and the gate runs before any code |
291	| Cross-ticket bug silently bundled into wrong commit | The "do not silently fold" rule in the ticket plan + 15-second `git diff --stat` eyeball before each commit | Partial — same kind of written rule that failed once already |
292	| Chart shipped with overlapping labels | [Question 5](#question-5--are-the-acceptance-checks-mechanical-or-subjective) + the [chart reviewer prompt](#chart-prompt) | Strong — reviewer never sees the original acceptance criteria, so it can't re-pass the same checks |
293	
294	### The honest caveats
295	
296	**The bundled-fix problem is mitigated, not eliminated.** The rule about separate attributed commits is in the ticket plan, but it's still a written rule the agent has to remember to follow. The short-term mitigation is fifteen seconds of you reading the staged diff yourself before each commit lands. The durable fix is a `PreToolUse` hook that rejects any commit touching files outside the assigned sub-task's scope. The hook is about a cycle's worth of work and is worth building if the manual diff-read isn't catching things after a couple of cycles.
297	
298	**Agent-team costs are not measured.** Anthropic hasn't published per-token figures. The rough mental model is roughly N+1× a single session for N parallel teammates, but that's a guess, not a number. Rule of thumb: only run agents in parallel when each parallel sub-task would take an hour or more of work on its own. Otherwise run them sequentially. The token cost is roughly the same either way — you only buy back wall-clock time by parallelising.
299	
300	**Agent teams use an experimental flag.** `CLAUDE_CODE_EXPERIMENTAL_AGENT_TEAMS=1` could be removed or renamed without warning. If it goes away, the fallback is to run the same sub-tasks sequentially in single sessions. The checklist still works; only the parallelism goes away.
301	
302	### One time-sensitive note
303	
304	On June 15 (about four weeks from today), Anthropic is changing how Agent SDK usage is billed on subscription plans — it moves to a separate monthly credit pool. The checklist does not recommend the Agent SDK for any of the seven shapes, so this does not change the recommendation. It is only relevant if you were considering building a custom SDK-based orchestrator this cycle.
305	
306	---
307	
308	## 8. Reference
309	
310	If this guide doesn't answer a question, the depth is in three companion files in the same folder:
311	
312	- **[`pattern-map.md`](pattern-map.md)** — the research-grade version of this guide. Read this if you want the formal axis definitions (A1–A7), the decision-tree leaves (L0–L7), the activation triggers, and the three worked examples in their original level of detail. It's denser and uses internal codes, but it's authoritative.
313	
314	- **[`landscape.md`](landscape.md)** — the catalogue of every Claude Code orchestration primitive (single session, sub-agents via the Agent tool, custom sub-agents, agent teams, hooks, skills, slash commands, the `/batch` command, MCP servers, channels, Agent SDK, worktrees, the agent view, plus third-party tools). Includes platform constraints, cost notes, and setup friction. Read this when you need to know what an agent team actually is or what `/batch` does.
315	
316	- **[`critique.md`](critique.md)** — the stress-test of the recommendation. Read this if you want the "what could still go wrong" view, the items that didn't fully close, or the reasoning behind why certain things were deferred.
317	
318	The three reference files are intentionally rougher — they were produced by an agent team during research and they're the record of how the recommendation got there. This guide is what you actually run during cycle planning.
319	
320	### Quick glossary
321	
322	| Term in this guide | What it is |
323	|--------------------|------------|
324	| Sub-task | One numbered item in a Linear ticket's `plan.md` |
325	| One-way door | A change that's expensive to reverse (schema field, public command name, methodology constant, frontmatter key, anything in `DESIGN.md`) |
326	| Chain | Multiple sub-tasks in a single session with explicit gates between them |
327	| Team | Agent teams primitive — top-level lead plus 3–5 teammates running in parallel |
328	| Composition | A ticket that mixes chain and team phases — most common shape in this repo |
329	| Reviewer pass | A fresh-context second look at subjective output using one of the prompts in [Section 5](#5-reviewer-prompts) |
330	| Gate check | The three-step procedure at each phase boundary: read the prior phase's acceptance criteria, scan `git log` for cross-phase bug folds, name the next shape out loud |
331	
```
